# Ludwig Klages, *Der Geist als Widersacher der Seele* (1929–1932)
## A chapter-by-chapter study guide, with an overall philosophical analysis

*Prepared from the bilingual edition in "unfiled pdf downloads" (German scans with an unrevised machine translation). Numbers in square brackets, e.g. [523], are pages of the German original. Each chapter entry gives: **Thesis** (what the chapter establishes), **Argument** (how it does it), **Key terms**, **Contribution to the whole**, and a **Critical note**. Each block of chapters closes with an "Arc" section on how its chapters build on one another. Part Two is an analysis of the work as a whole; Part Three surveys its reception, later developments and kindred thinkers, with a guide to the secondary literature.*

*About the source: the scan is missing p. 70 (the opening of Ch. 9). Ch. 56 breaks off mid-sentence in the source. Some figures (Figs. 9–15 in Ch. 66–67) and formulas (Ch. 51 [701]) are absent or garbled. These gaps are flagged where they occur. Passages that are antisemitic, or that make sweeping assertions about race, gender and sexuality, are marked as assertion, not argument.*

# Part One — Chapter by Chapter

## Introduction

### Introduction (*Einleitung*) [1–8]

**Thesis.** Psychology (*Seelenkunde*) cannot be done without metaphysics. The confusion of contemporary psychology comes from losing the Greek insight that the human being is made of three substances: Body (*soma*, *Leib*), Soul (*psyche*, *Seele*) and Spirit (*nous/pneuma/logos*, *Geist*). Klages proposes to make this triad conceptually rigorous and to correct its traditional ranking. Body and Soul are the inseparable poles of the "life-cell". Spirit drives into that cell from outside "like a wedge" and pulls the poles apart [7].

**Argument.**
1. *Against progressivism (historical-methodological).* A researcher whose insights depart from the consensus will look to the past for kindred views. He does not assume we are "further along" than the ancients. Nearly every philosophical question and answer was already reached by the Greeks. Whether we know more than Heraclitus is an open question, not a settled one [1–2].
2. *Against the scientific claim to knowledge.* Modern science has counted, weighed and measured, and has achieved "mastery over nature". But spying on the world for whatever lets us subjugate it is not the same as knowing its meaning and essence [2].
3. *Turning skepticism around.* The anti-metaphysician says metaphysics only expresses the mood of an age. Klages answers with a mirror-image skepticism, voiced hypothetically by a "doubter from metaphysics" with whom he does "not yet" say he aligns himself [3–4]. Science too rests on a belief, and it substitutes mere acquaintance (*Kenntnis*) for knowledge (*Wissen*). His examples are the horse-dealer, the timber-dealer and the "judge of men": each is a connoisseur, not a scientist [3]. Every act of taking cognizance is the resultant of a guiding interest and the image of actuality, so it cannot reveal essence. Science kept only those threads of metaphysics that promised practical application. Its real criterion of truth is utility. Its continuity suggests a selection process that lets only propositions serving a "comprehensive will to power" survive. Pragmatism is the "epistemological caricature" of this [3–4]. The genealogy is asserted, not demonstrated; the book is offered as the proof that will vindicate it.
4. *Cultural diagnosis.* Ontology has sunk below the level of antiquity and the Middle Ages. Romanticism, an attempt to restore contemplative spirituality, ended prematurely. Nietzsche is wrongly "disposed of", although Klages himself calls the will to power Nietzsche's "decisive error" [4].
5. *Psychology entails metaphysics (conceptual analysis by cases).* Whatever basic concept a psychology chooses commits it at once to metaphysics. "Phenomena of consciousness" raise the question of consciousness and the unconscious, and so of monism, dualism or parallelism. "Inner/immediate experience" requires an outer or mediate counterpart. "Soul" is already ontological. Brain-psychology is a form of materialism. The "intuitive" or "subjectivizing" psychologists are "closet Platonists" [5–6]. So: "One should cease speaking of psychology if one forbids oneself to be a metaphysician" [6]. The proliferation of psychologies ("as many psychologies as psychologists") is a symptom of this suppressed metaphysics.
6. *Retrieval of the triad.* Klages traces its history. It was prepared by the Presocratics, carried through the Middle Ages, destroyed by Cartesian rationalism, briefly revived by Romanticism, and finished off by academic psychology after 1850 [6].
7. *Two qualifications.* (a) The Greeks and Romantics "knew" the triad the way one knows what one sees, but did not *grasp* it conceptually. Klages promises distinguishing marks that will settle, for any fact of consciousness, what in it comes from Body, Soul and Spirit [7]. (b) The traditional hierarchy (Body as foundation, Soul as middle storey, Spirit as gable) came from a "deeply partisan valuation". Against it he sets the book's thesis: Spirit tends to "desoul the Body, disembody the Soul" and so to kill life [7].
8. *What the reader needs.* No special acuity is required, only lived experience (*Erlebnis*) and a decision. Experience cannot be communicated; it can only be pointed to (*Hinweisung*) [8].

**Key terms.** *Kenntnis* vs *Wissen* (acquaintance in the service of an interest vs knowledge of essence); *Leib/Seele/Geist*; "life-cell" (*Lebenszelle*: Body and Soul as poles); *Erlebnis*; *Hinweisung* (pointing out as against proof, which anticipates ch. 11).

**Contribution to the whole.** The Introduction states the title thesis in one image (the wedge), which Books II–V elaborate. It sets the programme of distinguishing marks for Body, Soul and Spirit that Book III and Book V Part 1 (ch. 55–61) carry out. It announces the genealogical method (truth shaped by interest and will) that ch. 14 develops into the shallow/deep distinction. And it fixes Klages's stance toward Nietzsche, whose will to power is reinterpreted in ch. 9 and in Book IV.

**Critical note.** The strongest point is the case-by-case demonstration that each candidate definition of psychology's subject carries metaphysical commitments; that part holds up well. The most vulnerable move is the skeptic's slide, which the book goes on to adopt, from "inquiry is guided by interest" to "it therefore can never disclose essence" [3]. That premise would also undercut Klages's own "contemplative" knowledge, unless contemplation is shown to be interest-free. The book only later tries to show this, through the claim that deep reflection serves life (ch. 14).

## Book I — Being and Actuality (*Sein und Wirklichkeit*)

### Chapter 1 — The Atemporality of the Object (*Die Außerzeitlichkeit des Gegenstandes*) [11–17]

**Thesis.** The present Now is strictly durationless. Since no experienced or external thing is durationless, the Now can only belong to the act of apprehension. The act and the object it apprehends are therefore atemporal, and the faculty that performs the act is extra-spatio-temporal. It follows that temporal actuality (*Wirklichkeit*) as such can never be an object, and that there is "no experience of the actual" in the sense of empirical knowledge [16].

**Argument.**
1. *Reductio on an extended present.* If the present had any duration, it could be bisected into past and future, and so on without end. So either the Now is a durationless point or succession, and with it the concept of time, collapses. Time cannot be thought without the "mathematical present-point" [11].
2. *Clock example.* The division-points of an hour must be durationless, or the hour would be sixty minutes plus the duration of the points. The boundary between earlier and later is "the generalized Now" [12].
3. *Where the point comes from (elimination).* Not from the outer world, where everything has duration. Not from recollected experience, where every sensation, mood or decision fills a span. Hence it must belong to the act of apprehension, which "projects" itself into the stream of events and cuts it at unextended points [12–13]. The act cannot itself be an object of experience, because it is what makes experience possible (a transcendental-style argument).
4. *Omnipresence argument.* A durationless instant cuts across the whole universe and makes all places simultaneous. Something temporal could never reach an arbitrary number of places in one instant. So the act is atemporal [13].
5. *Faculty.* Whatever has the capacity to cut time must lie outside time; otherwise "time itself divides itself into days, hours, minutes" [13]. Nothing spatial lacks duration, so the faculty (provisionally "the apprehending", *das Erfassende*) is outside space too: extra-spatio-temporal [13–14].
6. *Object.* A timeless act cannot catch temporal flow, so the object is atemporal. This seems to contradict the fact that we do apprehend time. Klages resolves it with a distinction: measurable **objective time / time-object** (*Zeitgegenstand*) and **real time** (*wirkliche Zeit*), which cannot be apprehended [14].
7. *Being = being-now.* "Time is" turns, for its parts, into "the past is", which is better denied. "Something is" means it is *now*. If the object is a being and being is being-in-the-Now, the object is as durationless as the Now [14–15].
8. *Evidence from language.* *Gegenwart* (from *gegenwärts*, "directed toward one") meant an object being present before it came to mean the Now (18th century). *Vergangen/vergehen* means both "elapsed" and "ceased to exist". *Vergänglichkeit* becomes the counter-word to Being [15]. Goethe's "All that is transient is but a symbol" quietly ranks timeless Being as a "higher actuality". Klages refuses to speak of "two actualities". He speaks instead of the duality of **Being** and **the actual** [15–16].
9. *Anticipation.* Atemporal faculty and atemporal object leave the temporally transient closed to apprehension. "Science" is the "violent encroachment" of a faculty whose "tendency" will be exposed later [16–17]. This conclusion is asserted ahead of the proof.

**Key terms.** *Jetztpunkt* (Now-point); *das Erfassende* / *Erfassungsvermögen* (the apprehending faculty); *Akt*; *Gegenstand* (object); *Zeitgegenstand* (objective time) vs *wirkliche Zeit* (real time); *Sein* (Being) vs *Wirklichkeit* (actuality).

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter lays the cornerstone of the whole ontology: Being belongs on the side of Spirit's punctual acts, actuality on the side of flowing happening. Ch. 2–3 extend the point to things and duration, ch. 4–5 to motion, and ch. 7 identifies the faculty as *Geist*. The objective/real time distinction returns in ch. 11 [97] and is re-founded in Book III as "experience" of real time.

**Critical note.** The reductio in step 1 shows only that a *boundary* between past and future must be unextended. It does not show that the *present as experienced* (the "specious present") is a point. Klages assumes that the phenomenological present and the mathematical cut are the same thing. The step from "the act sets durationless cuts" to "the act occurs in no time at all" is also a category leap: an act can produce point-like determinations without being point-like itself.

### Chapter 2 — On the Nature of Existence (*Vom Wesen der Existenz*) [18–23]

**Thesis.** The perceived thing is not its appearance. It is an abstract, unextended **relational point** for a changing manifold of images, wrested from appearance by the act and re-identified across aspects. Existence (*Existenz*) is the persistence of this point through a span of time. Duration ("always") is a concept the Spirit creates.

**Argument.**
1. *Impression vs apprehension of the thing.* Seeing a tree gives both an impression and the awareness of an existing thing. The judgment "there is a tree" does not refer to the appearance. Aspects (*Ansicht*) change with every position and observer, while the tree is one. Properties (fifteen metres tall, green) do not show in the aspect, which can shrink to a point or lose its colour in darkness [18–19]. So two things must be kept strictly apart: **impression-experience** (*Eindruckserlebnis*) and **apprehension of the existing thing** [19].
2. *Point-character.* The thing cannot be identified with any one of indefinitely many impression-contents, so it must be a "merely thinkable point". This point is a relating-centre for a manifold of images (it does not sit *inside* the thing) [19]. The act thus (a) wrests its object from appearance and (b) re-finds it as identical. Historical illustration: the Scholastic doctrine of transubstantiation (substance vs accidents) correctly presupposed that the perceived thing is an abstractum [19].
3. *Concrete vs abstract.* Logically the tree is "concrete" relative to its properties. Epistemologically it is abstract relative to its appearance. Etymologically *concretum* means "grown together", which concerns the cohering of properties, not actuality [20].
4. *Objection from individuality.* The existential judgment concerns *this* tree, here, now. Klages reverses the objection. The judgment also detaches the here and now from the flow, so that "what has been cannot be unhappened" [20]. The place and instant are abstracta too. So no concept of the individual escapes abstractness [20–21].
5. *Position.* The Now divides time, but what gives it a fixed *position*? Not the point itself, but the medium it marks. Every temporal point gets its character only from relation to other positions. The "generalized Now" contains the higher abstractum "temporal position" (*Zeitstelle*) [21].
6. *First resolution of the ch. 1 paradox.* We never apprehend time or space themselves, only positions "to which time merely belongs" [22]. What that *belonging* is remains open.
7. *The Now's uniqueness.* Historians can narrate from any position as if it were present. But only the actual Now grounds the concept of position, and its uniqueness cannot be explained from apprehension. There is a "proto-concrete X" that determines the act but cannot become an object. In coming into existence the act "tears itself away from its immediate ground of possibility" [22]. This is a place-holder for life and experience (ch. 8).
8. *Duration derived.* A thing cannot exist at an instant. Its determinability requires self-sameness at two or more positions, which can be indefinitely close. Since unlimited points can be interpolated, we replace the point-series by the stretch. The result is the concept of "always", or **Duration** (*Dauer*) [23].

**Key terms.** *Eindruckserlebnis* (impression-experience); *Ansicht* (aspect); *Ding* as relational point (*Bezugspunkt* of images); *Existenz* (persistence of the thing-point through a span); *Zeitstelle* (temporal position); *Dauer*; "proto-concrete X".

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter introduces the thing as a point-relatum of images. That becomes the It-point (*Espunkt*) of ch. 10–12, and later the *Ding* vs *Bild* opposition (ch. 20–21 onward). The unexplained "X" that grounds the uniqueness of the Now is what ch. 8 calls life, or experiencing.

**Critical note.** The point that thing-identity across aspects cannot be read off any single aspect is a strong anti-sensualist argument. The step from "not identical with any aspect" to "an unextended point" is the weak one. Rival models (the thing as a rule or series of aspects, as in Husserl's adumbrations or Mill's permanent possibilities) are neither considered nor excluded.

### Chapter 3 — The Concept of Duration (*Der Begriff der Dauer*) [24–33]

**Thesis.** Duration belongs only to existing things. Transferring it to time itself ("time-duration", time as a flowing river, infinite duration) is a constitutive paralogism. We measure time by the duration of things, not the reverse. Real time flows from the future into the past past a timeless standing point in us. "Infinite duration" is a *contradictio in adjecto*. The principle of identity expresses exactly the atemporality of the object of thought.

**Argument.**
1. *Paralogism of "Zeitdauer".* Persistence requires two temporal positions. So duration *of time* would require a second time, and so on without end. Thought is "condemned" to grasp all being after the model of the thing. The thing is the prototype of the noun. This explains the reifications of process, fate, life, youth and the like, and the copula use of *sein* [24–25].
2. *Measurement.* We apprehend positions and their succession, not intervals. Intervals are inferred from things that persist unchanged. Whether real time flows uniformly cannot be settled by any measurement [25].
3. *Contradictions of time as a line.* Time closed at the Now and beginningless behind is "one-sidedly infinite", both finite and infinite. This shows how badly the apprehended limit and unapprehended reality fit together [25–26].
4. *Direction of the stream (argument from language and from aging).* Idioms like "time passes", "the past lies behind us" and "the future comes" show time carrying each Now *backward*. If we ran *with* time, we would stay in one Now and could not age. Our consciousness of growing duration requires that "what exists in us" be something timelessly standing that marks positions on time flowing toward it [26–27].
5. *Target.* A celebrated recent thinker built a system on confusing time with duration, likening cosmic life to compound interest on a penny invested at Christ's birth [27]. He is unnamed, almost certainly Bergson. Popular and "half-philosophical" beliefs in personal immortality as endless continuation rest on the same confusion.
6. *Two eternities.* (a) Real time is "eternal" because it is not even present at the Now, let alone durable. The mystic who "passes away" in time enters it. (b) The time-independence of universal concepts and judgment-contents (the triangle, virtue) is a second, Platonic sense. Neither is sempiternality [27–28].
7. *Infinite duration is impossible.* Existence is self-sameness *within* temporal limits, and duration is the distance between them. Remove the limits and you get either a timeless object or actuality itself, which cannot be apprehended. Whatever endures, endures finitely, the mechanical universe included. Countability excludes infinity. Grimm's shepherd boy shows that eternity cannot be built from seconds [28–29].
8. *Recapitulation* [29–31]. The temporal position is the one universal concept tied to time. The thing-point traverses a stretch. Dependence on time is not participation in it. Actual time is neither constant nor instantaneous, "simply fleeting", and is incompatible with duration.
9. *Principle of identity (conceptual analysis).* Against Locke's "empty tautology", "A is A" posits the object twice, so it demands identity at two times: the object of thought is atemporal. Only one logician (Palágyi, per note 3) saw this [31]. Applied to the fleeting, the principle shows that our concept of fleeing does not flee. Comprehension rigidifies actuality's most general property [32]. Positing rests on distinction (no permanence without change, and so on). The identity principle becomes a principle of duration when applied to time-dependent things. Being has a "double face": atemporality as such, or persistence through happening [32].
10. *Genealogical coda.* Historically, existence and duration were grasped first, and the Now only later, at "fracture-points" of beginning and ending: "once life begins, once it ends" [33]. Where in actuality the ground for beginning and end lies is left as a question.

**Key terms.** *Dauer* vs *Zeit*; *Zeitdauer* (a self-contradictory compound); sempiternality vs eternity; *Satz der Identität* (as atemporality of the thought-object); "double face" of Being.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter grounds the anti-immortality and anti-progress themes. Infinite increment is the same structure that ch. 14 attributes to science's endless accumulation. It anticipates the critique of Bergson in ch. 5 and the direction of time in Volume II (note 2). The identity principle as the "law of spirit" becomes one of the two truth-conditions in ch. 13.

**Critical note.** The diagnosis that "duration of time" is a regress-generating reification is sharp and well grounded. The argument for the direction of time [26–27] is the weakest step. It moves from idioms and an intuition about aging to a metaphysical claim, and it presupposes, without arguing it, a "timelessly standing" element in us. Yet ch. 7 is supposed to *derive* that element.

### Chapter 4 — The Incapacity of Motion for Being (*Die Seinsunfähigkeit der Bewegung*) [34–49]

**Thesis.** Motion is the archetype of all change. At any instant of apprehension, motion yields only a *position*. So the same act that grounds belief in existing things makes motion, and change in general, inconceivable. Every thing-property is likewise a time-independent abstraction that draws limits on a continuum. "Change and being exclude one another" [44]. This is the core truth of Eleaticism (Zeno's arrow; Parmenides' Being).

**Argument.**
1. *Thing-point vs space-point.* No place has zero duration, and every moment of existence needs a place. Rigid form (cube corners, the house, Africa's outline on the map) seems to give actuality as constancy [34–35].
2. *Polemic against system-building.* Knowing has been practised like house-building ("system" = assemblage). Modern "dynamicism" has only swapped houses for railway carriages and aeroplanes [35]. Plato's ideas and the demiurge of the *Timaeus* who "led them from disorder into order" stand for the understanding's projection of order. Klages offers counter-images: storm, plague, war, and the lightning-swirled cloud rather than the snail-shell. Myth at least reports *events*, which science's concepts of being miss [35–36]. This is rhetorical assertion.
3. *Phenomenology of change.* (a) *Indirectly perceived* change (grass grows, the hour hand moves) is inferred by comparing states but read as change of one identical thing. It presupposes (b) *directly perceived continuous* change: the whistle of a locomotive getting louder, colour dilution, cooling. Continuous change is divisible without limit. (c) *Sudden* change: litmus, crystallisation, a detonating diazo powder, lightning. Some opposites admit no intermediate: solid/liquid, rest/motion, dead/alive [36–38]. Children under five and dreamers accept appearance from nothing (a developmental and oneiric illustration). Continuous perceived change is the conceptual basis, and perceived *motion* is its archetype. Language shows this: time "comes", temperature "rises", current "flows", a thought "passes through the head" [38–39]. Physics reduces qualities to processes and processes to motions [39].
4. *The arrow (Zeno via Klages's concepts).* At any present point the arrow has a determinate position and no motion. The gaze that gives the position takes away its motion [40]. *Objection*: by ch. 2 nothing exists in an instant. *Reply*: what is posited at the instant is not the arrow's existence but its *position*, a property independent of duration, like the *angle* between two rails, which is not "in" time [40]. So the arrow argument tells us what motion *is* at the instant: a position. It also proves again that the apprehending act is punctual [40–41].
5. *Extension to all properties.* Angle, shape, position, colour, hardness, weight: all are time-independent and "one step more abstract than the thing" [41]. Motion cannot escape by being reified as a thing. Compared at two times it is not an identical something but a *difference* of positions [41–42].
6. *Correcting a common error.* The arrow does not *rest* at the instant either, since rest also takes time. It *passes through* a position. Illustrations: the Sleeping Beauty freeze, and a lightning flash revealing the arrow in darkness [42–43].
7. *Resolving the apparent self-refutation* (denying motion "with stuffed ears") by the distinction of the *actuality* vs the *being* of motion: the act aimed at being yields only the timeless position [43].
8. *Properties as limit-setting (conceptual analysis).* Red means only "differs from blue", colour "differs from sound", and so on. "Merely and exclusively drawing limits is what the apprehending can do" [44–45]. Logical abstract and concrete are interdependent [45]. The aspect-objection to ch. 2 is answered: we count as existing only what can be represented as a property or state of the thing. Drawing students show this. They draw the house front as rectangular instead of as seen, and the printed page is "seen" as rectilinear though the visual image converges and curves [45–47]. "Only on the basis of the time-independent thing can Being be distinguished from Non-Being" [47]. Hypostatized properties are an error: "There is no light, color, heat... but there are shining, colored, warm... things" [47].
9. *Motion's peculiarity.* Speed builds a temporal span into a *property*. Motion's intelligible sense is "differs from speed zero", so it is conceived only by reference to time [47]. Thought experiment: if all properties changed continuously, as position does in motion, the thing would vanish (cf. Kant's cinnabar, note 4) [47–48].
10. *Additive argument.* Zero added to zero never gives magnitude, and a billion positions never give flight. This contains the core of the other Zenonian paradoxes [48]. Parmenides had the deeper truth: the "incapacity of *happening* to be". Zeno's sober proof came after Parmenides' Being: "never was it, nor will it be, only present" [48–49].

**Key terms.** *Seinsunfähigkeit* (incapacity for being); continuous / sudden / indirect change; *Lage/Stelle* (position); *Eigenschaft* as time-independent abstraction; *Wirklichkeit* vs *Sein* of motion; *Geschehen* (happening).

**Contribution to the whole.** This is the longest argument of Book I. It turns the ch. 1–3 results into the thesis that the world of appearances lacks being, which ch. 5 then "reverses in value". The theory of properties as limit-drawing is the basis for concepts as direction-determinacies (ch. 11). The phenomenology of perceived motion returns in Book V (ch. 62, "On the Experience of Motion").

**Critical note.** The angle example is an elegant way to show that relational and positional properties are not "in time". The vulnerable step is the claim that the instantaneous position *exhausts* what apprehension can say of motion. Modern analysis defines instantaneous velocity as a property at an instant, through the limit, and ch. 5 has to dismiss this as a contradiction. The "zero plus zero" image also treats the continuum as a sum of points, which measure theory does not do.

### Chapter 5 — Truth and Error of Eleatism (*Wahrheit und Irrtum des Eleatismus*) [50–56]

**Thesis.** Zeno's proof is irrefutable. Kant, Dühring and Bergson fail to answer it, and the calculus confirms it rather than overcoming it. The *truth* of Eleaticism is that thinkable Being never coincides with the temporal continuum. Its *error*, which it bequeathed to the West, is the inference that what cannot be grasped is not real. That inference springs from hostility to the senses.

**Argument.**
1. *Kant.* The antinomies derive from Eleaticism. Kant "soothes" them by declaring space and time subjective. But the status of space (thing in itself, appearance or dream) leaves its properties unchanged: "Red remains red" [50]. A full critique is deferred to ch. 15ff.
2. *Dühring.* He deserves credit for reviving the Eleatics and for his critique of completed infinity. But his atomism of space and time does not help. A finite countable series of positions yields no motion any more than an infinite one does. Continuity cannot be built from discrete units [51].
3. *Bergson.* He claims Zeno confused motion with the divisible path. Reply: the Eleatic point concerns continuity *as such*. The line is no more composable from points than motion is from positions. Bergson, "fettered by the riddle of time", overlooks the analogous riddle of space [51]. Every line-concept rests on an experience of motion; language says the path "goes, bends, rises" [51–52]. Measurement presupposes a unit that is again a line, so measuring thought stays at a fixed distance from the real [52].
4. *Calculus (conceptual analysis; note 6 expands it).* In the series 1/2 + 1/4 + ... the sum never reaches 1, because a remainder always stays. The mathematician invents a *limit*. The differential is a "vanishing magnitude", a concept that combines still-existing and already-vanished, Something and Nothing at once. Klages calls it an "inconcept" (*Unbegriff*) [52–54]. "A magnitude does not vanish; it is only marked off. The transition from magnitude to magnitude is a jump, but the happening flows" [53–54]. Mathematical counter-logic is "Eleaticism turned upside down": because I can reduce the distance indefinitely, it is overcome [54]. The calculus is a brilliant achievement, but "dynamic" thinking is the *final* thingification of the event. It masters process by fixing it at the extensionless point [54]. Note 6 adds Klages's diagnosis of *why* the calculus works. The differential quotient 0/0 yields definite values only because it uses a zero that "has swallowed its own history", a *becoming*-zero, alongside ordinary *being*-zero. This double use of one symbol breaks the law of identity: "because the continuum cannot be attained logically, an error of thought is needed to attain it computationally". He adds that the two millennia it took to *compute* the continuous show that continuity comes from the senses, not from Spirit.
5. *Parmenides' identity of thinking and being.* "To think oneself into being and to be are one" expresses the intoxication of the discoverer. It is milder than Christianity's making the knower a creator god, and it even contains a germ of truth: Spirit and Being *are* of one essence [54–55].
6. *The fork (interpretive).* Zeno could have concluded that *Being is unreal*, so that Being lies beyond the actual. Instead he concluded that *the actual lacks being, and therefore reality*. The Greek habit of expressing differences of value as degrees of reality produced the absurd conclusion that conceptual thought is the sole judge of reality [55].
7. *Genealogy of motive.* "The driving force... was hostility to the senses" [55]. Parmenides called mortals' names "empty sound". Melissus said "we did not see rightly". This force has governed the whole history of thought and has falsified the image of reality so thoroughly that it cannot be cured [55–56].

**Key terms.** Eleaticism's truth (Being does not coincide with the continuum) vs its error (therefore no reality); *verschwindende Größe* (vanishing magnitude) as an inconcept; hostility to the senses (*Sinnenfeindschaft*); identity systems (Schelling and Hegel, implied).

**Contribution to the whole.** This is the hinge of Book I. It inverts the Eleatic valuation: Being is derivative and "unreal", actuality is primary. The rest of the book takes that inversion as its premise. It also introduces the theme that Spirit misjudges itself as the judge of reality, which leads into the logistic critique of ch. 10–13, the Kant critique of ch. 15ff, and the claim that the Spirit's motive is hostile to life.

**Critical note.** The objection to Bergson, that the riddle of continuity is spatial as much as temporal, is a good one. The calculus argument is weaker. Klages does state the limit definition (note 6), but he reads it as a process that must "reach" its limit, so that a magnitude vanishes or 0/0 is evaluated. The Cauchy–Weierstrass ε–δ definition does neither: the limit is defined by conditions on quotients with Δx ≠ 0, so there is no "becoming-zero" and no double use of the symbol. Klages's "inconcept" charge holds against Leibnizian infinitesimals, not against post-1870 analysis, which he does not engage. The motive diagnosis (hostility to the senses) is asserted from three quotations rather than argued.

### Chapter 6 — Appearance and Thought-Thing (Phainomenon and Noumenon) (*Erscheinung und Gedankending*) [57–60]

**Thesis.** The phenomenon/noumenon distinction is "the deepest of all oppositions". From its start (the Eleatics, Plato) it was meant to absorb and annihilate the phenomenal. Mechanism does not overcome Eleatic denial of motion; it completes it, by turning motion into a thing (velocity as the ratio of objective space to objective time) and inventing "forces" whose action cannot be explained.

**Argument.**
1. *Against a cheap rebuttal.* "Motion is sense-confirmed, so Eleaticism is sophistry." Those who say this have themselves, without noticing, removed the whole world of appearance from comprehension. They reduce physics to a "logocentric construction" [57]. One could equally ask Kant what calling space and time "forms of intuition" changes. Kant's nihilism goes beyond the Eleatics' [57].
2. *Mechanism as consummation.* Movement had to be denied "with a hundred hammer-blows" before humanity was ready for the "mechanical world" [58]. Mechanism makes motion a *thing* with velocity as its mark. It needs "forces" to pass motion from body to body, and from one time-unit to the next. Their transeunt action is an "impenetrable riddle". Hence the attempt to make bodies themselves pure force or movability, against which logicians of physics point out that motion needs movable things [58]. In the equation, motion appears as a quantity of force that cannot be told apart, metaphysically, from mass [58].
3. *Reinterpreting Zeno charitably.* For the Eleatic, motion is still a symbol of the event, and his attack presupposes "the strongest feeling for the event-character of the Real". Without false intention his result would read: reality is wholly change; apprehension leaves only exchange of states on something identical. Mechanical motion could not refute that [58–59].
4. *History of the opposition.* Gorgias: Being is invisible and does not appear, appearance is weak and does not attain being [59]. Plato has archetypes for everything except motion, so change and events sink to *doxa* (note 7: Aristotle's reproach that Plato cannot derive motion). The resulting theme of civilisation is "the disempowerment of the phenomenal by the noumenal... of temporally flowing events by atemporal being" [59]. English empiricism reifies phenomena into object-properties. Kant pulls the world into the mind. These are opposite but equivalent moves [59].
5. *Goethe again.* If the transient is a "simile", then the simile-free imperishable is "the multiplication table" [59–60]. Faust's "highest moment" comes as a capitalist entrepreneur, and only in imagination, to the sound of his grave being dug. This is a cultural-symbolic reading.

*Order note:* the English column scrambles this chapter's paragraphs. The steps above follow the German order and pages.

**Key terms.** *Phainomenon/Noumenon* (*Erscheinung/Gedankending*); *doxa*; mechanical force; transeunt causation; "logocentric construction" (first appearance of *logozentrisch*).

**Contribution to the whole.** This short chapter links the Eleatic critique to the modern mechanistic world-picture, which ch. 14, ch. 26 (living vs mechanical process) and Book IV target. It introduces "logocentric". It pairs empiricism and Kantianism as two forms of noumenalising, which prepares the claim in ch. 14 that idealism and materialism are both shallow reflection.

**Critical note.** The point about transeunt force-transmission revives a genuine Humean/Leibnizian difficulty and is well aimed at naive mechanism. But the chapter treats nineteenth-century force-mechanics as "the" physical picture. Ch. 4 [36] raised the objection from "electrodynamic systems" and "energetics", and this chapter's reply to bodies-as-pure-force is deferred ("We return to this later" [58]). So the strongest contemporary alternative is still unanswered at the point where the thesis is drawn. The Faust passage is evocative but carries no argument.

### Chapter 7 — The Person as Bearer of Spirit (*Die Person als Geistesträger*) [61–65]

**Thesis.** The source of apprehending acts must be named *Geist* (Spirit: logos, pneuma, nous), an extra-spatio-temporal being. Its uniformity across all judging beings is the ground of the universal validity of truth. Where Spirit works in a living being it constitutes a persisting I or Self, which bears both spirit and life: the person. Its existence is the one case where the concept includes existence. Yet the plurality of I's forces the I to be temporal too, so the contradiction of Being and actuality now reappears "from within".

**Argument.**
1. *Naming.* Every action needs an agent. The neutral participle "the apprehending" does not avoid a substrate concept. Judging, apprehending and conceiving are by universal usage activities of *Geist*. Whether one says understanding, reason or judgment does not matter [61–62].
2. *Historical genealogy.* Discovery of the product (what is judged) came about three hundred years before discovery of the producer (Spirit). Xenophanes' one god is "all eye, all ear, all thought", motionless. Heraclitus "adulterated" his doctrine with an indwelling logos. Then come Parmenides, Anaxagoras' material *nous*, Plato's *topos noetos*, and Aristotle's transcendental spirit [62].
3. *Definition.* There is an extra-spatiotemporal being, Spirit, by which every judging being reaches the same concepts of unity, number and measure, and must view the real as a system of countable points. Objectivity, or universal validity, is "in the first instance" the uniformity of the faculty of judgment in all judging beings [62].
4. *Persistence of the I.* For Spirit to work in a temporal life, the same Spirit must be present in all moments of apprehension. We cannot avoid giving subsistence and duration to whatever in us devised the concepts of subsistence and duration. This is the **I or Self**, and its bearer is the **personal life-bearer** (*persönlicher Lebensträger*) [62–63]. Klages concedes that this is "in truth an impossible state of affairs", whose ground of possibility is deferred.
5. *Direct proof (reflexive argument).* In "I judge that..." the main clause takes the content of a prior judgment as its object. If the relating I were not the same as the judging I, the relation could not occur [63–64]. So the I is the one state of affairs whose concept includes its existence, "after the manner of the ontological proofs of God". The cogito is right in this sense [64].
6. *I-hood as universal.* "I am" implies "I was" and "I will be", since it is consciousness of the duration of existence. As purely being, every person resembles every other [64].
7. *Genealogy of the thing-concept.* The thing-concept arises from the existential feeling of the spirit-bearing life-bearer (compare *objectum/subjectum*). Only to a being that has something identical alongside time does reality appear as a structure of persisting units. Knowing this is metaphysically impossible does not change perception [64].
8. *Other minds and the reappearing contradiction.* We believe in other I's with the same immediacy as in things. Organisms have a "nature", and those with spirit are "persons". A plurality of I's requires the I to be a *temporal* reality as well as a participant in extra-temporal Spirit. Hence "the very same contradiction... like a mocking echo" [65].

**Key terms.** *Geist* (logos/pneuma/nous) as the faculty producing acts; *Ich/Selbst*; *Lebensträger* vs *persönlicher Lebensträger*; *Person*; universal validity as the uniformity of Spirit.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter gives the official definition of *Geist* that the title presupposes. It introduces the two-sided person, completed in ch. 8–9. The "impossible" union of timeless Spirit with temporal life is the central metaphysical problem (ch. 13 [120]: "the actuality of the I... becomes the central problem of metaphysics"), taken up in Book III (ch. 39, "On the Fatality of Selfhood") and Book IV (the I as will).

**Critical note.** The reflexive argument in step 5 is strong for the *synchronic* unity of the judging subject. It does not establish the persistence of an existing I-thing, which is what Klages needs. Kant's paralogism critique is exactly that the unity of apperception does not yield a substantial persisting self, and Klages does not answer it. Note 13 (to p. 115) adds only that the I, unlike any thing, cannot be replaced by anything else in analysis ("All things exist merely 'relatively', every thinking I exists 'absolutely'"). That is a claim of irreducibility, not of persistence. Grounding objectivity in "the same Spirit in all judges" also risks psychologising validity. Klages accepts this risk (ch. 13).

### Chapter 8 — The Person as Bearer of Life (*Die Person als Lebensträger*) [66–69]

**Thesis.** If Spirit can apprehend only points, the continuous, which we undeniably perceive, must be known by a power different in essence from Spirit. We know the continuous by *experiencing* it (*erleben*). The person bears both Spirit and life. Klages gives a programmatic table of oppositions that ends with history as Spirit's advancing war on life.

**Argument.**
1. *Problem.* How can a subsisting point relate to a flowing occurrence? How do we *know* the motion the Eleatics called semblance? We *see* motion, and we see the continuity of space, of spatial process and of time [66].
2. *Historical caution.* The Greeks paired the phenomenon/noumenon distinction with the error that sensibility *serves* the understanding. But Greek *aisthesis* meant a reality of "substrateless occurrence". The Greek saw "not things but images, not existences but... occurrence". The Greek ranking of higher over lower rested on being vs becoming, not on logical abstract vs concrete. Plato's and Aristotle's soul is the life-principle, tied to images [66–67]. Moderns have replaced the sensible with the "factual" without noticing the loss of phenomenality [67].
3. *Dogmatic statement.* The mediating X must be as different in essence from Spirit as occurrence is from being. No "mediating concept" is allowed [68].
4. *Answer.* "We become aware of the continuous by experiencing it" [68]. Outside us there is only occurrence, which Spirit is compelled to treat as semblance concealing point-like being. Our *participation* in occurrence forces Spirit to multiply points of being without limit. That is why perception targets particularised beings, not Being as such [68].
5. *Table of oppositions* (explicitly asserted, "requiring far-reaching interpretation") [68–69]:
   - Spirit and object are the halves of Being; life and image (*Bild*) are the poles of actuality.
   - Spirit "is"; life passes away.
   - Spirit judges; life experiences. Judgment is an act (*Tat*); experience is a *pathos*.
   - Being is extra-spatio-temporal like Spirit; happening is spatio-temporal like life.
   - Being is thinkable but never experienceable; occurrence is experienceable but never conceivable.
   - Judgment needs experiencing life as its support; life does not need Spirit to experience.
   - Spirit dwelling in life is a force *against* it; life resists with an instinct of self-defence.
   - The historical process ("progress") is Spirit's victorious struggle against life, whose logically foreseeable end is life's destruction.

**Key terms.** *Erleben* (to live through, experience) vs *Urteilen/Erfassen*; *Leben* and *Bild* as poles of *Wirklichkeit*; *Geist* and *Gegenstand* as halves of *Sein*; *Tat* vs *Pathos*; *Lebensträger*.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter fixes the master asymmetry: life is independent of Spirit, and Spirit depends on life. The book's anti-progressive philosophy of history (Introduction; ch. 14) rests on it. The poles life/image anticipate Book V's "actuality of images". The claim that perception is forced by life to multiply points prepares the theory of the *Mitbezogenheit* (co-relatedness) of actuality in ch. 10–11.

**Critical note.** The asymmetry thesis (life experiences without Spirit, but not conversely) is the most important premise of the book. Here it is simply laid down. The argument for it is the elimination in ch. 1–5 (Spirit grasps only points, yet we perceive continua). That supports *a* non-spiritual source of continuity but not its independence of Spirit. "Experiencing" is introduced by name with no further characterisation, so at this stage the answer to "how do we know the continuous?" risks being "by the faculty of knowing the continuous".

### Chapter 9 — The Opposition of Soul and Spirit (*Die Gegensätzlichkeit von Seele und Geist*) [70–76]

*Note: p. 70, the chapter's opening, is missing from the source scan (in both the German and the English columns). The text resumes mid-sentence on p. 71.*

**Thesis.** The soul is pure self-transformation in experienced happening: "the soul is the transience of the body" (*die Seele ist des Leibes Vergänglichkeit*) [71]. Spirit, as being, can be actual in the person only as a compulsion to self-assertion (*Zwang zur Selbstbehauptung*), that is, as will. The person is therefore divided by a rift that cannot be reconciled. The rift shows itself most plainly as the conflict between Spirit and sensuousness (*Sinnlichkeit*) or Eros.

**Argument.**
1. *Soul as happening.* Genuine poetry, like myth, voices a happening and so testifies to the happening-character of the soul. What we experience is parting and reunion, coming-to-be and dying, day and night, the seasons, youth and age [71]. Plutarch: the youth dies in the man, yesterday's self in today's. Hence the formula above [71].
2. *Contrast.* No moment of experience repeats. Every judgment's sense can be re-thought identically by anyone. So philosophy and science stand in "unmixable rigidity". They may be forgotten but never grow and wither, unlike poetry, which renews itself in each generation [71].
3. *Asymmetry restated.* We can experience without apprehending, but not apprehend without experience [72]. Something can *befall* (*widerfahren*) the soul. Relative to what befalls it, apprehension has the character of an *assertion*, and the power of being behind it that of a compulsion to self-assertion. As soul the person is driven, carried away, self-surrendering. As Spirit it is willing, holding fast, and judges surrender as weakness or seducibility [72].
4. *Etymology (appeal to language).* *Behaupten* originally meant "to defend victoriously against an attack". Compare *Feststellung* (making fast), Latin *ponere*, and Greek *thesis* (spatial position, then law-giving, then assertion). Every positing of being must be "held fast" against a threat [72–73]. Metaphysically, the threat is ever-flowing actuality itself. Goethe: "As soon as one speaks, one has begun to err" [73].
5. *Will.* Self-assertion follows necessarily from Spirit-as-being becoming actual through coupling with a soul. Klages says it contains no admixture of "hypothesis". The I as tendency to assertion is "the will". Nietzsche's will to power names one side of it. It is not to be equated with egoism, nor with animal drives misread as "self-preservation" [73].
6. *Taxonomy of interests.* Self-assertion divides into supra-personal wills: to knowledge (logic), to order (aesthetics), to morality (ethics). It also divides into personal egoisms (acquisitiveness, ambition, lust for domination, retaliation, envy...), into self-mastery (restraint, self-denial, the drive to perfection), and into the craving for self-esteem [73–74]. These can conflict with each other, which shows Spirit's "atomizing effect" on life, parallel to its fragmenting the world into individual things. They belong together because genuine drives or passions disempower all of them alike [74].
7. *No reconciliation.* Spirit is "intentional inhibition" against every stirring of the soul. Apparent harmony, as in Goethe, is a compromise, a "style" bought with loss of psychic immediacy. Being divided, the person is by nature exposed to derangement. "Mental disturbance" (*Geistesstörung*) is really a disturbance of the soul, with disturbances of consciousness accompanying it [74–75].
8. *Sensuousness.* The deepest chasm is not between intuition-objects and abstract concepts, which are both already apprehended. It runs between what is *sensuously apprehended* and the *sensuous impression*. The impression is a process that carries a "mood" and a pull of sympathy or antipathy. In ordinary speech *Sinnlichkeit* means openness of soul to that mood, not the sharpness of the senses. Hence the perennial conflict: Spirit must forbid itself sensuousness to keep its "standpoint". A self-quotation from *Mensch und Erde* (note 9) says sense-experience captivates and carries away, while judgment locks the world in the "safe" of experience. Its strongest form is "elemental Eros" tearing down conceptual dams [75–76].

**Key terms.** *Seele* as self-transformation; *Widerfahrnis* (what befalls); *Behauptung/Selbstbehauptung*; *Wille* (the I as tendency to assertion); *Interessen* (springs of action dependent on Spirit) vs *Triebe* (drives) and *Leidenschaften/Begeisterungen*; *Sinnlichkeit* (as psychic receptivity to mood); *Eros*.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter supplies the first theory of the will as Spirit's self-assertion, the thesis that Book IV (ch. 41–54) develops in full. The taxonomy of interests and drives anticipates Klages's characterology. Sensuousness as receptivity to mood prepares the doctrine of impression-qualities and the "*Schauung*" of the senses (ch. 21–23). The Eros reference points to Book V and the Pelasgian world-picture.

**Critical note.** The etymological argument (*behaupten*, *ponere*, *thesis*) is suggestive. But it supports only a metaphor of defence. The claim that self-assertion follows "necessarily" from Spirit's being is called non-hypothetical [73]. But the derivation rests on a shift in the sense of "holding fast". Logically, positing an identical object means abstracting it from flux (ch. 3). Practically, it would mean the person *resisting* and *inhibiting* the soul's surge. Only the first sense is established by Book I. Nothing shown so far makes an atemporal "finding" (ch. 1) an opposing force rather than simply a different one. Putting the will to knowledge, ethics and envy under one genus is a bold unification that the book later has to defend against obvious differences of value.

## Book II — Spirit and Life (*Geist und Leben*)

### Chapter 10 — Act and Achievement of the Act (*Akt und Leistung des Aktes*) [79–86]

**Thesis.** Panlogism (logistic idealism) argues: whatever I speak of, I have thought, so actuality is a product of thought. This rests on two equivocations. The first is between *meaning* (*meinen*) an object and *conceiving* (*begreifen*) it. The second is between relating in general and apprehending relation. The act forges point-to-point relations only. Its achievement (*Leistung*) also includes the unconceived co-relatedness of each point to an irrational, continuous actuality. So the logistician confuses actuality with truth, the "utmost intensification of the Eleatic error" [85].

**Argument.**
1. *Plan.* All false system-building comes from one error: confusing life with Spirit. It takes an idealist or a sensualist form. Klages will refute idealism, which is the trunk and is held by the stronger minds, then Kant, then sensualism [79]. Idealists and sensualists quarrel over "supposed difference amid real sameness". "Psychologism" is only a part of logistic [79–80].
2. *The logistic objection, stated fairly.* To speak of "actuality", "happening" or "life" is to think them, so they are thought-products like "being" or "point". Calling them unthinkable is self-contradictory [80].
3. *First reply.* To think *of* something (meaning) is not to *conceive* it. We have concepts of the unknowable and inconceivable without conceiving their objects. Everyone admits impressions are inexhaustible by concepts [80]. Panlogism is "the worst of all delusions... the most incurable". Consistently held, as by Hegel, it ends history [80].
4. *Rejoinder.* "Even the concept of the inconceivable is an identical concept, and hence a product of Spirit" [81]. To meet this Klages asks what a concept is.
5. *Analysis of conceiving.* When I predicate "inconceivable" of happening, I *have* conceived something of it: its inaccessibility to conception [81–82]. Every concept "conceives" its object insofar as it posits it as *one*, and only that. The concept of an army is indivisibly the same in each of the three armies it is divided into [82]. Klages cites the Megarians' sorites (heap, bald head, army, swarm/pack/herd): vague application leaves the transitionless difference of concepts untouched. The Megarians inferred the unreality of the manifold. Klages infers only that what is conceived in an object is its indivisibility, symbolised by the mathematical point [82–83].
6. *Concepts exist only in a conceiving I.* "If we take away the conceiving I, we have... taken away all concepts... but by no means... a single experience" [83]. The impression of red does not produce the judgment "something red". It *prompts* the judging I to perform one. A **schema** follows: I-point → It-point (*Ichpunkt*, *Espunkt*) [83].
7. *Separation requires a medium.* Conceiving rests on the separateness of conceiving and conceived. If the medium that separates them were also conceived, everything would collapse into one point [84]. Fichte and Hegel's thesis–antithesis–synthesis has sense only against a presupposed inconceivable continuum [84].
8. *Two relations.* Every It-point (thing, property, process) and the I-point are each the relating centre of a happening, outer or inner. The I is connected with "living life" [84]. So the achievement of the act has two inseparable but essentially different sides: (a) *positing the conceived point*, and (b) *dividing the co-related actuality* [84–85]. Figure 2: dashed line for It–I relation, dotted line for co-relatedness, solid line for the sector of actuality.
9. *Diagnosis.* The panlogist rightly sees that only Spirit forges relations. He misses that apprehension sets up two kinds: conceived point-to-point and *unconceived point-to-happening*. Treating everything related as apprehended makes everything identical, and so annihilates apprehension [85]. The Eleatic exchanged actuality for being; the logistician exchanges it for *truth*. Against Bolzano's "propositions in themselves" and his "outbidders" (Husserl, Meinong and the like, unnamed here) with their "truths in themselves": these exist only in the thinking consciousness of living beings, which are "transient manifestations of the inconceivable happening" [85–86].

**Key terms.** *Meinen* vs *Begreifen*; *Panlogismus*/*Logistik* (Klages's name for logicist idealism, not symbolic logic in the narrow sense); *Leistung des Aktes*; *Ichpunkt/Espunkt*; *Mitbezogenheit/Mitbezogensein* (co-relatedness of actuality); "rational" vs "irrational" relation.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter opens Book II's polemical method (dismantling opponents in order to build). It supplies the formal schema (I-point, It-point, co-related actuality) that ch. 11–13 refine. It identifies "logistic" as the culmination of the Eleatic error, preparing ch. 13 (Lotze, the realm of validity) and ch. 38 (formalism). The claim that concepts depend on a living I is the root of the theory of truth in ch. 13.

**Critical note.** The meaning/conceiving distinction is a real answer to the "you just thought it" retort. It resembles the distinction between reference and comprehension. The co-relatedness claim is argued, not just diagrammed. Conceiving requires the conceiver and the conceived to be separate, and if the separating medium were also conceived, "everything would collapse into one … point" [84]. The weak step is that inference. It holds only if to conceive something just *is* to posit it as one indivisible point, which is the ch. 10 thesis about concepts. A logistician who conceives the medium as a relation or a continuum identifies nothing with anything, so the argument presupposes Klages's theory of concepts rather than proving it against the opponent. Klages also does not explain how the act, which is timeless, can "divide" a continuum it cannot grasp.

### Chapter 11 — Conceiving and Indicating Thinking (*Begreifendes und hinweisendes Denken*) [87–97]

**Thesis.** What is *conceived* in any concept is only a fixed **direction** of Spirit's "gaze", or more exactly the imaginary angle between two directions. The experiential content (the intuited red, the here and now) is never conceived, only *meant* or *indicated*. Every concept therefore has two functions: **conceiving** (*begreifend*), which orders thought-objects among themselves, and **indicating** (*hinweisend*), which points back to the unconceivable experiential occasion. Klages claims this as a logical discovery. It ends the dispute between absolutist and fictionalist accounts of truth.

**Argument.**
1. *From point to direction.* In front view the co-related actuality is a circle, like the old Euler circles. But the relating line is *directed*, and a direction is fixed only relative to another direction [87]. So subject–object (Kant, Fichte's I/not-I and their variants) is a high abstraction. What is experienced is always "a thing among other things", distinguished from properties, states, processes, place and duration [88]. Paired concepts (unity/plurality, cause/effect, inner/outer and so on) are each thought only as the complement of another [88]. Language speaks of "direction" of drive, will, taste, faith and politics [88].
2. *Concepts as direction-determinacies.* Extensional circle-logic reifies concepts and misses that "concepts are nothing else than fixed direction-determinacies of apprehension" (*festgelegte Richtungsbestimmtheiten*) [89]. A position at the crossing of many series gains no likeness to the continuum it divides. The conceived is only the *difference* of one section from others [89]. Klages derives two primal oppositions: boundary/continuity (from point-character) and conditioned/unconditioned, or "relative/absolute" (from position-character) [89].
3. *Red and blue; the blind man.* We conceive the difference of red from blue, but we *mean* the impression-content of red, which no list of differences touches. A blind man can learn that red differs from blue, the laws of optics, even analogies with high and low tones, but not *one colour* [89–90]. Spirit's gaze "goes in a different direction" for red and for blue; only the angle is conceived [90].
4. *Thinking vs representing.* Fixating must be distinguished from making-present (*Vergegenwärtigen*, "representing"), which often does not occur at all. Thinking is a "leap" between directions of gaze, a change of Spirit's bearing, confirmed by the science of expression [90]. Particles and conjunctions show it: "and" is a gliding advance, "but/however/nevertheless" a swing-about. It is very hard to say what, if anything, is made present [90–91].
5. *Two propositions* [91]: (i) all conceiving is exact awareness of the angle of two directions, not apprehension of the experiential contents they cross; (ii) a direction can at the same time *indicate* its occasion of origin, the experiential content that can never be conceived. The point-symbol of ch. 10 was a makeshift. What matters is direction, and the indivisibility is that of a direction, not an atom [91]. Every judgment's origin lies in an experiential content, specifically in the medium of *spatiality* [91].
6. *Reply to the logicist.* Yes, "actuality", "happening", "lived experience", "continuity" are concepts. But every concept can be used with a double intent: unfolding the conceived (concept to concept), or indicating the inconceivable from which conceiving had to abstract [92]. Example: "this table here" vs "that table there". The conceived difference is fully expressed, but none of the sensory images of pointing enters the meaning [92–93].
7. *Two poles of the vocabulary (an appeal to reflective linguistic intuition).* The first group is equality, difference, unity, number, point, line, thing, weight, possibility, certainty. These *feel* fully definable, though they too have a "layer of darkly surging inapprehensibilities". The second group is night, spring, youth, cold, darkness, glow, roaring, wonder, chance, mystery, actuality, happening, lived experience, infinity [93]. The first group dominates in mathematics, mechanics and logic; the second in poetry. They are "meaning-poor concept-words" vs "concept-weak meaning-words" (*bedeutungsarme Begriffswörter* / *begriffsschwache Bedeutungswörter*) [93–94].
8. *Consequences claimed.* (a) The truth dispute: logisticians save truth by making Spirit spin the world; sensualists and fictionalists (Vaihinger and "economy" theories, implied) make all judgments fictions. On Klages's view all thought-objects are fictions *if* taken to conceive actuality, and none need be if the conceived is distinguished from what is indicated. Concepts contradicting their experiential occasions are "useless non-concepts" [94]. (b) Concept-research since Plato gets two questions: the sense of "this" vs "that", and the experiential occasion both presuppose. The second requires reversing the direction of gaze, a "retrospective" or "centripetal" reflection [94–95].
9. *Etymology and gesture.* *Begreifen* comes from *umgreifen* (grasping round with the hand). Only the abiding can be grasped, and the angle shares firmness with the graspable body [95–96]. Grasping is common to animals and humans. *Pointing* belongs to man alone. It was originally aimed at what could not be grasped (stars, the fleeing deer, the far bank, the cave bear, the flame), so it cannot be derived from grasping. It develops into sighting instruments. In thought, gazing sharpens into "sighting", but what Spirit's eye sights can never be grasped [96].
10. *Payoff for ch. 1–3.* Conceiving lets us apprehend objective time and distinguish it from objective space. Indicating lets us *mean* the experience of real time and space without which apprehension could not occur [97].

**Key terms.** *Richtung/Richtungsbestimmtheit*; *begreifendes* vs *hinweisendes Denken*; *Vergegenwärtigen* (making present); *Begriffswörter* vs *Bedeutungswörter*; *Zentripetalität* (centripetal, retrospective reflection); grasping vs pointing gesture.

**Contribution to the whole.** This is the methodological core of the book. It legitimates Klages's own talk about the inconceivable (his "indicating" use of concepts). It grounds the "deep reflection" and name-research of ch. 14, and it anticipates the theory of symbolic vs conceptual thinking (ch. 36) and of the "meaning" of words in Book III. The distinction of conceived difference from experienced quality is reused in the critique of sensualism (ch. 18–19).

**Critical note.** The blind-man argument and the conceiving/indicating distinction express a genuine knowledge-argument point, the gap between structural and qualitative content. This is the chapter's most lasting contribution. The claim that what is conceived is *only* an angle between directions is far less secure. Klages does not say what fixes a direction, other than difference from another direction. That threatens a regress, or leaves the content of concepts unexplained except by the "indicated" experience, which the theory declares unconceivable. The two-list test of words relies on felt impressions, not criteria.

### Chapter 12 — Objectification and Estrangement (*Vergegenständlichung und Entfremdung*) [98–106]

**Thesis.** Spirit cannot create the over-against (*Gegenüber*) of subject and object. The duality of self and thing is grounded in a pre-spiritual **polarity** within experience: between experiencing life and the appearing, experienced as foreign (**estrangement**, *Entfremdung*). Objectification (*Vergegenständlichung*) presupposes estrangement. On this basis three stages of reflection are distinguished: thing-, self- and concept-reflection. They correspond to the three perennial system-types: materialism, spiritualism and idealism.

**Argument.**
1. *Objectifying the I.* Into the place of the It-point can move the thing, the I, or the act of conceiving and its product. In "I think my I" a thought I is distinguished from a thinking I. Psychologists say the conceived I is the just-elapsed I. But how can the relating power occupy the object-place without losing its nature [98–99]?
2. *Against "Spirit creates the over-against."* *Gegenstand* (formerly *Gegenwurf*, from *objectum*; note 10) suggests "standing against". Recent psychologists say Spirit sets contents out of the soul and over against it, and so creates actuality [99]. *Reductio by "equation"*: Spirit + continuous medium = confrontation of two relational points. Strike out the medium and everything collapses into one positionless point. Strike out Spirit and the points go, but confrontation remains as lived being-outside-one-another [99]. So "the duality of self and thing... is grounded in the polarity of experiencing life to appearing happening" [99]. Spirit can only *find* and wrest something that meets it [99–100].
3. *Phenomenology of sensing.* In mere experiencing I am polarly connected with something experienced as *foreign*, not with my experiencing. We see colours but do not see our seeing, and so for each sense. By virtue of its foreignness alone we experience the *actuality* of colour, sound and warmth [100].
4. *Actuality ≠ objectivity.* Experiencing connects me with the experienced. Thinking of existents (saurians millions of years ago, an eclipse seven months away) separates me from them [100]. Objectification by itself cannot distinguish consciousness-foreign from consciousness-proper objects, since house, mathematics and virtue are all equally "objects of thought" [100–101]. That distinction requires estrangement. Even the saurian thought needs an experienced *space of appearance* as the setting of possible existence [101].
5. *Reflection-back.* Self-apprehension is a second step that turns back from thing to thinker: "I judge that here a tree stands". It is possible because thinking the thing "tore asunder the fetter" binding experience to appearance, so the object-place is now neutral [101]. Hence (a) self-apprehension goes by way of thing-apprehension, and (b) once the polar connection is broken, Spirit can freely steer its direction. Only now does it become conscious that every relation has a direction. "Thinking too is inner steering, and the steering power is called the thinking I". The key to the I lies in the will (Book IV) [102].
6. *Polarity defended.* "Unseeable colours" or "unhearable sounds" are impossible, as are seeing without colours. Appearance and experienceability belong together yet are essentially unlike: seeing differs from colour otherwise than red from blue. Against reducing seeing to molecular movement [102–103]. Mutually dependent and mutually irreplaceable terms are *poles* (magnetism, right/left, sperm/egg) [103–104]. This explains why objectifying the I or concepts need *not* reify them, and also why it constantly tempts us to: the object-place takes its character from the thing, its original occupant [104].
7. *Three steps of reflection.* Thing-reflection, self-reflection, concept-reflection (*Ding-*, *Selbst-*, *Begriffsbesinnung*). Concept-reflection is a fundamentally higher abstraction: "looking toward the concept, I look away from both" [104–105]. It alone can "survey" everything, including the relation of Spirit to life. For that reason it is most in danger of forgetting its two-sided dependence and of making the concept creator of the world. "Socrates began with this; modern logistic celebrates its triumphs in it" [105].
8. *Historical schema* (self-quotation from *Vom Wesen des Bewußtseins*, note 11). The Ionian hylicists worked from thing-consciousness, the Sophists (Protagoras) from self-consciousness, Plato from relation-consciousness. From these come materialism, spiritualism and idealism, with the ens realissimum respectively matter, spirit and idea. All forget *essentia* for substrate, *vivere* for *cogitare*, *fluctuare* for *existere* [105]. Klages endorses Palágyi's root-formula of all error: "we can take for spiritual what is merely alive, and for alive what is merely spiritual" [106].

**Key terms.** *Vergegenständlichung* (objectification, the act's achievement); *Entfremdung* (estrangement, the experiential foreignness of the experienced); *Polarität* (of *Erleben* and *Erscheinung/Bild*); *bewußtseinsfremd* vs *bewußtseinseigen*; *Ding-/Selbst-/Begriffsbesinnung*; *Rückbesinnung* (reflection-back); the I as steering power.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter gives the subject–object relation a vital rather than a spiritual foundation. It is a central premise of Book III (the "polar" structure of experience, ch. 29 "Beholding and Mirroring", ch. 32 "Vital Mirroring") and of Book V's doctrine of the soul–image pole. The three-stage typology of systems organises Klages's polemics: ch. 15–17 against Kant, ch. 18–20 against sensualism. It names Palágyi as the one contemporary "in fundamental feud with every kind of ideologism" [106] (cf. ch. 40).

**Critical note.** The "equation" argument is clever. It shows that Spirit's point-positing presupposes an extended over-against it did not make. The phenomenological claim that "we do not see our seeing" is also apt. The weak step is the equation's second half: "strike out Spirit and the confrontation remains" [99]. That there is a being-outside-one-another prior to any positing is exactly what the idealist denies, so this half begs the question. The independent support offered is the observation that we do not see our seeing. It shows that experiencing and the experienced are asymmetrical, but not that the asymmetry is pre-spiritual. Klages's proof that the two poles are essentially unlike ("seeing differs from colour otherwise than red from blue" [103]) is itself a piece of reflective, conceptual distinguishing.

### Chapter 13 — On the Sense of the "Universals" (*Vom Sinn der „Universalien"*) [107–120]

**Thesis.** Every concept, not only generic ones, is abstract relative to the actual. It contains the actual only "as separated" (*als abgetrennt*), and every thought-act includes, set apart, spatial being-outside-one-another. Klages rejects both scholastic Realism and its modern successor, Lotze's realm of "validity" (*Gelten*) and the logisticians' truths-in-themselves. In actuality there are no things, properties, relations or laws. Truth is a valid judgment-content that exists only in thinking beings. It relates to actuality as a *score* to a piece of music. The single exception is the thinking I, in which thought and actuality coincide. Its actuality is "the central problem of metaphysics" [120].

**Argument.**
1. *Objection.* Estrangement may explain finding outer things, but universals (virtue, government, manifoldness) have no spatial existence. So thought and its object are not spatially apart [107].
2. *Reply.* Apprehending and apprehended are never spatially apart. Yet both have a place in spatio-temporal happening. Spirit is "blind" to the separating layer and fixes the directing point. It abstracts from time's flow to find time, from image-change to find the thing, and from the experienced counterpart to find the object at all [107–108]. Abstracting presupposes the abstracted-from. In "surface-shape", colour participates "through detachment". So every concept contains, as separated, the being-outside-one-another of thought-object and Spirit. The I–It line is symbolic: its "length" would be a senseless question [108].
3. *Spatiality in thought.* Thought-acts always involve space "set apart". This is shown by the felt sense of direction and by the vague localisation of objects (even "identity") in intuitive space. It explains why the mimicry of thinking centres on the eye (Sante de Sanctis, note 12) [108–109]. "Without knowledge of the separate presentness of experiences of space, the psychology of thinking is hopeless" [109].
4. *Universals controversy updated.* Present logic extends conceptuality even to the "individual thing". Platonists now deny Plato meant *being* for the Ideas and speak of a "timeless realm" of contents that are present without being. This is only a verbal change [109–110]. Klages takes Lotze (*Logik*, 1874) as the serious instigator of logistic, deferring "the newest formalism" to ch. 38 [110].
5. *Lotze quoted at length.* German *Wirklichkeit* covers four non-reducible affirmations: a thing is, an event occurs, a relation obtains, a proposition holds (*gilt*). Holding is a self-sustained basic concept. Plato meant only validity apart from being, but Greek had no word for it. Truths hold even when no one thinks them. That general truths govern things is "an abyss of wonder" [110–112]. Lotze's *Metaphysik* nevertheless calls the world of ideas a "system of abstractions" with no priority over things [113].
6. *Internal critique.* Lotze admits the distinction does not remove the "wonder" that prompts the confusion. That is the "delicate point" where converted Platonism is identical with medieval Realism [113]. Whoever speaks of an *actuality of truth* (with Hegel) cannot say how it depends on other actualities. Strike out the dependence, and "holding" becomes meaningless [113]. Against Leibniz's *vérités de raison* and Kant's apriorism (ideal vs real laws): no concept is thought without detaching a co-related actuality, so it is always something actual of which the valid holds, even in mathematics. Validity is senseless without beings for whom it holds. Plato was consistent in moving souls to the supracelestial place too [113–114].
7. *Lotze's coordination of being and happening.* If both were actualities, we would live in one and forget the other. Klages takes up the analogy with dream and waking. Waking remembers dreams, but dreaming does not remember waking. Dream-space cannot be fitted into perceptual space, so they cannot be actual in the same way [114–115]. Lotze, like Descartes, really takes only *being* as actual, and knows happening only as mechanical motion of things. His criterion of actuality reduces to assent by the understanding [115]. (Note 13 adds that Augustine–Descartes and the logisticians both hunt a "pure subject" cut off from objects, though certainty of one's own being presupposes certainty of alien being. It also argues that judging is by essence "a personifying act".) Thus "the whole Western logistic since Plato is, exactly like... Materialism, ... false reification": matter on one side, a "weightless astral body" on the other [115]. Logicians who bring even "And", "How" and "But" to "self-givenness" (aimed at Husserl's categorial intuition, unnamed) outdo medieval Realism. Klages hints at a kinship of "Spiritualism" and "Spiritism" [115].
8. *Threefold declaration.*
   (1) To actuality there corresponds a conceptual counterpart, but there is no actuality of the conceived. Remove thinking beings and you remove the objects of thought. G. E. Schulze: "Were there no thinking, there would also be no truth". Actuality lies "on this side" of true/false and subject/object [115–116]. Colour remains *visible* without eyes, but the *existence* of colour, as a conceived object, depends on a faculty of conception. "In actuality there are neither things, nor their properties... nor relations... nor lawfulness" [116].
   (2) Truth is a valid judgment-content realizable only in thinking. It has two metaphysically distinct conditions. The judgment must obey Spirit's law (identity/contradiction), and the impulse must come from experienced actuality. Error never lies in Spirit but always in a "dislocation of the lived experience of actuality" [116–117]. This is asserted "without proof". Truth is the trans-ference of actuality into an incomparable un-actuality whose sign-language allows conceptual reproduction. True judgments are only means of understanding among thinking beings [117]. **Score analogy**: (a) the music exists without notes; (b) notes without tones are meaningless marks; (c) score and music have no similarity, and dancing note-heads are not songs; (d) a score can be right or wrong [117–118]. Against Nietzsche's "what can be thought must be a fiction": this is profound if it means incomparability, but self-refuting as skepticism, since "fiction" presupposes a non-fictitious truth. Nietzsche stayed in "Socratism" by taking truth for a kind of actuality [118]. The incomparability of truth and actuality is "the most invincible weapon against every kind of skepticism" [118–119].
   (3) Nietzsche says logic presupposes identical cases, and in actuality there are none. Klages accepts this with one exception. When I think myself as existing, the thought coincides with what is actual, or the thinking could not occur. So there is "an actuality of the thought": the thinking I [119]. Logistic is uprooted not by distinguishing being from thinking-of-being but by a critique of the thinkable itself [119]. The I belongs to the actuality of happening while also enduring through it. It exists only attached to a changing life-space (as personal I), and not as a property of the life-bearer. "The actuality of the I... becomes the central problem of metaphysics" [119–120].

**Key terms.** *Universalien*; *Abstraktheit* (as separated presence of the actual, *abgetrennte Gegenwart*); *Gelten/Geltung* (Lotze's validity); *Sein* vs *Geschehen* vs *Gelten*; *Wahrheit* as valid judgment-content; *Partitur* (score) analogy; the two truth-conditions (law of Spirit plus compulsion from experienced actuality); *Wirklichkeit des Ichs*.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter gives the book's theory of truth and its anti-skeptical stance. That stance protects Klages's later claims from being read as mere irrationalism. The theory of error ("dislocation of lived experience") is promised and developed in Book III. The spatial component of all thinking anticipates ch. 31 (the essence of sense-space). The exception of the I sets the agenda for ch. 39 and Book IV. The promise of a critique of "newest formalism" is redeemed in ch. 38.

**Critical note.** The score analogy is a precise, useful model of a non-mirroring correspondence theory, and it is the chapter's best point. Step (1), "the *existence* of colour depends on a faculty of conception" [116], is not an equivocation. *Existenz* has been a technical term since ch. 2 (a thing-point persisting through time), and Klages explicitly sets it apart from the *actuality* of colour. The most vulnerable step is in (2): the thesis, stated "without proof", that error never lies in Spirit but always in a "dislocation of the lived experience of actuality", explicitly "including … mere miscalculation" [116–117]. A slip in arithmetic is the clearest case of an error that is internal to rule-following, so this is where the two-condition theory of truth is weakest. The validity of logic is also grounded in "the uniformity of Spirit in all judges" (ch. 7) plus experience. That makes mathematical truth hostage to the existence of thinkers, a consequence Klages accepts but does not reconcile with its apparent necessity.

### Chapter 14 — The Shallow and the Deep Reflection (*Die flache und die tiefe Besinnung*) [121–140]
