**Critical note.** The examples effectively undermine the identification of mind with consciousness, and the dilemma "either 'think' means a conscious act or nothing" is a clean conceptual point. But the examples support only "these occur without accompanying awareness". The strong thesis, "no lived experience is conscious", is offered not as their conclusion but as the psychological translation of the spirit/life dualism already "proven" [229]. It follows from Klages's point-act concept of consciousness, so the examples can confirm it but cannot test it. The literary evidence shows changing self-interpretation, which Klages admits, and so cannot also serve as evidence about the processes themselves.

### Chapter 26 — Living and Mechanical Process (*Lebendiger und mechanischer Vorgang*) [239–254]

**Thesis.** Activity always belongs to an I and is inwardly transitive. Process belongs to an It. The mechanical process is an "intransitive It-process" that can neither cause nor be caused, so it does not exist in actuality. The life-process is "transitive": lived experience is always the undergoing of something by someone. The soul is "pathic through and through". Life is uninterrupted lived experience and consciousness is a gappy series of acts. Spirit and life are "two actualities hostile to one another from the ground up", with no third thing that unites them.

**Argument.**
1. *Method.* The life-process must be bounded both against the mechanical and against the spiritual act. Every act is I-activity and inwardly transitive (one thinks *something*, wills *something*). So processes, mechanical or living, cannot have the I as bearer [239].
2. *Mechanical process.* To see a motion as mechanical we must disregard any liveliness in the moving thing. Physicists still speak transitively ("the steam drives the piston"), but they mean a finished state of affairs following another state and nothing self-active. Causes and forces are computational quantities stripped of activity [239–240].
3. *The impersonal pronoun (appeal to language).* *Es blitzt, es donnert, es regnet* point to a bearer that is neither personal nor spirit-like. The It is late in the languages that have it and replaced a vanishing class of subjects: Greek *Zeus huei* became *huei* ("it rains"), and Zeus was originally a daemonic, not a personal, being. Grammatical gender is not personification: the river was a masculine *living* being before it became the seat of a male god. As consciousness separated from experience and life was narrowed to men and animals, the efficacious powers beyond the human lost determinacy, and the neutral It expressed this. The It thus became "the substructure of mechanistic thinking", while surviving in the fairy-tale *Es war einmal* [240–243].
4. *Definition.* Cross-referencing ch. 13 n. 13 (the grammatical subject borrows a personal colouring from the I as origin of objective being): if the subject was secretly active, then an impersonal subject, once its daemonic sense faded, robbed the verb of causation altogether. "The mechanical process is an intransitive It-process." The stone does not shatter the vessel; as a consequence of its falling the vessel broke. Since no physicist can say how one inert process brings about another, the mechanical process "does not exist in actuality". Every real process must be living [243].
5. *The life-process is transitive.* *Erleben* requires something lived and someone living it [244]. Objection: then the experiencer is an I and experience is an act. Reply in three parts:
   - (a) In "I live through", the I is the suffering subject, really the object of an event. Language shows this for emotions: *pathos*, *passio*, *Leidenschaft*; "we have the will, and are had by feeling" (quoting Klages's *Grundlagen der Charakterkunde*, n. 34). Gods of love and war, the "devils" of 17th-century titles (*Saufteufel*, *Spielteufel*), and the Stoics' affects as wild beasts all point the same way [244–245].
   - (b) "I live through" therefore means "I apprehend that something befell me". This holds even more for sensing, beholding and dreaming: strictly, "to me appears the visible, the audible…". No one claims the colours he saw as his achievement [245–246].
   - (c) The intransitive "wake, sleep, live, die" are known only because these states overwhelm the I. More It-idioms follow: *es friert, hungert, reut mich*, *es träumte mir* [246].
6. *Why the active form for sensing?* "I see", "I hear", even "I have forgotten" are active in form. Man mostly remembers undergoings to which an act of apprehension immediately attached, so he cannot separate in memory the apprehension from the sensing. Peoples strong in understanding (the Chinese) or in will (the "Aryans") favoured the active form. Greenlandic is cited as free of the confusion. There is also an asymmetry. Feeling hides what is felt behind the process of feeling ("soul-pain" is spoken of like toothache). Sense-experience hides the process behind what is sensed: the person watching fireworks never thinks of seeing. Hence Greek thought explained beholding from the beheld (the luminous eye), and modern thought explains sensing from bodily processes [246–249].
7. *Result.* "The bearer of doing is the I, the bearer of happening the It; the mechanical process is intransitive, the life-process transitive" [249].
8. *The pathic soul.* Lived experiences are undergoings, first by the soul and secondarily by the I. The I receives only psychic or bodily effects. The soul also undergoes impulses from spirit. The soul "moves… out of undergoing, never out of exertion of force"; it answers a call. Spirit is *actus purus*, and coupled with the soul it becomes a capacity for doing or being done to. The I's passivity is itself a mode of activity, an urge to counter-activity. The compelled forms are bracing against, succumbing, and surrender. Terms are fixed: *Widerfahrnis* and *Erleidnis* for the soul's pathic undergoing, *Erliegen* or *Anheimfallen* for the spirit's compelled activity [249–250].
9. *Sense and number.* The polarity between efficacious world and undergoing soul is "the no-further-resolvable meaning of all 'sense'". The original manifold is a twofold, and from it comes the significance of three, the middle term of a sacred triad uniting the poles (magnet, bilateral body, day). Klages sums up: "life-process = lived experience = poled happening = polarity between efficacious power and undergoing soul = reciprocity of estrangement and transition = primal schema of the meaningful process = point of origin of the appearing triad" [250–251].
10. *Continuity vs gaps.* Life is "the capacity for uninterrupted lived experience" across the individual and the generations, back to the first cell. Consciousness is a series of momentary acts, each separated by unconscious intervals and connected only through the continuity of experience. Against James's "stream of consciousness" (n. 35): where is it in dreamless sleep? Hamlet's "pale cast of thought" is read as a premonition of the conflict between experience and consciousness [251–253].
11. *Metaphysical finale.* There is a list of oppositions: continuity vs gaps; poles joined vs I and object separated; experience extinguished in reflection; aeons vs a lifespan; all-embracing vs sundering. Consciousness is "aqua fortis for all connections". Spirit and life are two hostile actualities with no third of which they are parts, which disposes of "monism" (for the bearer of consciousness) and "dualism" (for actuality). The Sāṃkhya ideal of separating spirit from life is praised above Buddhist Nirvana. Open questions are listed (how and when they clashed, why consciousness belongs to creatures, whether only man thinks). All of them hang on one: to which property of lived experience the act of reflection is bound [253–254].

**Key terms.**
- *Ich* (bearer of doing, *Tun*) vs *Es* (bearer of happening, *Geschehen*).
- *Es-Vorgang* (It-process).
- transitive / intransitive.
- *pathisch* (pathic).
- *Widerfahrnis*, *Erleidnis* (the soul's undergoing).
- *Erliegen*, *Anheimfallen* (the spirit's succumbing).
- *actus purus*.
- *Polarität*, *Dreiheit* (polarity, triad).
- *Stetigkeit des Erlebens* (continuity of experience).
- *Scheidewasser* (aqua fortis).

**Contribution to the whole.** This is the conceptual centre of Book III, Part 1. It fixes the I/It, act/process and spirit/life oppositions that Book IV relies on (will as act, ch. 41ff.; cause and force, ch. 51). It states the triadic polarity schema behind the later doctrine of the *coincidentia oppositorum* (ch. 68) and Pelasgian symbolism (ch. 73). The open questions set the agenda for ch. 27 and beyond, including ch. 39 ("Fatality of Selfhood") and Book V.

**Critical note.** The phenomenology of passivity in emotion and perception is persuasive and well documented in language. But "transitive" cannot be the differentia. Klages first called *acts* "inwardly transitive" (one thinks or wills *something*) [239], and then defines the life-process as transitive [244]. What actually separates them is the bearer (I vs It) and the direction (doing vs undergoing), so "transitive" names only the *relational polarity* of an undergoer and an undergone. And the inference "mechanical process cannot cause → does not exist in actuality" turns Hume's epistemic point into an ontological verdict. Calling spirit an "actuality" also strains Book I, where actuality was reserved for spatio-temporal happening and spirit was placed outside space and time.

### Chapter 27 — The Gradient of the Stream of Life (*Das Gefälle des Lebensstromes*) [255–268]

**Thesis.** Acts of reflection are triggered by disturbances, steep drops in the stream of life. Retrospective reflection (*Rückbesinnung*) grasps the "fundamental colour" of a life-period only after a transformation, and never while one lives within it. Reflection upon matters (*Sachbesinnung*) is awakened by affect-laden shocks, and without feelings there would be no consciousness. Strong affect also narrows consciousness by splitting off the outer layer of life from its core, which explains lapses of consciousness, and under extreme conditions it frees beholding in visions. *Ekstasis* (image-intoxication) is distinguished from "rapture" (*Entrückung*) out of torment.

**Argument.**
1. *Viewed from life,* reflection is a having-to-stop, a stepping out of the stream, like being woken. Klages's conjecture: shock-points in the stream open the gate to spirit, whose original work is to harden "rock of fate surf-beaten by life" into lifeless facts [255]. Klages also corrects ch. 25. Its textbook cases were habits, experiences that only *seem* unconscious in retrospect because reflection once accompanied them. They invite the error that unconsciousness is a side-effect of repeated acts. He now turns to experiences that can never become conscious while they are lived [255–256].
2. *The fundamental mood of childhood* (retrospective phenomenology). Looking back, the youth discovers an unbroken mood-tone over his childhood that he knew nothing of at the time. Examples: the apple, the Christmas tree, the honeycomb smell, the garden wall, the cumulus cloud, the fear of the churchyard, scarlet fever. The same holds for the man looking back on youth and the old man on middle age. "No mortal is empowered to grasp the ever-present fundamental color of his lived experience" [256–257]. A sequence of increasing degrees of awareness corresponds to the successive river-sections.
3. *Naive vs "conscious" (sentimental) characters.* Schiller's typology is implied but not named. A character is more naive the stronger the rapids must be before reflection arises. The child is absorbed in experiences that would at once tear the adult out of them. Yet with growing experience the rules of experience are incorporated and reflection requires less effort. "The waters… in the channels of habit are withdrawn from the riverbed of lived experience", and repeated disturbance may end in a rigidity "robbed of consciousness" [257–258].
4. *Smaller bends.* School entry reveals the happiness of idleness, apprenticeship the moods of schooldays, independence the colour of dependence. Klages cites the folk saying: when love is with love it does not know what love is [258].
5. *Objection 1: this is just comparison after the fact.* The reply is that all apprehension is comparative, and comparison requires a difference, here originally a difference in kind, that is, a transformation. *Objection 2: the moods are projected backward.* The reply: distance does transfigure, and memory sifts towards the blissful. Klages adds that consciousness as disturbance cuts deeper going from fullness to lack. But this does not explain the strongly different colourings, including the "wastelands" one discovers one crossed. Klages cites Stifter's *Das alte Siegel* ("long, long not lived") and Peer Gynt ("I was long dead before my death") [258–259].
6. *Height, not time.* What matters is the difference in elevation between two life-states, not the temporal distance. Mortal illness, bereavement and childbirth can make one "know" one's whole life. Further examples: the prophetic dying (Hector to Achilles), the life-review in mountain falls, mantic inspiration. Ecstasy is not prophetic in itself: in its fulfilment it extinguishes consciousness in a flood of images. It is the breaking of the life-wave against the resistance of the I-shaped spirit that makes the seer. Hölderlin: "The heart's wave would not foam up so beautifully and become spirit, did not the ancient, mute rock, fate, stand against it" [260–261].
7. *The man of action.* Why is he not the most knowing? Klages traces busyness, and the achievement-conditions of "world-historical" figures, to "a peculiar impoverishment and flattening of life". The reach of reflection depends on the size of the shock *and* on the fullness of the life shocked, as a brook and a river sound differently over the same drop [261].
8. *Reflection upon matters.* The objection: retrospective comparison requires knowledge of the present mood, so the present mood would be conscious. The reply distinguishes (a) an experienced difference ("formerly I felt otherwise") from (b) a side-by-side of two moods. It also distinguishes comparing two flowers, both objects before me, from comparing past and present moods, where only the past one stands before me. One can reflect *that* one feels, but never reaches the fundamental colour. The depth of comparing acts grows with distance and strangeness. "The primal act of consciousness attaches itself to the strangeness of the image and is thus always reflection upon a matter." There is a gap here: both the German and the English print an ellipsis ("… ;") just before this sentence [262–263].
9. *Affects awaken attention.* Klages leans on etymology: affect as undergone shock, and *Aufmerksamkeit* from *aufmerken* (to heed). The tree stump noticed in the dark through fright, the indifferent person noticed through surprise, the lazy pupil woken by a threat. "There can be no compelled acts of reflection at all except as a consequence of… a surge of feeling, however faint." This qualifies Hobbes: a change in sensing becomes an object only with an accompanying surge of feeling. "Wholly without feelings, the faculty of judgement would no longer become 'actual'." "The primal act of spirit is a 'reacting' act, and its occasion the abrupt shock to life" [263–265].
10. *The inhibiting side.* The wounded soldier who feels nothing in battle is not showing weakened reflection. His consciousness-capable life-space is "chained, as if with a steel hawser" to the enemy (Herbart's "one-sidedness" of passion, the moderns' "narrowing"). Reflection tends to split the consciousness-capable outer layer of life from the core that is not capable of consciousness. If the split succeeds, acts can no longer be related, and the result is lapses, memory gaps, loss of consciousness. Language marks this two ways: "beside oneself" (the I cannot reflect on itself), and both boundedness ("seized, fettered", whether by the overemphasized object or by an image) and unboundedness ("without restraint", the will disempowered) [265–267].
11. *Two kinds of ecstasy.* Feelings may need to be layered by image-content. Some, image-laden, strengthen inwardness. Others, violent, prepare their splitting by reflection. Antiquity's *ekstasis*, akin to joy and intoxication, is distinguished from the "Asian-Christian rapture" (*Entrückung*), which breaks out of torment. Martyrs exult on the gridiron, and witch-trials took silence under torture as proof of guilt (*maleficium taciturnitatis*); Klages quotes his own *Vom Traumbewußtsein*, n. 36. Once the shock splits off reflection, an inwardness still full of life lets the current of beholding rule in "visions". These differ from images that arise from "a reflection-drowning fullness of the elemental soul" [267–268].

**Key terms.**
- *Gefälle des Lebensstromes / Lebensgefälle* (gradient of the stream of life).
- *Lebenserschütterung* (shock to life).
- *Rückbesinnung* vs *Sachbesinnung* (retrospective vs matter-directed reflection).
- *Selbstbesinnung* (self-reflection).
- *Grundstimmung / Grundfarbe* (fundamental mood or colour).
- *naiv* vs *bewußt (sentimentalisch)*.
- *Aufmerksamkeit* (attention).
- *Abspaltung* of outer layer from core.
- *Ekstasis* vs *Entrückung* (rapture).

**Contribution to the whole.** This works out ch. 25's propositions IV–V (disturbance as the condition of reflection). The claim that spirit is "reacting" prepares the doctrine of will (Book IV), and Klages explicitly says the same splitting tendency must be shown for acts of volition. The posthumous character of self-knowledge is generalized to history in ch. 28. The two ecstasies point to the later typology of image-intoxication (ch. 29ff., ch. 69 "Primal Images and Phantoms").

**Critical note.** The phenomenology of retrospective discovery and the account of attention through affect are well observed and anticipate later work on salience. But the core claim, that a fundamental mood is lived without being knowable at the time, is protected against counterexamples. Any present awareness of one's mood is declared not to reach the "fundamental colour", which makes the thesis hard to test. Klages also needs feeling to be both life (pathic) and the trigger of spirit's acts. He has not yet explained how something of wholly different essence can be "awakened" by it, and that is the very question ch. 26 left open.

### Chapter 28 — Conditions and Limits of World-Historical Knowledge (*Bedingungen und Grenzen weltgeschichtlicher Erkenntnis*) [269–279]

**Thesis.** What holds for the single life holds for epochs. No age knows the ground of life of its own reflecting, and "all knowledge of the driving powers, of historical happening too, is necessarily posthumous" [270]. Distance gives the "far-reflection" (*Fernbesinnung*) that can see what an age *lived*. But for that very reason it is shut out from re-experiencing how things were "in the mind" of earlier people, because the corresponding formation of essence is gone. Historiography should give up reconstructing past states of consciousness and motives, and instead read the shaping powers of an age from the expressive content of everything it left behind.

**Argument.**
1. *Protesting spirits.* Heraclitus, Cassandra, reformers, founders of faiths. However opposed to their age, each still bears witness to its feeling of life. Since lived life is never known at the same time, even the most penetrating spirit cannot find the source of his own willing in his age's undercurrent: "What he weaves, no weaver knows." At the turn of ages, as at the turns of a single life, only a new direction of the stream produces consciousness [269–270].
2. *Three illustrations of far-reflection* (historical-aesthetic description). Paestum reveals traits of the ancient soul of which "no line of Empedocles, Plato, or Plotinus" shows awareness. The quarrels of thinkers and of Sparta and Athens become surface ripples. Gothic cathedrals and Scholasticism together mirror the disembodiment of the thought of world-dominion (Emperor vs Pope). Rococo gardens and palaces show the dying nobility's attempt to restore pagan Eros, "the century of fleeting happiness" (Nietzsche). Klages puts no weight on these formulas, which only illustrate near- vs far-reflection. A few words are bracketed in the translation as uncertain [270–271].
3. *A new critical task.* Older critiques of reason ask about the conditions and limits of cognition. The new task, whose pathbreaker is Nietzsche, asks about the relation between the decisions of thinking and the course of destiny, and why translating images of life into graspables should seem valuable at all. Beside it, dialectical and sceptical doubt is "as play stands to earnest" [271–272].
4. *Example: Stoics vs Epicureans.* Their real opposition lay elsewhere than in their doctrines, which was a "misunderstanding". Both express the dying of the symbiotic community feeling of the "autochthons". Both teach *ataraxia* against the old belief in enthusiasm, and both devalue the soul of the people in favour of the "sage". The real difference is temperamental: the Stoic is power-minded, legislative and state-fortifying, and his "endure and renounce" is like a military command; the Epicurean is withdrawn, a little nearer the lost field of life. No contemporary could have seen this. Hence "by their fruits ye shall know them": the depth of a work is not found in the author's intention. "The 'spirit of the ages' is venal only at the price of the death of the ages" [272–273].
5. *The reverse side.* Distance gives coherence but denies knowledge of the near-viewer's state of consciousness. We know what they lived, but not how they hoped and feared. Feelings named alike differ: Carthaginian vs modern hatred; the "savage" bent on revenge after a death; the cruelty of autos-da-fé vs modern bureaucratic cruelty (an aside on Soviet terror-famine and a venal press); drawing lots as divination vs as game. Cult becomes festival custom, which becomes children's games or utility. There are histories of spectacles (agon → gladiator → tournament), festivals (thiasoi → Saturnalia → marksmen's fairs) and fools. Mithraic initiation is unlike Masonic ritual, medieval stigmata unlike American table-rapping, bride-purchase unlike the hidden purchase of men by dowry, and the knight in the wolf's pelt is beyond reliving [273–276].
6. *Language.* Words "are heard exactly by no one in the meaning with which another once bore them". Change of sound and shift of concept are "watermarks" of the oscillation between life and spirit [276].
7. *Homer vs Goethe's* Hermann und Dorothea. Goethe gives inward romantic love; Homer knows none (except faintly in Nausicaa). Odysseus passes from murderous wrath to bed with Circe. The proportions of narration differ: Homer is prolix about shields and lineages, while Goethe names blushes (n. 37 on the "late" laughter passage in *Odyssey* 20). Most decisively, word-meanings were once fuller. Homer's nine-day shipwreck is told in a few spare lines, while Goethe must spell out dusty shoes and sweat, where "dusty they come home!" would have sufficed for a Greek [276–278].
8. *Generalization* (cashing in ch. 14). This holds even more for chronicler, researcher and thinker. Judgement-content is transmitted but meaning-content is not, because meaning depends on the discoverer's, his people's and his age's reflective constitution. We cannot hear the uproar of "Kraft und Stoff", the intoxication of Hegel's "spirit in and for itself", the pathos of Plato's "Idea", the soul-vibrations of *quidditas* and *haecceitas* [278–279].
9. *Goethe and Nietzsche rejected "history"* as an after-the-fact fable. Klages supplies the "ground of right": reflection on the core of a state of life requires another state of life, and the state capable of deep sounding is no longer the one sounded [279].
10. *Programme.* The only achievable task of history is to objectify the shaping forces of past periods. It should compare all remains (buildings, images, songs, garments, coins, tombs) and test them against reports on estates, law, economy, science, manners, marriage, belief and burial. It should not criticize political events from supposed motives. Such a "history of culture and morals" does not yet exist (n. 38 names Bachofen as having gone furthest, for "Pelasgianism"). Whether the past can exceptionally be made present by means other than research is deferred to the next part, on mirroring beholding [279].

**Key terms.**
- *weltgeschichtliche Erkenntnis* (world-historical knowledge).
- *Nahbesinnung* vs *Fernbesinnung* (near- vs far-reflection).
- *Lebensgefühl* (feeling of life of an age).
- *Geist der Zeiten* (spirit of the ages).
- *Urteilsgehalt* vs *Sinngehalt* (transmissible judgement-content vs non-transmissible meaning-content).
- *Ausdrucksgehalt* (expressive content of remains).
- *Pelasgertum* (Pelasgianism, n. 38).

**Contribution to the whole.** This extends the disturbance/gradient model (ch. 27) to collective history. It cashes in ch. 14 (the finder vs the learner of a truth). It states the hermeneutic method, reading expressive content, that Klages's characterology presupposes and Book V uses for Pelasgianism (ch. 71–75, Bachofen). It hands forward to ch. 29 (*Schauung und Spiegelung*) the question of a non-research access to the past.

**Critical note.** The distinction between what an age *lived*, readable from its expressive remains, and how it was *in its mind*, irrecoverable, is a careful refinement of the "posthumous knowledge" thesis and anticipates later histories of mentalities. But it threatens self-reference. If no age can know its own ground, Klages's diagnoses of his own age, and his claim to have finally settled the conflict of experience and consciousness (ch. 26), lack the distance he says is required. His confident readings of Paestum and the Rococo rely on the same "near" feelings he says are incommensurable with the past. The ethnographic examples (bride-price pride, revenge) are anecdotal and serve as illustration rather than evidence.

### Arc of Ch. 15–28

The block runs from the demolition of two modern theories of the given, Kant's and the sensualists', through a positive doctrine of the image, into the opening of Book III's account of consciousness as a disturbance of life. Chapters 15–17 apply Book I's actuality/being distinction and the act theory of ch. 10–14 to Kant. The space of the Transcendental Aesthetic is shown to be the *space-object* of geometry, not experienced being-outside-one-another (*Außereinander*). Kant's "intuition" turns out to be already perception, an act. Chapter 16 adds the criterion of the given, a compulsion "not stemming from the soul" that separates perceiving from representing. Chapter 17 introduces the key positive term, *Schauen* or beholding, as the deedless, temporal receptivity to a stream of images. It also concedes that beholding alone cannot produce apprehension, so a second partial process is needed.

Chapters 18–20 turn the same analysis on sensualism. Sensations are shown to be abstractions: always contextual, mood-coloured, habituating, and in truth thing-properties smuggled in as experience. Their "disparateness" belongs to stimuli, their definitions by "objectivity" fail against dream and passion, and the doctrine of specific sense energies leaves the universe dark and cannot explain the one space of the senses. Chapter 20 makes the "Copernican reversal". What is given in sensibility is the *intuitive image* (*Anschauungsbild*): holistic, cross-modal, an event in which actual space and time are present. The image is in turn the appearance of an *essence* (*Wesen*), a living power whose non-intuitive *Urbild* the soul beholds. Chapters 21–23 support this with a large body of evidence from language (synaesthetic and spatial characterology, colour etymology, impersonal and daemonic idiom) and from animal instinct. They also give the positive architecture. One soul has one image-space. The senses are "messengers of the soul". Beholding (soul-side, present in sleep) and sensing (body-side, the ground of waking and of the images' corporeality) fuse in intuition. The thing-property is derived as an image-quality cut from its essence-context and fixed to a *place* posited by spirit. With this the block completes Book II's case that spirit is a dissecting power working on a given whose structure is image, not thing.

Chapters 24–28 open Book III by attacking the concept that would absorb that given into spirit: modern "consciousness", with its double the "unconscious" (Leibniz, Lipps, implicitly Freud). Chapter 25 then shows from automatisms, skills, literature and ancient *mania* that lived experience as such takes place before consciousness. Its seven propositions set the positive model: consciousness is a series of point-like acts of reflection, triggered by disturbances and fixing the boundaries between waves of experience. Chapter 26 gives the conceptual grounding. The I bears doing, the It bears happening. The mechanical process is an intransitive It-process that is not actual, and the life-process is polar "undergoing" by a *pathic* soul. Life is continuous and consciousness gappy, and spirit and life are two hostile actualities with no third. Chapter 27 makes the disturbance concrete as the *gradient* of the life-stream. Retrospective self-knowledge comes only after transformation, attention to objects comes only through affect, and violent affect splits off the outer, consciousness-capable layer of life. Chapter 28 extends this to history: all knowledge of an age's driving powers is posthumous, and past states of consciousness cannot be relived.

The block presupposes Book I's results: the atemporality of the object, the being/actuality split, things as thought-additions that cannot appear, Eleatic error. It also presupposes Book II's earlier chapters: act theory, the relational nature of property (ch. 11), objectification and estrangement (ch. 12), the grammatical subject's personal colouring (ch. 13 n. 13), and finder vs learner (ch. 14). It hands forward a number of explicit promissory notes:
- the real life-function of sensing (ch. 18, 22, 25 V);
- the dream/perception relation (ch. 16);
- the boundary-quality vs intervening qualities and the doctrine of recollection (ch. 25 VII; taken up in ch. 41, 46);
- the image/thing relation and "the law of composition of the thing" (ch. 23);
- the place as the vital occasion of thinghood (ch. 23; presumably ch. 58–59);
- the two ecstasies and visions (ch. 27; ch. 29ff., 69);
- the will's share in the splitting tendency (ch. 27; Book IV);
- non-research access to the past (ch. 28 → ch. 29 "Beholding and Mirroring").

Several tensions are worth recording.
1. **The status of spirit.** Ch. 26 calls spirit and life "two actualities", while Book I reserved "actuality" for spatio-temporal happening and placed spirit outside space and time. Ch. 26 also says there is "no intelligible reason" why they clashed in man, yet ch. 27 makes spirit a "reacting act" awakened by affect. That presupposes a regular causal or occasional link that the dualism forbids explaining.
2. **The status of sensing.** Ch. 21 reassigns the falsely so-called sensations to the soul's beholding, as "interpretive processes" that disclose images. Genuine sensing then becomes the *bodily* separating function (ch. 22), the occasion of place (ch. 23) and the ultimate ground of disturbance (ch. 25 V). Yet ch. 22 also requires it to be ensouled by beholding [199]. Genuine sensing is thus asked to be both a life-process and the source of the interruption of life, and its "real life-function" stays officially undiscovered (ch. 18).
3. **Heterogeneity vs kinship.** Ch. 19 insists that all qualities are objectively heterogeneous, while ch. 23 relies on kinships of character across modalities. Klages keeps these consistent by putting the kinships in "expressive content", not in the object, but this means the whole essence-doctrine rests on a similarity relation that has been asserted, not analysed (deferred to "elementary similarity", ch. 34).
4. **Self-reference.** Ch. 27–28 say self-knowledge and epochal knowledge are possible only from a distance, yet the book claims to diagnose its own age and to settle the experience/consciousness conflict "with a final answer" (ch. 26).
5. **Asserted evidence.** The evidential weight given to clairvoyance, psychometry and dowsing (ch. 23), and to poetic animism as testimony to essences (ch. 20–21), is asserted, not argued. It is the block's most exposed flank.

### Chapter 29 — Beholding and Mirroring (*Schauung und Spiegelung*) [280–289]

**Thesis.** The continuous flow of lived experience (*Erleben*) must be rhythmically articulated, like a wave, for the timeless, point-like act of apprehension to have anything to attach to. At each turning-point of the wave, *beholding* (*Schauen*) gives way briefly to *mirroring* (*Spiegeln*): a looking-back at the after-shine of what has just been beheld. This mirroring state is the waking state, and the psychic side of sensory impression.

**Argument.**
1. *Restating the guiding thought* (end of ch. 28): reflection arises at points where life is disturbed, and freezes what disturbed it into a lawful world of facts. Klages says the equation consciousness = disturbance of life will be borne out only gradually, and announces a change of approach [280–281].
2. *The structural argument (transcendental in form).* Experience is never present. If it flowed evenly, the absolutely present act would have nothing to aim at, and "now-consciousness" would be "a logically impossible miracle" [281]. So experience must contain turning-points that offer the act "a lasting substitute for the vanished actual now." Wave motion is the "most exact simile": the stream is undivided yet keeps ending and beginning again, as a water surface stays unbroken through its waves [281].
3. *Support from Palágyi* (*Logik auf dem Scheidewege*). Klages quotes at length [281–284]. Every "impression-judgement" is really a "recollection-judgement". Sensing swings between impression and recollection, like an unnoticed pulse. The step into judgement is a "mathematical point of time" that adds nothing to the content except validity, and it is aimed at something already past. The jug-at-the-spring simile does this work. Palágyi later replaced "recollection" with "phantasm". Klages sums up in five theses [284]: experience flows while judgement is punctual; what is lived is always already past when reflected on; the process must be articulated; a substitute must be on hand at the act; and the turning-point of each wave differs in state, offering an "object-like quid pro quo".
4. *A genealogy of the mirror theory of knowledge.* The Greek view of cognition as mirroring (the photographic plate, Democritean *eidola*) cannot be explained by visual richness alone. Its source is that the thinker reflects on thinking only in rare contemplative hours. Moved from agent to spectator, he confuses the *activity* of thinking with the contemplative *state* in which he came to know it [285].
5. *Two kinds of image, two kinds of beholding.* There are originals (*ursprüngliche Bilder*) and copies (*Abbilder*), which Klages has so far called "intuitive images" (*Anschauungsbilder*). Matching them are beholding proper and spectating beholding (*zuschauendes Schauen*), and both differ from thinking apprehension. The act attaches to the turning-point at which beholding passes into spectating, and spectating faces "after-shines" of what has been lived, not originals [285].
6. *Removing an apparent paradox.* Surely the spectator is the *undisturbed* one, so how can spectating be the precondition of disturbance? Klages answers from Nietzsche's *Birth of Tragedy*: the true beholder is "enraptured" and has visions, while the "theoretician" only contemplates. Etymology helps: *theoria*, *speculari*. Theatre began in cult festivals that had demon/man or hierophant/neophyte but no uninvolved audience. Contemplation already assumes an active wakefulness that is tormented by the will and wants "redemption" from it [286].
7. *Schopenhauer and Plato as witnesses against themselves.* Schopenhauer's aesthetic reading of the Platonic Ideas comes from the state of mirroring. He was deceiving himself when he took the "Idea", a lawful schema, for the fruit of the soul's surrender. Both he and Plato want their Ideas to be at once objective and intuitable, "remembered, phasmic". That betrays the basic experience they share: not beholding, but *looking back on something already beheld*. Standing copies underlie the Ideas, not streaming images [287].
8. *Mapping the psychic onto the bodily.* The moment of mirroring ("literally a glance of the eye", *Augenblick*) stands to the continuity of beholding as waking stands to sleeping. On the psychic side it marks the same reversal point that the onset of sensation marks on the bodily side. In dreams we are outside both sensation and mirroring, and so never mere spectators of our visions [288].
9. *Programme.* The sense-impression, taken psychically, has the character of a *copy*. The analysis of the copy begins here. A copy is not a repetition: identical mass-produced plates are "specimens", not copies. Copyhood belongs to *visible appearance*. Wood or stone sculpture is a copy only through its visible features, and its bodily presence makes it merely a second object. Visible appearance can stand in for bodily presence because it is free of place: the photographed waterfall is "everywhere", while the real one is "in its own place" [288–289].

**Key terms.** *Schauen*, *Schauung* (beholding): unconscious participation in streaming images. *Zuschauen / spiegelndes Schauen* (spectating, mirroring beholding). *Spiegelung* (mirroring). *ursprüngliches Bild* (later *Urbild*) / *Abbild* (original image / copy). *Wiederschein* (after-shine, re-shine). *Wendepunkt* (turning-point). *Wachheit* (wakefulness). *Ortsfreiheit* (freedom from place) of visible appearance.

**Contribution to the whole.** Ch. 29 sets up the wave model of articulated experience, which chs. 31–32 develop into "vital mirroring", and it locates the act's foothold in life. It introduces the theme of visible appearance and placelessness that ch. 30 develops. It carries forward the critique of Platonic/Schopenhauerian "Ideas" (chs. 6, 13). It also anticipates the split between the psychic and bodily sides of sensibility (chs. 32–33) and the later treatment of dream (Book V, ch. 55).

**Critical note.** The strongest move is the structural point. A timeless act cannot attach to a uniform flow, so some articulation of the flow is a real requirement on any theory that separates act from experience. The weak step is the jump from "there must be articulation" to the particular content of the turning-points (a mirroring of what has just elapsed). That is filled in by simile (waves, mirror) and by Palágyi's authority, not by an independent argument. The genealogy of the mirror theory is suggestive, but it is a psychological speculation stated as diagnosis.

### Chapter 30 — The Conceptual Affinity of Visible Appearance (*Die Begriffsverwandtschaft des Augenscheins*) [290–305]

**Thesis.** Only visible appearance (*Augenschein*) can depict, and so only it can carry conceptual meaning. The reason is not any refinement of the eye. It is the "empirical timelessness of shape" (*Gestalt*): visible shape is free of place and appears in an instant, so it presents "time in the medium of space" [305]. That makes it the natural vicar of the timeless object of thought.

**Argument.**
1. *Linguistic and phenomenological data.* A child takes the picture of a rose for the rose itself. The cloud's mirror-image is "the cloud once more", while its rain is something else. A phonograph's roar is an "imitated roar", not a "depicted lion" [290]. Only sight depicts.
2. *Proofs from imagination.* Sounds and smells call up *visual* images, not images of their own kind. A voice calls up a face, rarely the reverse. We remember sentences long after the voice has faded. Above all, we cannot "picture" the sense of any word (sweet, thundering, virtue, the conjunction "and") without visual shapes. Generic concepts became anthropomorphic gods and allegories [290–292].
3. *Appeal to language.* *Vorstellung* wavers between showing something and meaning something (the actor "represents" a king), which is why it became psychology's "problem child". Further cases: *Anschauung* → "conviction"; *Erscheinung*, *Phantasma*, *Bild*; *Zeitraum*, *Zeitstrecke*; *Einsicht*, *Absicht*, *Gesichtspunkt*. Indo-European cases first meant place and direction. Platonic *eidos* is "an ornamental flourish… upon the far mightier edifice of language" [291–294].
4. *Lazarus Geiger* (*Ursprung und Entwicklung der menschlichen Sprache und Vernunft*) saw this. Klages quotes him on thinking gas, electricity and even spirit as visible shapes, but corrects him on two errors: he confused visible appearance with concept, and he explained the fact by the eye's "delicacy" [293–294].
5. *The real ground: rigidity, not optical quality.* This is a genealogy of the form/matter distinction. *Forma* is the vessel. What lets it stand for the ideal is not colour but its relative *rigidity*. Shape stands to the other sensory traits as the abiding to the changing. Klages inverts the order: sight did not come first and then supply concepts. Visible appearances came to dominate *in step with* a thinking that needed time-withdrawn traits [295].
6. *Confirmation from touch.* Tangible solidity also lasts beyond the moment, and it grounds empiricism's "eternal matter" just as visible rigidity grounds idealism's "eternal forms". Materialism and idealism thus come from the rivalry of tactile and visual experience. No philosophy was ever modelled on hearing, smell or taste, because in those "middle senses" the spatial pole stays dependent on the temporal one. Tactile words: *Eindruck*, *Reiz*, *Einprägung*, *Erinnerungsspur*, *Begriff* < *begreifen* [295–296].
7. *Why visible shape beats tactile shape.* Touch gets shape only by moving over time and then abstracting the path. Sight gives the whole shape in the "empirical now". A shape seen in an instant can even *represent* movement: the lightning-lit landscape, the child's drawn waterfall [297–298]. Colours belong to the spatial pole not by their quality but because they make shapes sensible. Unshaped light phenomena (dark, fog, twilight) stay expressive rather than thing-like, hence "warm red" but not "red warm". This answers a question from ch. 23 [298–299].
8. *Expression versus meaning.* Two drawings of a waterfall can be equally recognizable while only the great artist's discloses its "down-tearing force". Visible line can absorb all kinds of qualities: wetness, glitter, heat. Humans far exceed animals in receptiveness to *relations of lines*: photographs, the child's house of four lines. This is extended to melody: transposability rests on grasping tone-relations as shapes of movement (*Tonleiter*, *Tonstufen*), which no parrot does [299–302].
9. *The key difference: position.* Tactile shape is bound to position (the blind man must re-palpate a person who has moved). Visual shape is position-free (a body mirrored five-fold). Physics' "real" and "virtual" images are both, for the science of appearance, semblance-images without a real place. "No chemical substance in the world is sensitive to shape" [302–303]. The omnipresence of visual shape is the "abutment" of the bridge to the placelessness of the universals (warmth, redness, speed…), which "occur everywhere and at all times" [303–304].
10. *Coda.* Only "a being of the eye" could become a detached and therefore thinking being, and only a thinking being's need for redemption could produce the "better beyond" of Plato and Schiller ("Das Ideal und das Leben", quoted) [304]. Formula: shape gives "of the fleeting, its experienceable presentness". The question is passed to the essence of sense-space [305].

**Key terms.** *Augenschein* (visible appearance). *Gestalt* (shape). *Abbildbarkeit* (depictability). *Begriffsverwandtschaft* (conceptual affinity). *Ortsfreiheit*, *Allgegenwart* (freedom from place, omnipresence). *Ausdrucksgehalt* versus *Bedeutungsgehalt* (expressive content versus meaning content). *Formel*: "empirical timelessness of shape".

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter grounds the earlier claim that the object of thought is extratemporal (Book I) in a feature of sensibility. It gives the genealogy of idealism/materialism that recurs in the Retrospect and Book V. It prepares ch. 31's thesis that sense-space is a mirror-like, ever-present now, and it feeds the theory of expression (ch. 23, and Book V's expressive "characters" of images).

**Critical note.** The cumulative evidence from imagination and language is impressive, and so is the inversion (thought selects sight, not sight thought). But the evidence is introspective and largely Indo-European. The blind, whose conceptual competence Klages admits, are handled only by saying they must "abstract more", which weakens the claim that visible appearance is the *substratum* of all universals. The step from "visual shape is placeless" to "universals are placeless *because of* it" is analogy presented as grounding.

### Chapter 31 — The Essence of Sense-Space (*Das Wesen des Sinnenraums*) [306–322]

**Thesis.** Between actuality-space (*Wirklichkeitsraum*, the ever-changing "flowing space" of happening) and thing-space (*Dingraum*) lies a third: sense-space or intuition-space (*Sinnenraum*, *Anschauungsraum*). It is never past and never changing, and it is identical with the "actual now" that Book I looked for and could not find. Objective time is only an aspect of objective space, a "spatialized time", whose conflation with actual time runs from Parmenides to Kant.

**Argument.**
1. *Thesis announced.* Sense-space, which we share with animals, is given not by original beholding but by a *mirroring* of what has already been beheld. It has the "form of actuality of a mirror-semblance" [306]. The semblance-character of a mirror-image cannot be seen in it, as anyone who has walked into a mirror knows. Since mirror-images can be mirrored indefinitely, the original actuality (the *Urbild*, "archetype") lies "on this side" of visible appearance. Optical mirroring and vital mirroring are related by a similarity of relations, not an identity [306–307].
2. *Distinctions recalled.* The experience of place versus the act that marks points. Receptivity for space versus receptivity for place within space: the psychic versus the bodily side. "Without sense-space, no place, and without place, no thing" [307].
3. *Phenomenological argument for the presentness and unchangeability of sense-space.* Actuality-space is ever already past, never the same, and co-determined by everything elapsed, so it cannot be beheld. Intuition-space is never experienced as past, and every change we see is referred to bodies, not to space. Even the light of a distant star is seen in a present space [308].
4. *A unity argument (reductio).* Touched smoothness and seen smoothness are qualitatively incomparable. If their spatial "outside-one-another" were as different as the qualities are, no concept of space could arise. So the spatial character of any impression is the spatial character of every impression, and sense-space must be *experienced* as a common medium. Theories based on movement sensations or occlusion cannot derive this. Simile: the searchlight beam is seen only on the dust drifting through it [309–310].
5. *Qualities have degrees, space does not.* Brighter is not larger. The carriage receding down the road is grasped as the carriage moving, not as depth growing. Goethe's "Already all nearness is far" means that things are carried off, not that space stretches [310–311].
6. *Identification.* Since sense-space is ever-present, "the actual now and intuition-space are one and the same", seen from two points of view. Language confirms it: the "linguistic equation" of *Gegenwart* (the present) and *Anwesenheit* (presence); "that which is present absolutely is sense-space". This answers ch. 2's question about the "metaphysical concretum": it is intuition-space. From it come being-there and an "always" [311–312].
7. *Derivation of objective time.* Separating what has been from what is present yields the line of time as "the extension of the ever-present space" through all past moments. "Objective time is merely one aspect of objective space" [312]. Hence spans and stretches of time, the straightness of the time-line (pure distance), and the past "behind" us. Measurability of time is bought "at the price of its detemporalization" [313].
8. *Thing-point versus space-point; a polemic against relativity.* Place is the immovable position, the thing the movable one. Relativizing all motion (sketched by Newton, recently made popular by relativity theory; Einstein is not named) violates the law of identity: A would be both at rest and moving. The concept of motion needs immovable places. It is the "false reification" of the space-point, a "futuristic dissolution" in place of Kant's subjectivization [314–315]. A triangle stays a triangle while it moves through world-space.
9. *A contradiction exposed.* If time is a fourth extension of space, the "temporal depth" grows while the three intuitive extensions do not. Space would become a growing space, a "front" of lengthening time. This contradiction "stands ineradicable" [317]. Ch. 2's limited duration of things is restated as their unintuitable motion "in the direction of growth of space" [317–318].
10. *Historical illustration.* Kant (*KrV*, Kehrbach pages quoted) calls time unchangeable and abiding, and also flowing: "the murkiest possible mixture". Replace "time" with "space" and the passages become correct [318–319]. Parmenides' fragments on Being read as descriptions of sense-space given the compactness of a body. European philosophy made no progress in 2300 years. Copernican infinite space affected theology and Newtonian physics, not metaphysics [319–320].
11. *Palágyi as precursor* (*Neue Theorie des Raumes und der Zeit*, 1901; *Das Relativitätsprinzip*, 1914). Klages claims Palágyi originated what relativity theory caricatured. "The point of time is world-space; the point of space is the stream of time." He introduced "flowing space", the "axiom of the conservation of space", "transverse spaces", and time as the symbolic fourth "longitudinal space" [320–322].

**Key terms.** *Wirklichkeitsraum* / *Ur-raum* (actuality-space, primal space, "flowing space"). *Sinnenraum* / *Anschauungsraum* / *Eindrucksraum* (sense-space). *Dingraum* / *Raumgegenstand* (thing-space). *wirkliches Jetzt* (actual now). *Immer* (the "always"). *verräumlichte Zeit* (spatialized time). *Ortspunkt* versus *Dingpunkt*. Palágyi's *Querraum* (transverse space) and *Längsraum* (longitudinal space).

**Contribution to the whole.** Cashes out Book I (chs. 2–3, the thing-point, duration, the missing "actual now", the critique of Parmenides/Kant). Supplies the account of sense-space that ch. 32 explains genetically as vital mirroring. Prepares the evaluation of Palágyi (ch. 40) and the Book V doctrine of nearness and distance (chs. 62–65; ch. 32 promises the widening of sense-space for Vol. III).

**Critical note.** The unity-of-space argument from smoothness is a real argument against associationist constructions of space. Identifying the "actual now" with sense-space is an elegant solution to Book I's puzzle. But the dismissal of relativity mistakes a claim about the *physical* indeterminacy of absolute rest for a logical claim about one point being "simultaneously at rest and in motion". Relational motion attributes no contradictory predicates, so the reductio fails against its intended target. The identification itself leans on the double sense of "presence" (temporal *Gegenwart*, spatial *Anwesenheit*): that language joins them is offered as confirmation, but it is exactly the point at issue.

### Chapter 32 — Vital Mirroring (*Die vitale Spiegelung*) [323–332]

**Thesis.** Sense-space is the present "cross-section" of an unintuitable happening that has already elapsed, and it arises through a vital mirroring at the turning-points of beholding. Sensibility involves two processes: making-present (*Vergegenwärtigung*, the soul's side, which grounds space-finding) and embodiment (*Verkörperung*, the body's side, which grounds place-finding). The first is prior: "the bodily pole… depends on the soul's pole" [331].

**Argument.**
1. *The fable.* A soul that only beheld would gaze into a "beginningless past" in which all forms dwindle away. Give it the gift of turning around, and there lies before it the cross-section of happening. A mirror arises with the turn and catches from longitudinal space a "view" [323]. Translated soberly: sense-space is present and actuality is never present. So the state through which the flowing appears as standing is best described as a mirroring of what has just been experienced [323–324].
2. *Disanalogies and analogies.* Differences: in optics, mirror-space and mirrored space are simultaneous, whereas sense-space follows on primal space. Optical mirror-space is intuitable, whereas primal space is not. Similarities: dependence (mirror-space on sense-space, sense-space on primal space), and the arising of an omnipresent reflection [324].
3. *Omnipresence and non-additivity.* The schoolboy's regress of space around space treats sense-space as part of thing-space. In fact every sense-space is *the* world-space of the senses, so sense-spaces cannot be added or measured. Illusory spaces show this: a painting's space or mirror-space is not a piece of the space in front of it. Sense-space represents thought world-space as "Peter" represents "man" [324–325].
4. *Presence requires co-experienced pastness.* If we stayed strictly at sense-space, consciousness of succession, and with it the now, would vanish. So the intuitive now must contain, unintuitably, a "having-been", a sign that it is the cross-section of the past's "longitudinal space" [325].
5. *The wave model made precise* (Fig. 6). Beholding is a pendulum movement with turning-points. The "pause" is a tiny experienceable interval around each turn, not an interruption. During movement the soul's gaze falls into the fleeing of time. At the pause it turns around. The impression-content at P2 mirrors what befell the soul between P1 and P2, and the act of apprehension (vertical arrow) attaches there. Klages stresses what licenses the word "mirroring": not the figurative relation to the archetypes, but that the property all impressions share, being-present, is the same property that makes things mirrorable [326]. The turning-points glide along with the wave, because this is the rhythm of a "happening without underlying substrate" [326–327]. Consciousness of direction is noted and set aside [327].
6. *Origin of consciousness (interposed).* Beholding is wholly unconscious and has no sensation. The pause marks the awakening of receptivity to impressions, which animals share, and the point of entry for spirit's act [326].
7. *Union of Palágyi's two theses.* "Transverse space crosses longitudinal space" and "every impression-judgement is a recollection-judgement" belong together. The touched ice: the *presence* of coldness differs from the fact "coldness". Lived presentness demands lived pastness, so the intuitive now is the mirroring of what was beheld just before. "Nothing can be recollected without the co-making-present of a spatiality that is not present" [327–329].
8. *Resolving a difficulty left from ch. 2.* If finding a thing requires two acts at different times, the second meets a new impression. Answer: a *single* act suffices, because the momentary impression already reflects the lived interval P1→P2. In spatial side-by-sideness we find the mirror of a succession, as the drawn waterfall signifies moving water [329]. Spirit supplies only the direction of consciousness. The object of thought is "merely a positional locus", and its "existence" signifies the lived interval [330].
9. *Making-present versus embodiment.* We see the full impression of ice in a mirror or in Ostade's winter landscape, without anything bodily. So what all impressions share is not bodiliness but *now-presentness*: "To render sensible means to make-present" [330]. Place-finding rests on embodiment, space-finding on making-present. Embodiment presupposes making-present, not the reverse (promised for later). Sense-space is "the original first re-seeing". "The world of consciousness-capable actuality is a world of present reflections" [331].
10. *Return to sight.* Touch gives the wall only when I go to it. Sight gives sense-space "literally uninterruptedly" as present depth. So the seeming timelessness of visible space comes from its being "the ever-and-again present reflex" of four-dimensional happening. The one remaining gap is to be closed in ch. 34 [331–332].

**Key terms.** *vitale Spiegelung* (vital mirroring). *Querschnitt* (cross-section). *Ur-raum* (primal space). *Pause / Innehalten des Schauens* (pausing of beholding). *Vergegenwärtigung* (making-present) versus *Verkörperung* (embodiment). *Gegenwärtigkeit / Jetztgegenwart* (now-presentness). *Wiedersehen* ("re-seeing").

**Contribution to the whole.** Completes the theory of the now-point begun in ch. 2 and the wave model of ch. 29. Gives the psychic foundation for all objectification (things, places, time-line). The making-present/embodiment split structures chs. 33–37 on sensation, and recurs in Book V (soul and body, chs. 58–61). The figure of the pause is the hinge for the later account of how spirit enters life.

**Critical note.** The argument from mirror-images and paintings, that impressions are marked by presentness rather than bodiliness, is neat and does real work in separating the two poles of sensibility. The model relies on spatial metaphors (turning around, cross-section, longitudinal space) to describe a process explicitly called unintuitable. And the claim that the impression at P2 "mirrors" the interval P1–P2 has no specification beyond the figure. It is closer to a postulated mechanism than a description.

### Chapter 33 — Embodiment and Making-Present (*Verkörperung und Vergegenwärtigung*) [333–341]

**Thesis.** Three spaces (thing-space, mirror-space and dream-space) share one medium, presentness. They are told apart by *kinds* of presentness: bodily, intangible, and bodiless. Bodiliness comes from a separate bodily process, embodiment, which is the experience of resistance. So the character of *actuality* (*Wirklichkeit*) of an experience must be sharply separated from its *bodiliness*. The relative weight of the two processes then serves as a key to the great world-pictures: Western idealism and materialism versus Asian, and above all Taoist, "de-bodying".

**Argument.**
1. *The problem.* How does an impression differ from an imagination of the same thing? A first guess fails: "tangible versus intangible presentness" fits mirror-space but not fantasy-space. Dream-images can have sounds, smells, temperatures and roughness. So dream-space is "bodiless image-presentness". A dreamed knife-cut is a seeming cut. That making-present can produce *all* qualities on its own is "an experiment of nature" confirming that intuition-space is a mirroring [333].
2. *Objection from a careful reader* [334]. Klages's whole account *identifies* the three spaces through common presentness, so how can he now tell them apart? He grants the point and changes the question. One medium is kept, and the real question becomes why mirror-space and dream-space are judged *dependent* on thing-space, since all three are reflections.
3. *Answer.* Being bodily, we look from thing-space into mirror-space and never the reverse. We know of dream-space only from within sense-space, and dreaming we know of no second presence. "Inner space = imagined space = non-presentness of images" when judged from waking. Waking and dream may alternate several times a second but never share one presentness. Hallucination is inner space by origin and perception-space by appearance. So the spaces separate only "through differences in their being-related to our thinking I" [335].
4. *The decisive separation.* Intangibility is not bodilessness: dreams contain tactile qualities. What differs is not touch versus the other senses but two forms of "estrangement" (*Entfremdung*), which is the reverse side of connectedness: the *making-present* estrangement and the *embodying* estrangement. The soul's connection with happening becomes, by estrangement, intuition of ever-present space. Only the resistance-experience of the "body-soul" (*Leibesseele*) yields bodiliness [336].
5. *Five consequences* [336–338]. (1) Purely bodily life-processes are unthinkable, since bodiliness presupposes an image. Touch is the predominantly *sensing* sense, and any sense "senses" to the degree that it registers intensity. (2) Vital mirroring = making-present = the process of intuition (*Anschauungsvorgang*), the mediating third between beholding and sensation. This is the "awakening of the soul". (3) The body awakens in sensation, which presupposes the soul's wakefulness. (4) Every quality can be dreamt, and optical ones dominate. Dreams only seem bodily because of dreamable tactile qualities. Proof of their bodilessness is deferred to a later discussion. (5) The most important result: what is experienced as *actual* is the present intuitive image alone. Sensation grounds the *existential judgement*, not the judgement of actuality. Dreams are fully actual to the dreamer.
6. *Resolving an apparent inconsistency* with ch. 13 (idealism as false reification) and ch. 30 (idealism as the dominance of sight). Seeing is dominated by making-present, touch by sensation. But the Western understanding treats even "forms" *as things*: existent Ideas, schemata, laws, the "thing in itself". Both Western idealism and Western materialism rest on belief in resistances. In metaphysical metaphor, spirit has "allied" itself with the bodily pole to drive out the soul [338–339].
7. *The Asian counter-path* (cultural-historical illustration, openly marked as a forward glance). Could spirit ally itself with the soul and *de-body* it, treating *being* as semblance? Indian systems resemble European idealism only in their "roofs and spires". The Buddha's "shadow, dream, soap-bubble" and the veil of Maya rest on recognizing no being behind appearances. India is an "Aryan/Asiatic mixture". Chinese Tao is pure: veneration of appearances, disbelief in resistances, *wu-wei*, *wu-hsing*. The Taoist "does not want at all". East Asian spirituality withers (flowers into straw-flowers) but does not spread like fire, unlike Christian expansion [339–341]. Chinese painting suggests the difference reaches into perception itself [341].

**Key terms.** *Verkörperung* (embodiment) / *Vergegenwärtigung* (making-present). *Ding-*, *Spiegel-*, *Traumraum* / *Innenraum* (thing-, mirror-, dream/inner space). *Bildgegenwart* (image-presentness): bodily, intangible, bodiless. *Entfremdung* (estrangement), the reverse of *Zusammenhang* (connectedness). *Leibesseele* (body-soul). *Anschauungsvorgang* (process of intuition). *Wirklichkeitscharakter* versus *Körperlichkeit*. *Existenzialurteil* (existential judgement).

**Contribution to the whole.** Fixes the triad beholding / intuition (making-present) / sensation that organizes the rest of Book III. It is the basis for ch. 34's dream/perception "struggle of spaces" and for the later theory of dream (Book V, ch. 55). It also restates the metaphysical thesis (spirit splitting the soul–body polarity, Book II), now with a typology: Occident = de-souling of the body; East Asia = de-bodying of the soul. This anticipates Book V's account of Pelasgian versus later world-pictures.

**Critical note.** Separating *actuality* (the present image) from *bodiliness* (resistance), and attaching the existential judgement to sensation, is a precise and fruitful distinction. It explains why dreams are actual while dreamt, without making them physical. But the ethnological typology rests on unexamined generalities ("races", "Aryan/Asiatic mixture"). Klages calls it a forward glance, yet it does argumentative work as "proof" that the ratio of the two processes varies among peoples. That proof is circular, because the evidence (philosophies, painting legends) is read through the very ratio it is meant to establish.

### Chapter 34 — Elementary Similarity (*Die elementare Ähnlichkeit*) [342–367]

*(Opens the Third Part, "The Psychic Foundations of Consciousness".)*

**Thesis.** The images of a life-wave combine at the turning-point into one present intuitive image by virtue of *elementary similarity* (*elementare Ähnlichkeit*). This is a lived, pre-judgemental similarity, not a relation set up by comparing consciousness. It is "the gap" in the theory of vital mirroring, now filled. The same principle explains how intuitive images affect one another: phantasm, recognition, familiarity, déjà vu, hallucination, instinct. Recollection proper (*Erinnerung*) additionally requires an act of self-reflection and so belongs only to beings with spirit.

**Argument.**
1. *Against the trace-theory* (critique of a false simile). The "trace", "engram" or "reproduction-basis" model either says harmlessly that experience alters the life-substance, or, if traces "reawaken", it substitutes a particular unexplained process for the general one. Live ruts would not resemble a travelling wagon. Habit actually *relieves* recollection: the fluent reader no longer remembers learning to read [342–344].
2. *Recollection is presupposed in the present impression.* Recollection of a past impression would be impossible "did its presupposition not already lie complete in the impression of the present" [344]. Whoever perceives consciously has co-apprehended side-by-sideness versus succession and now versus past. We recollect not because of the trace but because, *despite* it, we can tell past from present. Without the phase-shape of experience every theory of memory is a *petitio principii* [345].
3. *Filling the gap.* If experience merely streamed, each moment determined by all its predecessors, no presence would arise. At the turning-point the images between two turns are "mirrored" together, and the "present impression" coincides with *unconscious recollection*. The connection needs a positive basis: object and mirror-image are *equal*, but primal images and the intuitive image are not. Their bond is elementary similarity [345–346].
4. *Defending elementary similarity* against the "apperception psychologist", who calls similarity a subjective relation made by comparison. That holds for *judged* similarity. Without *experienced* similarity we could never find "self-identical compendia of properties" in the flux at all [346]. Similarity is not diminished equality. Equations and scales assert equality with no similarity at all. We feel that a stranger resembles *someone* before we know whom. Equality rests on sameness (*Selbigkeit*) of determining constituents [346–347]. Nietzsche: logic presupposes identical cases. Kant's cinnabar passage, thought through, shows that even properties could not be known by a being that experienced only fleetingness [347–348].
5. *Phenomenological demonstration: the ink-spot.* Seeing a spot on a tablecloth in a single glance presupposes co-apprehended similarity of the surrounding sub-parts. A scanning glance over uniform grey yields different shape-impressions, which merge into one colour by similarity. So the "law": *every sense impression presupposes the beholding of the similarity of images* [348]. The same holds on the time-pole: an instant stands out only against a course, which rests on co-experienced similarity of images apart in time [349].
6. *Vital mirroring without simile.* "From every life-wave… at the turning-point, the images belonging to it are united thanks to their similarities" [349]. Diagnosis of sensualism: it tore sense-data out of space and time, insulated them, and then spent centuries trying to glue them back into things, forgetting that judgements of sameness cannot be made over unspatial, untemporal data [349–350].
7. *The now and its direction.* Presentness contains a co-experienced sequence. The tension to "glide off" into the past gives the now its accent (cashing out ch. 3). Primitive languages often lack "to be", which attests the event-character of early thought. We place remembered states in the past, not the future, because the impression's "potential direction of movement" cannot be reversed. Hence making-present is originally making-present of something that has been, and myths too are pasts [350–351].
8. *Phantasm versus recollection-image; the struggle of spaces.* Mirror-of-a-mirror simile: noticing only the mirrored image yields a *phantasm*. Noticing the mirror as well yields a *recollection-image* (*Erinnerungsbild*) [351–352]. Impression and phantasm fight a "struggle for space". If the phantasm wins, dream-space replaces perception-space: momentary waking reverie, the "dreamer" versus the "man of action". If it loses, the impression gains "knownness" (*Bekanntheit*), which as it grows withdraws content from consciousness [352].
9. *Recognition* (*Wiedererkennen*) is neither recollection nor identification. Odysseus's dog recognizes by scent and without remembering. A plant's seasonal habits show linkage of experiences, but recognition requires the mirrorability of impressions, which only animals have. Eurykleia recognizes by a *shape*-mark (the scar), and the decisive point is that an act of *identification* precedes her recognition. Handwriting experts, fingerprinting and blood-tests identify without recognizing. "Animals recognize, but they do not identify" [352–354]. Mechanistic accounts (intensification) fail. The phantasm alters by "induction" the *meaning* of the intuitive image [354–355].
10. *No criterion of correct recognition.* There are no identities in appearance (the dog recognized an Odysseus aged by twenty years), and similarity cannot be graded. Déjà vu and estrangement from the world are mirror cases. "Correct" recognition, not false, is what needs explaining. The aid comes later, in Volume III [355–356].
11. *Kinship and conflict.* Since every intuitive image is originally the expression of a living stirring, elementary similarity = "kinship of essence" (*Wesensverwandtschaft*). The pre-conscious crystallizations follow an "Empedoclean" inclination and conflict. Hunger/satiety generates the preconceptual unit "edible". Likewise moist/dry, habitable/uninhabitable, life-protecting/life-threatening [356–358].
12. *Hallucination and race* [358–360]. If readiness for making-present outweighs embodiment, phantasms penetrate sense-space. Chinese painter-legends (Han Kan's limping horse, Li Ssu-hsün's fish, Wu Tao-tzu vanishing into his landscape) are adduced as evidence of an East-Asian predominance of making-present. Böcklin is the contrast case. Three propositions separate power of imagination, ease of illusion, and the ambiguity of illusion (from strong intuition or weak sensation).
13. *Instinct.* "Instinctive" recognitions rest on the bodily efficacy of unconscious beholding, which deep sleep does not interrupt and which is transmissible through the germ-plasm. Hering's 1876 lecture and Wilhelm Jordan's *Andachten* (long verse extract on "ancestral memory") are cited. Klages accepts Jordan's closing phrase, "memory of bodily matter", but restricts "recollection" to conscious remembering [360–363].
14. *Recollection proper.* "Animals have an astonishing memory… but they remember nothing" [363]. To take a phantasm as a recollection-image I must take my present consciousness as identical with the earlier one: I see the mirror as well, with temporal distance perspectivally foreshortened. "To remember something means… also: to remember oneself" (*sich erinnern*). The conflict of spaces prompts self-reflection: "not I who am… but I who was received it". The conflict becomes sharp only after the thing has been found, with its fixed place in space and time. Klages borrows Palágyi's phrase, coined "for a different purpose", and calls this kink a "refraction of fantasy" [363–365]. Agnosia is a counter-case: memory-phantasms block recognition. The dying dog recognized Odysseus while Penelope, absorbed in memories, did not [365].
15. *Polarity.* Similarity relations are inexhaustible, and what is recognized is selected by life-needs. Animals recognize essences (brightness, thicket, this cat). Lived *oppositions* are the vital source of all dualities: bipolar symbols, Aryan myths of light against dark (Kurus/Pandavas, Iran/Turan, Völsungs/Nibelungs), ethics' good/evil. The squirrel distinguishes reachable from unreachable nearness. The chapter closes by promising two reasons why human soul-life differs from animal soul-life [366–367].

**Key terms.** *elementare Ähnlichkeit* (elementary similarity) versus judged similarity. *Gleichheit* (equality) versus *Selbigkeit* (sameness). *Wesensverwandtschaft* / *Wesenswiderstreit* (kinship / conflict of essence). *Phantasma* versus *Erinnerungsbild* (recollection-image). *unbewußte Erinnerung* (unconscious recollection). *Kampf um den Raum* (struggle of spaces). *Bekanntheit / Vertrautheit* (knownness, familiarity). *Wiedererkennen* (recognition) versus *Identifizieren*. *Aneignungskraft* (power of appropriation). "Memory of bodily matter" (*Gedächtnis des Körperstoffs*). *Sich-erinnern* as self-remembering.

**Contribution to the whole.** Closes the gap announced at the end of ch. 32 and completes the "vital mirroring" model. Provides the vital substrate (similarity, kinship/conflict, units of meaning) that chs. 35–37 contrast with conceptual identity and association. Links recollection to self-reflection, preparing the treatment of the I (ch. 39) and voluntary memory (Vol. II). The polarity thesis prepares Book V's symbol theory.

**Critical note.** The attack on trace-theory and the ink-spot argument are strong. Both show that grasping uniformity and difference is built into perception rather than added by comparison. The vulnerable step is turning "similarity of images" into a causal power ("kinship of essence", "Empedoclean inclination") that *unites* images and decides the struggle of spaces. That reifies a relation, which is the very error Klages charges against the trace-theorists. The racial reading of Chinese legends is illustration offered as verification.

### Chapter 35 — On Animal and Human Cognition (*Vom tierischen und menschlichen Erkennen*) [368–378]

**Thesis.** The difference between animal and human lies neither in tools, language, thought or art, nor first of all in spirit (which explains nothing about what in life made spirit's entry possible). It is a prior *change of poles* within life itself. In the animal, beholding is "led on a leash" by sensation. In "original man" (*Urmensch*), sensation is governed by a beholding soul that has been set free. The one sign that cannot be argued away is that no animal ever draws a likeness. This emancipation of the intuitive image is what made man vulnerable to spirit. It also yields two stages of spirit-dependent thinking: the life-dependent ("Promethean", symbolic) and the life-detached ("Heraclean", conceptual).

**Argument.**
1. *Sensory differences first.* Human dependence on visible form went with the withering of smell. A whole animal world of scents (and of pressure, magnetism, orientation) is closed to us, and we wrongly credit animals with "intelligence" for feats done by scent-meanings [368].
2. *Against "homo sapiens"* (conceptual point). Saying spirit distinguishes man explains nothing about *what change in life* allowed spirit to join it. Spirit comes from outside (*thyrathen*, Aristotle), but the change into original man did not at once make man spirited [369].
3. *The thesis of pole-reversal* (asserted, presented as a finding of the metaphysics). The animal is "bodily awakened". Original man arose by "the liberation of beholding from the rule of sensing". This was an abrupt change of state like sleep to waking, not a gradual transition. Its effect makes life *more like* the world-interwoven sleep of plants. The animal is held behind "the insurmountable barrier of presentness" of drive-bound impression. Original man lives in a world of *bodily images*, and his expression expresses soul and so reveals "the essence of actuality itself". Original man's state was later remembered as "divine or demonic" [369–370].
4. *Illustrations.* The cow has impressions of meadow and dog, but her intuition never gains enough independence to drive the representation of what she has lived. "The sheep on the alp enjoys… the freshness of the brook… only not — the view!" Eagles, buzzards and migrating birds (flying by night!) have no intuitive images of their landscapes [370–371]. The animal's lack of memory is restated: it lacks the "life-content of distance", and its making-present is bound to place [370].
5. *Disposing of rival criteria* (survey of zoology). Tool-use: insects and fungus-growing ants (Deegener). Language: ape-languages and the endless dispute. Thought: Cartesian mechanists versus trainers and "calculating horses". Art (Schiller): the bowerbird's "love-garden". None is unassailable [371–372].
6. *The decisive criterion.* A circus horse, or a dog or cat whose paws plainly could do it, never sketches the simplest outline in the sand. Children and Stone Age man do so spontaneously. So the lack is neither motor nor "intelligence". Animals "distinguish shapes in so far as differences of shape serve to distinguish… bodies; but they never distinguish shapes as such" [372–373]. Original drawing is not copying but production from memory-phantasms [373–374]. Klages claims this as metaphysics bearing fruit on a factual question that biologists missed.
7. *From pole-reversal to spirit.* The stress on beholding brings a stress on making-present. The intuitive image's place-free, object-like independence "prepared" it to "tip over into a separate form of existence". At a moment of extreme loosening, spirit's "severing blow" split off the timeless thing from the temporal image [374].
8. *Two stages and a stratigraphy.* Promethean (life-dependent, symbolic) thinking precedes Heraclean (life-detached) thinking, and these deepen the earlier biocentric/logocentric distinction (ch. 14, shallow and deep reflection). The earlier stratum survives as an unconscious motive force. "What unfolds… into a temporal sequence presents itself, in cross-section, as a layering of strata". An absolutely detached thinking would not exist. Its goal would be the "re-excretion of spirit out of life", which Klages holds to be the fate of mankind, "already decided inwardly" [374–375].
9. *Two tributaries of meaning.* Each impression stands in two contexts: the chain of past life-moments and the images made present in it. So meaning is fed now by the soul's state, now by extra-psychic actuality. Klages avoids "subjective/objective" and speaks of weakly versus strongly *objectivizing* states. For the animal, meaning is set by vital importance (the bang as a signal to flee).
