The alternative needs an I behind the I, ad infinitum [561].
11. *Drives vs motive-springs.* Drives (*Triebe*) are the substrate of impulses not referable to fact ("elementary" and "animalic" ones). A purely animal being forms no purposes. **Motive-springs** (*Triebfedern* = "interests") are the substrate of impulses referable to fact. "The problem of will coincides with the problem of the genesis of motive-springs out of mere drives" [562]. Conversions, the "new life" made by one resolution, are themselves choices made because of a suffered impulse, with a motive-spring beneath it. No intention exists without a "because". The true cause of willing is a life-process to which a groundable thought of the goal has attached itself *in statu nascendi* through the split [563].
12. *Character is variable.* Acquired motive-springs are a "mean proportional" of innate ones and contingent circumstances. Twins raised alike still diverge. Conversion succeeds or fails according to the character at the time [563–564].
13. *Difference from determinism, and close of Part One.* The command of spirit really contends with necessity and brings about changes in appearance. Inwardly it must *modify* existing impulses by splitting them. Outwardly, mechanical causality cannot by definition include a purely spiritual act. Klages calls that the standpoint of a consistent determinism and leaves it untouched. He announces instead that spirit's "immeasurable effects" happen "this side of" all causal connection. Mechanical law is "an extremely artificial net with which one imagined one could scoop out the ocean" [565].

**Key terms.** *Befehl* (command of spirit); *Willensantrieb* (impulse of will); *Antrieb* (impulse, suffered); *Beweggrund / Motiv* (the purpose seen as its "why"); *Zweck / Absicht* (purpose, intention); *sachbeziehbare Antriebe* (impulses referable to fact); *Triebfedern* (motive-springs, "interests") vs *Triebe* (drives); outer and inner necessity; the will dispositions (*Spannkraft*/energy, ease of will, *Entschlußfähigkeit*/resoluteness).

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter finishes Part One with a compatibilist-sounding position. The I decides, and decides according to its two-fold constitution. It defines what Part Two must explain: the genesis of motive-springs from drives (ch. 44–47), and what the spiritual act does to an impulse. It also promises the "outer side" (ch. 48–51): spirit's effects on appearance outside mechanical causality. The treatment of motives is used again in ch. 49 (true vs supposed purpose).

**Critical note.** The point that deliberation is itself willed, and that motive-*thoughts* lack force of their own, is sharp and anticipates later criticisms of belief-desire models. The vulnerable step is the claim that the command is "the I's own" yet "according to the measure" of a constitution. That is either determinism by another name or a regress Klages blocks only by stipulating that the I is a mere "geometrical locus". The asymmetry he wants (command not under necessity, impulse under it) is asserted, not demonstrated.

### Chapter 44 — Critique of Previous Conceptions of Drive (*Kritik bisheriger Triebauffassungen*) [566–575]

**Thesis.** Almost everything written about drives is a "heap of nonsense" [566]. It interprets drive by analogy with will. It reads purposes into impulses, studies the consequences of drives instead of the drives themselves, and projects the I's interest in self-preservation onto all life as a "self-preservation drive". This utilitarian teleology, shared by idealism, materialism, vitalism and Darwinism, turns nature into a business firm and blocks any real psychology of drives.

**Argument.**
1. *The task.* Part Two must show what the spiritual act separates off in the impulse. That needs a concept of drive, but the existing literature is worse than ignorance. Partial exceptions: Romantic philosophy, and Nietzsche, whose concept of drive was bent by the "will to power" [566].
2. *Three mechanisms of distortion.* Whether one reads drive as will (Lipps) or will as drive (Schopenhauer), spirit gets carried into the sub-spiritual. (a) Will pursues purposes, so the life-impulse is said to purpose and nature becomes a system of purposes. (b) The purposes of will are read off from what they achieve, so the *consequences* of drives are studied in place of the drives. (c) Only an I wills and asserts itself, so the I's self-preservation interest becomes a universal self-preservation *drive* [567].
3. *Nutrition.* Animals eat without knowing about calories. A newly hatched chick pecks grain, birds migrate. Positing a "nutritive drive" grounded in self-preservation yields "unpurposed purposes, unthought thoughts, unconscious consciousness" [567]. A financially trained "nature" is planning on the animal's behalf.
4. *Reproduction (reductio).* Care of the young, mothers who sacrifice themselves, the male spider eaten in mating: none of this fits self-assertion. So a second, species-preserving purpose is brought in, and "nature" becomes a bank director with the genera and species as department heads [568]. Klages notes "the conceptual pattern of the human state". A purpose needs a purposer, so the species would have to be a person. The naturalist then dissolves the person into drives that already have the calculating I smuggled into them [569].
5. *Utility can never be refuted.* Pleasure is a "pleasure-premium" (Schopenhauer). Play, adornment and wandering drives are each given some profit. The thousands of rotting rowan-berries and rose hips are "insurance". Flower colours exist for insects, yet some insects and fish are partly colour-blind, and diurnal birds are nearly blue-blind but blue-feathered (Erhard) [569–570]. Klages notes that the research is changing and does not rest his case on it. The method personifies nature as a "colossal factory", carrying the competition of a Mammon-ruled society over onto nature. A verse from Jordan mocks anthropocentric teleology [570].
6. *Genealogy.* The slander of life starts with the "plebeian Socrates" in the money economy of the Greeks. Idealism and materialism are secretly allied, and idealism is more consistent in its error [571]. Kant calls purposiveness merely regulative, yet says no Newton will explain a blade of grass. Carried through, purposes need a hierarchy of purposers: individual, variety, species, genus, God, the creative Logos, Platonic "Idea" [571]. In Carus the "Idea" slides between organism, Archeus, purpose, product, *vis vitalis*, a higher intelligence, and a string of substitute words (image, prototype, type, entelechy, life) [572]. Since Aristotle all such views are emanations of the intelligence we find only in ourselves. Modern vitalism hides behind them the "Judaic" belief that a purposing will begets the world [572]. Klages lists self-preservation in Spinoza, Leibniz's monads, Fichte's I, Herbart's Reals and Schopenhauer's Will [573].
7. *Model case.* Schiller's lines saying nature keeps the world's machinery going "through hunger and through love" equal nutritive drive plus reproductive drive. Darwinism presupposes the same pair and shrinks purposiveness to "poorhouse dimensions": survival by giving up one's own character to adapt ("stretch yourself to fit the blanket") [573]. This ranking of drives by fitness for competition underlies all theories of life, mechanist and teleological alike.
8. *The pessimistic flip.* Hobbes's and Darwin's "war of all against all", and hunger described as a "drive to murder", make the planet a Satan's work (God = Satan), an endless regress of means with no end [574]. Klages attacks modern writing on sexuality as "repugnant twaddle".
9. *Outlook.* Klages states his own view that humanity "will shortly have played itself out". Nietzsche's work is a volcanic eruption that may yet start a quite new natural science before "the iron curtain falls" [574–575]. Present knowledge of drives stands to drives as the fact that water softens beans stands to the properties of water. Scope of what follows: not a complete doctrine of drives, but how drive takes effect in impulse-experience and what hold it offers to spirit [575].

**Key terms.** *Trieb* (drive) vs *Willensantrieb*; "self-preservation drive" (*Selbsterhaltungstrieb*) and "reproductive drive" as projections; teleology and utilitarianism of nature; "Idea", entelechy, *vis vitalis* as masks of spirit; Darwinian adaptation as giving up one's own character.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter is the negative preliminary for ch. 45. It extends ch. 41's ban on reifying the end into biology, and applies the earlier claim that the I and its self-assertion (ch. 9, 39) are spirit's, not life's. It prepares the positive thesis that drives are not purposive but directed at images. It promises the dissolution of mechanical causation (ch. 50–51).

**Critical note.** The core logical point is sound and well aimed. Purposive language without a purposer is either metaphor or an unacknowledged theology, and utility explanations can explain anything after the fact. But the chapter is mostly ridicule. It does not deal with the mechanistic reading of selection (functional explanation without intention). That is exactly the reading that escapes the "purpose needs a purposer" dilemma, and Klages files it under "poorhouse" teleology without argument.

### Chapter 45 — Drive and Drive-Impulse (*Trieb und Triebantrieb*) [576–599]

**Thesis.** A drive is the *vital cause of movement*. In the drive-impulse (*Triebantrieb*) the embodied soul feels a non-intuitive *pull of images* (*Zug der Bilder*), the "essence" of water, for instance, working on the thirsty creature like a magnet on iron filings. Because bodies are separate, this pull combines with a felt *lack* (*Mangelleiden*, privation). The goal of a drive is a polar, complementary image, not a purpose, and goal-direction does not require any representation of the goal. Vital causation is therefore different in kind from mechanical causation: the goal causes the movement.

**Argument.**
1. *Definition from experience.* Every impulse is an impulse to move, and every felt impulse belongs to a body that can move. So drive is the "vital cause of movement". Motive-springs (ch. 43) are "causes of will". The task, which "still seems paradoxical today", is to explain "how spirit alters the life-impulse without the addition of a new impulse" [576].
2. *Kind vs strength.* The usual analysis separates the intensity of an impulse from its quality. Klages notes in passing that strength is really only one side, the other being direction. The *kind* of an impulse can be specified only by the images it would unite with or flee. Example: Aristotle's hunger as an urge toward the dry and thirst toward the moist [577]. Consequence: there are as many drives as there are distinguishable fulfilling images. Water, milk, Bordeaux, Rhine wine and Tokay each satisfy a different thirst, and for humans, who respond to distance-features of images, so does drinking from a glass rather than a cup. Drives therefore run into the thousands, and into the millions across ages and individuals [577–578].
3. *Love as illustration.* Love changes across the seven-year periods of life, with each kind of object (parents, friends, rulers, keepsakes, landscapes, the fatherland), and within sexual love across practices and persons [578–579]. Man's love and woman's love differ in kind because their objects differ. Klages also asserts that predominantly homosexual love is not heterosexual love "hopped over" onto the other class of objects, and not an effect of "feminine admixture" (his examples: Caesar, Michelangelo, Shakespeare). It is a difference reaching down to the root, which brings a different world-image with it [579–580]. This is offered as typology, not demonstrated.
4. *Does the species concept dissolve?* No, any more than "red" dissolves because there are many reds. Pure qualities can be communicated only by pointing. The person blind to red, or incapable of love, cannot be taught what they lack [580–581]. Result in passing: "there is no specific peculiarity of a feeling in which the peculiarity of a stirring does not share, and conversely" [582]. Every mood has an impulse-side.
5. *Admitted gap.* Klages concedes that the concept of impulse lacks premises in Volume I. The doctrine of sensation and of embodiment is postponed (to Book V). He proceeds on unproven propositions [582–583]. *Assumed:* once images are embodied, movement stands out from mere happening, and every experience of a self-being is also an impulse undergone [583].
6. *Core theorem.* In pure beholding, estrangement and connectedness of soul and image are in balance. Embody both, and estrangement shows fully, because "where a body is, no other body is". The incompleteness of the connection then appears as a *pull* from the image that becomes an impulse to move toward touching or incorporating it [583–584]. Proof by elimination: neither physiology (chemistry) nor sensation-theory (summed visceral sensations) explains why the movements of a thirsty animal "fall precisely upon the finding of water". Only the image of water, acting as a pull, does [584].
7. *Against "phantasms of the goal" (empirical examples).* A polyp repositions its tentacles when hungry. An octopus turns the crab's unprotected rear to its mouth. Ants carry aphid larvae to the roots of grain. Young migratory birds fly to Africa without a guide. A moorhen chick inside the egg stops hammering at the hen's warning call. Insects provide for broods that do not yet exist. Goal-determinacy is not a representation of the goal [585]. Recalling ch. 23: images are *actual*, the "weaving power of all actuality", as real as physical forces. "Image of water" and "essence of water" are interchangeable [585–586].
8. *Romantic witnesses (long quotation).* Passavant (1837): drive and object are "two complementary poles", like the earth's magnetic poles or the after-image complementary colour. Treviranus: instinct as "productive power of imagination". G. H. Schubert (1833): the individual lives "for the sake of a higher whole", and lack is "the vessel into which the forces of life flow" [586–590]. Klages says they confused spirit and soul but came close to his polar concept. Note 6 adds Cuvier (1836): animals act from innate images, "a kind of dream or vision", like somnambulists. Klages calls it the deepest insight into instinct he knows, and treats Darwinism as a relapse from it.
9. *Why separateness becomes a pull.* A thought-experiment: a bodiless soul would also be placeless, so there would be no distance and the pull would vanish the moment it arose [587–588]. With bodies, the disturbed bond is restored by eliminating the space in between. Language agrees ("drawn to", "attracted by"). Against Schopenhauer: a world-will would make water *wish* to be drunk and sheep wish to be eaten by tigers. The point is to separate the "pathic drive-impulse" from the "active impulse of will" [588].
10. *Lack as the inner side of the pull.* The child grabbing apples shows that seduction is met by a desire from within. Phenomenologically the pull should be described from inside. What is found there is *privation*, a suffering of lack. Every lack is a lack *of something*: drive-impulses are always *directed* intensities, though not physical vectors [590–591]. Yet need as such does not show what is lacking. Privation comes from separateness from the place of what is lacking, so it leads over into intensity.
11. *Two sources of strength.* Depth of experience (the power of the image) and disturbability (lack) differ. "It entices me powerfully" is not "I feel the lack of it keenly". Examples: the urge to throw oneself into the surf from a cliff, against the parched wanderer grabbing a jug. Myths of sirens, the Lorelei, the Pied Piper, and Hebbel's "Rubin" show "image-originated" pull [591–592]. Strength *as magnitude* is known only as *counter-pressure* against obstacles to union [592].
12. *Reactive vs body-originated impulses.* Body-originated (*leibgeboren*) impulses such as hunger and thirst arise with inner-bodily impressions. Reactive impulses arise with outer impressions. Both follow the non-intuitive pull of a non-present image. Seeing water is not drinking it [593–594]. Five-point summary of the soul–body polarity: the embodied soul experiences images only *through* impressions, and the living body is the immediate counter-pole of the soul's suffering, *transmitting* the pull of images [594–595].
13. *Vital vs mechanical cause.* The falling piece of plaster does not fall *because* of where it lands. For the thirsty animal, "the drinking of water is really the vital cause of those movements" [595]. Drives are "living forces", meaning goal-directed and ensouled. "Direction" in physics is an angle. In biology it is the conditioning of a course of movement by its goal. This is not a relation to the future, which would require knowledge of objective time, since drives are unconscious. Vital time is "the generative ground of what we call the future" [595–596].
14. *Refinements.* Drive can start, stop or redirect movement (the dog snapping at meat, the cat clawing the sleeve). Its movements need not be translatory (birdcalls). Avoidance is goal-directed too ("negatively phototropic" amoebae are alive). Longing for images of life has fear of images of death as its obverse [596–597]. There are analogies with physics: tension and movement alternate like potential and kinetic energy, and force is measured by future displacement (deferred to ch. 51) [597]. Satiation flips the sign (an over-sated man flees the smell of roast meat). Drives follow rhythms: thirst at roughly regular intervals, rutting seasons, migration, hibernation [598].
15. *Summary propositions I–V* [598–599]. (I) The drive-impulse is the felt pull of essences that can appear, and the urge to move takes its direction from polar images. (II) Two sides: body-originated need (*vis a tergo*) and the image that promises stilling (*vis a fronte*). (III) Strength fuses need and depth of beholding into counter-pressure. (IV) A rhythm of waxing and waning. (V) Every being has many drives, as many as there are kinds of privation and of pulling images.

**Key terms.** *Trieb* (drive) = vital cause of movement; *Triebantrieb* (drive-impulse); *Zug der Bilder* (pull of images); *Mangelleiden* / *Entbehrung* (suffering of lack, privation); *vis a tergo* / *vis a fronte*; *Gegendruck* (counter-pressure) as the measure of strength; body-originated vs reactive impulses; *Bewegungsdrang* (urge to movement); polarity and complementarity of drive and image.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter supplies the material that the act of will splits (ch. 46). It cashes out the doctrine of the actuality of images (ch. 23, 31) for animal behaviour, and flags dependencies on the still-missing theory of sensation and embodiment (Book V, esp. ch. 58–61). It sets up the contrast between vital and mechanical causality, the subject of ch. 50–51, and the dynamics of tension used in ch. 49.

**Critical note.** The argument by elimination in step 6 is strong. It shows that summed sensations and physiological processes do not explain *why* the movements converge on the goal. Goal-directedness without a goal-representation is a real explanandum. But calling the explanans a "pull" exerted by the image or essence names the explanandum rather than explaining it, and Klages admits the embodiment premises are unproven [583]. The magnet analogy seems to bring back the physical-force model. Klages answers that physics borrowed its force concepts from the experience of drives [597], but that reversal is only promised (ch. 51) at this point, not shown.

### Chapter 46 — Drive and Impulse of Will (*Trieb und Willensantrieb*) [600–613]

**Thesis.** The act of will splits off the *specific character* (the image-content) of an impulse that is referable to fact and keeps its *pull* as an urge to move with no direction of its own. Spirit's command then gives that urge a direction. With the image's pull (*vis a fronte*) gone, the I takes over the direction-giving role that privation (*vis a tergo*) plays in the drive [607]. A purpose is a *thought* that fixes a goal *within happening*, just as an object fixes something within appearance. From this follow: self-reflection is first occasioned by willing (*volo ergo sum*); every judgement is at root a negation; truth and error belong only to judgement; and the source of error is interest acting on a weakened sense of actuality.

**Argument.**
1. *Answer to an objection to ch. 41.* The boundary-point was derived from the presentness of impressions. Inner-bodily impressions, the reverse side of the drive-impulse, also have presentness, so impression and impulse can be told apart at the turning-point by which predominates, the intuitive appearance or the pull [600].
2. *Parallel structure of the two acts.* In the act of apprehension, actuality's *happening*-side is torn off (duration belongs only to existence) and its *appearance*-side is transformed into properties. The thing is "the sum of its properties" [601]. In the act of will the reverse holds: the *specificity* of happening is split off, and its *pull* remains, transformed "down to the root" [601].
3. *Not raw drives.* If raw drive-impulses could be split, strength would remain as a goalless urge. But there is no purely directionless urge, and animal drive-impulses cannot be split. Only impulses referable to matter, which presuppose motive-springs, the personal I, and at least one prior act of apprehension (ch. 38), can be split [601–602].
4. *Telling drive-impulses apart within the person.* Violent surges do not prove that a drive is at work, because "interests" can also produce affect-actions (ch. 31). A loss of self-reflection may be either a loss of will-power or a loss of self [602]. **Criterion:** pleasure (*Lust*, sensual delight) and its pole anxiety (*Angst*) are the signs of goal-giving drives. Klages's example is a rapist's lying in wait: his ambush and overpowering are genuine volitions driven by *incentives*, and only at the moment of satisfaction does drive replace will [602–603]. The example is grim and chosen to make a structural point. Feelings of success, such as winning a lottery or a stock-market coup, are not pleasure of this kind. Sipping water from a spring is [603].
5. *Anxiety vs fear (linguistic and clinical).* Fear (*Furcht*, *timor*) has a definite object. Anxiety (*Angst*, *pavor*) has none. "Every anxiety is fear of death", but felt fear of death differs from thinking about death, so that some people kill themselves *from* fear of death [603–604]. Free-floating anxiety attaches to plausible objects, and a hypochondriac may be calm in real danger. In a theatre fire, panic, a drive set loose by the "co-fear" of seeing others afraid, works *against* self-preservation [604–605]. So anxiety, like pleasure, is a drive and not an interest. Where these mood-tones are absent, the impulse is one referable to fact.
6. *The derivation of the purpose.* Example: an intellectual worker decides to take a walk. His supposed motive (fatigue) may not be the real motive-spring (the sunshine). In the resolve, the generic character was split from an impulse referable to fact, and spirit "forged the relation-to-object" that was present in it as a possibility [605]. What remains is a directionless urge to move, which the command directs at once. The bearer is separated from the goal, so "the willing I … pursues purposes of its own activity" [606]. "Every purpose is merely a thought or … a concept". The object fixes a direction of *relation* in the capacity for impressions, and the purpose fixes a direction of *effect* in the urge to move [606].
7. *Phenomenological proof of the difference.* Exertion, straining and striving are unique to will. Drive-movements are at bottom expressive movements. A chamois fleeing, or panic, may be violent, but the panicked man knows afterwards that "it was not he who exerted himself" [606–607]. In willing, once the *vis a fronte* (the pull of images) falls away, the I steps into the place of the *vis a tergo*, glossed here as "the directional determinacy of the urge to movement" that comes from bodily privation [607]. (Ch. 49 [677–678] instead says the resolve replaces the *vis a fronte*. See the Arc.) Its commands are carried out only through a recurring conflict of driving and inhibiting impulses, which is promised for ch. 49. Note 8 meets a puzzle: the act must split off the very impulse that occasioned it. This is possible because the act takes no time. The difference between the two acts is then that apprehension splits off a content alien to the soul, and will splits off a content proper to it.
8. *The bare will is directed against what exists.* "I will something" presupposes something existing as the resistance it means to overcome. So volition is "an urge to activity directed against the momentary situation of the world" [607].
9. *Origin of self-reflection.* Ch. 12 showed *why* self-reflection is possible but not why it actually happens. A being that only apprehended would have no motive to apprehend itself. The person finds himself in experiencing a volition *against* what he apprehends, that is, as a demanding power. Linguistically, "there stands a tree" does not point to a speaker, but an imperative's "ought" uncovers the one who commands. "Instead of *cogito ergo sum* … *volo ergo sum*" [607–608]. Note 9 credits the formula to Bouterwek, whom Maine de Biran followed and who first pushed Schopenhauer toward a metaphysics of will. The man of deeds still reflects little, because reflection opposes willing.
10. *Negation and judgement.* If self-activity is directed against something, the object becomes something "thrown against". Negative judgements are disguised acts of will ("I deny having seen it"). Klages cites Bantu negative conjugation and Finnish conjugated negation (note 10) [608]. Further, "all commandments are prohibitions": a judgement raises its content to law and forbids that it was ever otherwise. It thereby denies actuality's changeability and robs it of its past. "The positive judgement is the original negation", and negative judgements are derived [608–609].
11. *Order of acts at the origin of the I.* At least one act of apprehension must come first, so the I's first beginning is apprehensive, a counter-blow against the actuality of something lived. But that boundary would dissolve at once without the boundary set on the urge to move by an act of will, the judgement that seals it [609–610]. Apprehension shows spirit's reactive dependence on life. Judgement shows spirit *binding* life. Complete reflection is two acts, the second an act of will, which is what the scholastics sensed.
12. *Truth and error.* Lived experience and bare apprehension lie on this side of truth and error. "Truths are true judgements, errors erroneous judgements" [610]. Two cases: (a) **one-sidedness**, where judgements made for thought-facilitation ("logocentric") drift from actuality without being false, and every concept was itself produced by a judgement; (b) **falsification**, where interest becomes the cause of holding-to-be-true ("the wish is father of the thought") [610–611]. Every original error posits a being with no actuality behind it, or denies one that has, so "every error is falsification of actuality" [612]. The executive power of error lies in the will. Its *possibility* lies in the soul: in how far spirit depends on the actuality-content of experience. Thinking deepens with the nearness of spirit to actuality. The personal **sense of actuality** (*Wirklichkeitssinn*) is the soul's capacity to "fetter spirit". When it weakens, shallowness, perversity of thought, abnormality of character and "derangement of spirit" follow [612–613].

**Key terms.** *Willensantrieb* (impulse of will); splitting (*Spaltung*) of an impulse into split-off specificity and remaining pull; *Zweck* as thought or concept fixing a goal in happening; *Anreiz* (incentive) vs drive; *Lust* / *Angst* (pleasure and anxiety) as drive-markers; *Furcht* vs *Angst*; *Streben* / *Anstrengung* (striving, exertion); *volo ergo sum*; the positive judgement as original negation; one-sidedness vs falsification; *Wirklichkeitssinn* (sense of actuality).

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter answers the question set in ch. 43 (what the act does to the impulse) and completes ch. 41's account of judgement. It grounds self-reflection (backing ch. 12) and ties the theory of judgement to ch. 9's account of "assertion". It prepares ch. 47 (will to truth, sense of actuality) and ch. 48 (the will directed "against" the present, and so time). The fixing of the past by judgement anticipates ch. 48's derivation of time-consciousness.

**Critical note.** The structural parallel in step 2 is elegant: apprehension keeps the *how* and loses the *happening*, while will keeps the *pull* and loses the *how*. The pleasure/anxiety criterion for telling drive from interest is concrete and testable in principle. But "the positive judgement is the original negation" slides between *excluding contradictories* (a logical property of any assertion) and *denying actuality's changeability* (a metaphysical charge). Fixing a truth about the past does not obviously deny that the thing has changed since. That equivocation carries a great deal of the anti-spirit thesis.

### Chapter 47 — On the Will to Truth (*Vom Willen zur Wahrheit*) [614–626]

**Thesis.** The will to truth is the motor side of the soul's sense of actuality. It is a motive-spring of the *personal* soul, formed when the pull of *actuality itself* is split off. Because every judgement it attains is also an act of spirit's will to mastery, the will to truth contradicts itself: deep reflection presses back toward origins and actuality, which judgement can never seize. Its true kin is the image-maker's will to work, the "self-negation of willing". It is opposed to the will to deed and to the "shallow", purpose-led reflection behind science and technology.

**Argument.**
1. *Problem.* Concrete thinking and willing are chains that mix compelled and self-active acts. If every judgement involves spirit's will to self-assert, how does the truth-seeking judgement differ from a will indifferent to truth? Error comes from interest (ch. 46). Is the will to truth psychic or spiritual? [614]
2. *Answer.* A spiritless soul has no will to truth, because it does not judge. But the "actuality-weight" of what is lived through draws spirit down. Since every drive comes from a pull of images, the actuality contained in images can also pull, and splitting that pull yields the purpose of truth-seeking. The will to truth is "nothing other than the motor side of the sense of actuality", belonging to the constitution of the *personal* soul [615].
3. *Against intuitionism.* Feelings of certainty (illumination, revelation) do not guarantee truth. (a) Every proof rests on sudden ideas: Goethe's leaf-spiral, a mathematician's more elegant proof, a child learning its mother tongue. Only the *testing* of the judgement decides. (b) Vehemence of experience is not actuality-weight [615–616].
4. *The tragic self-contradiction.* Even passionate love of truth yields only judgements. In judgement spirit's will to mastery takes part, so the result is "a lump of ice no longer to be melted" drawn from the flood of actuality [616]. Shallow reflection builds bridges out of such lumps. Deep reflection feels "betrayed", strives "from knowing to surmising", and would pay for removing what separates it with its own destruction [616–617]. This conflict "illuminates the life-hostility of the will and hence of spirit" [617].
5. *Deep critique.* Not Herbart's working-over of concepts, but asking every concept "of how much feeling for actuality do you bear witness, of how much will to mastery of spirit?" Purposive thinking climbs, presses outward and forward. Contemplative thinking descends, presses inward and backward toward the *archai*. If it found the root of thinking it would tear it out, "murdering, together with the willing, the ground of the possibility of willing" [617]. Measured against actuality, every truth is "tendentious one-sidedness". The measure is a *feeling* of actuality, not a judgement.
6. *The Janus-head: assertion vs statement (appeal to language).* Called an *assertion* (*Behauptung*), judgement is a concealed law: it forbids acting as if the truth were unasserted and forbids asserting its contradiction. Klages again connects this to "Jahwism" and the scientific "lawful order". Nietzsche, out of an "admixture of priestliness", turned the researcher back into commander and lawgiver [618]. Called a *statement* (*Aussage*), it opens an "objectivity" in which feeling and will "run out of breath". "Statement" points to apprehension, "assertion" to the act of will [618]. Spirit finds existing things only because sensibly lived actuality compels it. "Without being ever and again referred to an actuality … being would rather be non-being" [619]. Spirit's "No" becomes the being of something extra-spiritual only because actuality throws it back. If language had grown only out of animal cries plus spirit, there would be only imperatives. Declarative sentences are owed to the will to truth [619–620].
7. *Being as nothing.* What the concept of being grasps is "the nothing". The actuality it points to cannot be grasped. Aristotle was right that first substance cannot be predicated, but first substance is itself a *product* of judgement, since "existing" already denies happening [620]. Klages quotes Zhuangzi (Knowledge, Do-Nothing-Say-Nothing, Wild-Whirl, the Yellow Emperor) for "those who grasp it do not speak it" [620–621]. Klages's advance on Taoism: the content of a genuine *docta ignorantia* is judgement's *indicative* capacity, and its limit is judgement's purposive, conceiving capacity. The cognitive purpose alone aims at the unattainable, at happening [621].
8. *The artist.* Just as a drive's pull ends in union, a pull of images on the far-beholding soul ends in objectification in lasting material. The images "make use of his spirit". The work succeeds to the extent it is *born* and fails to the extent it bears traces of arbitrary will [621–622]. Since the Renaissance artists have had to criticise their own willed form. Klages quotes an unnamed passage on "latecomers" walking "dizzying footbridges". Researcher and artist alike belong to the image-makers. "The only possible life-affirming achievement of willing takes place through the self-negation of willing. What could not be a miracle wants to become a work; what could not be a work becomes a deed" [622–623]. **Will to work** (*Werkwille*) is set against **will to deed** (*Tatwille*).
9. *Shallow reflection and its critique.* Relating objects of thought to one another, not to actuality, can be governed by the will to mastery without being false. The drive to appropriate stands to personal egoism as abstract to concrete, and serves arbitrary will by supplying "takings-note" [623]. Newton defined force and mass not by asking what they are but by what would make motions calculable. Deep reflection would show them "enemies of actuality" [624]. Klages links this to technology and to a "devastation of the face of the earth" beside which Genghis Khan's misdeeds look like boys' pranks.
10. *How can concepts hostile to actuality succeed in technology?* Through extreme one-sidedness, which keeps exactly as much of actuality as is needed to *use* it [624]. Examples: the boulder seen by the landscape painter and by the blasting engineer, the grove by the painter and by the speculator, the waterfall by the painter and by the electrician [625]. Concessions: meter-wide lenses, microgram weighings, counting chromosomes and wireless telephony to Adelaide require *some* feeling for actuality, "in one respect … far beyond" intuition's (promised for later chapters) [625]. Against that: a Greek gem, a Chinese vase, a Japanese sword-guard, a list of stones, painters of atmosphere (Ruysdael, Claude, Turner, Whistler, Böcklin), East Asian "distanceless depth". All of these disclose properties that mineralogy, optics and geometry cannot reach [625–626]. Thing-reflection and image-reflection meet at a single point and then diverge. Since "actuality is image", even the objective thought-purpose speaks more of spirit's activity than of actuality [626].

**Key terms.** *Wille zur Wahrheit* (will to truth) = motor side of *Wirklichkeitssinn*; *Wirklichkeitsgewicht* (actuality-weight); deep vs shallow reflection (*Tiefbesinnung* / *seichte Besinnung*); *Behauptung* vs *Aussage* (assertion vs statement); *Werkwille* vs *Tatwille* (will to work vs will to deed); *docta ignorantia*; thing-reflection vs image-reflection.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter closes Part Two. It turns ch. 46's sense of actuality into a theory of deep and shallow knowing, and of art as image-objectification, looking ahead to Book V (efficacy of images, ch. 66–70). It sets up the Third Part's narrowing to the "will to deed" (ch. 48–54). The concession about technology's real hold on actuality is promised for the energy and causality chapters (ch. 50–51).

**Critical note.** The assertion/statement analysis is the most original point. It locates in the grammar of the declarative sentence both spirit's legislating claim and the constraint that actuality places on it, which is more nuanced than Klages's usual polemic. The weak point is that the chapter's own claims are judgements. If every truth is "tendentious one-sidedness" measured by an unjudged "feeling of actuality", Klages owes an account of how his own theses (including "actuality is image") escape the same verdict. The appeal to *docta ignorantia* names the problem but does not solve it.

### Chapter 48 — Analysis of the Will to Deed (*Zerlegung des Tatwillens*) [627–639]

**Thesis.** The capacity for will in a living being rests not on the strength of an image's pull but on *privation without images*: "poverty of kind" combined with unbroken strength of impulse. Volition is a counter-urge, directed *against the present*, toward a non-existent that ought to be. That urge is the origin of future-consciousness, of *dynamic* time, and so of measurable, linear time and the time-object. The same urge is also the inexhaustible source of illusion.

**Argument.**
1. *What prepares volition.* If a strong image-pull were split, volition would fall back into drive or rise into ecstasy (union with happening). So the precondition is "privation and not plenitude, disturbance and not concord" [627]. Even violent emotion is not the "rupture in which spirit could nest" unless the counter-urge against the hindrance outweighs what moves [628]. The question from ch. 27, whether feelings can be graded by image-content, is answered yes [628]. Capacity for will comes from desensualisation or de-souling: the "negating" moment of need overstrained at the expense of "affirming" desire.
2. *Affect and irritability (appeal to language).* "Affect" equals excitability or irritability, which reflects *disturbability*, not depth. Irritability grows as I-hood grows, which is what the ideal of *ataraxia* hoped to counter [628–629]. Philosophers confused removing a deficiency with fulfilment, and delight in success with vital bliss. Will does not crown a feeling. It follows a rebound against disturbances of self-assertion. "In relation to the life-process, the I is reacting power and nothing but this" [629].
3. *Isolating effect.* Purposing seeks "the being of a non-being and consequently the non-being of a being". The willing I is moved not only from happening into being but "out of the existent into a non-existent" [629]. Example: a soldier does not notice his wound because he has "transformed himself wholly into the will to fend off the enemy". A being that was pure will would be more desensualised than the Buddha (black magic, ascetics hostile to undergoing). Everyday versions: the employer who sees only the stain on an applicant's collar, the engineer who reckons a waterfall in horsepower [629–630]. Pure willing, empty of images, would lose consciousness and stop willing. Its tendency points to "the death of consciousness … the shutting-down of all happening" [630].
4. *Spatial vs temporal not-yet.* A drive-goal is experienced as a *spatial* not-yet-presence, a disturbing Here to be overcome by movement. A will-goal is a thought-thing hovering as what *ought* to be. It would leave the present moment behind it, so it is a *temporal* not-yet-presence. Reflection on purposes must therefore grasp "the one and only direction of time … as the direction of a movement out of space" [630–631].
5. *Static past vs dynamic future.* The past (already grounded in earlier chapters) is the space-analogical distance between appearance-spaces: a phantasm pushed back into its own space by the present impression. The future is the track of an urge directed against the Now: a wish-image that would push the Now out into a never-experienced space. Both are rectilinear (only near/far, earlier/later). But the static view looks at a distance covered, while the dynamic view relates to a movement that lengthens it beyond the Now. "Only thereby does the concept of time become independent" [631].
6. *Derivation of the time-object.* The Now detaches from the space of intuition because volition aims at the not-yet. Space-opposed dynamic time replaces space-analogous time and becomes "the foundation of measurable time and consequently of the time-object itself" [632]. Elements: (i) dimly experienced actuality-time (passing); (ii) the standing space of intuition, which gives the static past; (iii) willing, which makes the present a "prison" and so detaches the Now. The anticipation breaks against what exists, so the past-line becomes time "in itself", a *ray*. This answers ch. 5's question why measuring time needs the spatial straight line: "direction as such" is the willing being's relatedness to purpose, seen through the space of intuition. Conceived time is "an objectity of willing" [632–633]. Adding space gives the four-dimensional manifold (note 14).
7. *Confirmation from self-observation and history.* A planner walks faster or gets up to pace about. Someone remembering slows down or sits [633]. Contemplative antiquity thought about space and motion, not speeds. Christian voluntarism gave acceleration (Galileo) and ever finer chronometers [633–634].
8. *Recollection depends on will.* Deliberate recollection alternates between groping back along the time-line and pressing against the Now. Failure to recollect is a torment of the will's impotence (forgotten names, silence in an examination: no answer is worse than a wrong one) [634]. So "the lowest occasioning ground of reflection-on-the-past must be sought in the conditions of reflection-on-the-future" [634]. Clear past-consciousness and clear future-consciousness go together. "Primitive" peoples have near-sighted past-apprehension, with ancestors made present rather than past. The "historical sense" grows as the counterpart of planning. The verb was originally a word of process, not time [635]. Finding existence is finding the Now, because it is finding *resistance*. The I-am makes the I-was possible because it is kindled by what, made conscious, would be the I-will [635–636].
9. *Linguistic evidence.* The future tense as a command ("You will present yourself"), the Latin future for wishing, the French future for probability, English *will/shall* [636]. The military command and the starter's pistol are a "symbol of the act itself and of its dagger-thrust-like effect". They cut the pulse of happening into a Now [636–637]. The less one hopes and wills, the more the present returns to the "undelimitable wave". Christianity's deification of the will gave the time-chopping law-language of science and "Time is money!" [637].
10. *Will as source of illusion.* "Expectation is the cause of deception." The will, satisfied only by success that it imagines in advance, is deceived about the course of events and above all about its own ability. "The optimism of success of men of will", above all programmatic world-improvers, may break mankind's neck [637–638]. "Expectation is the cause of illusion of the senses": Schiller's "Die Erwartung" shows mild cases. The main source of hallucination is not lively phantasms but "the paleness and bloodlessness of the sense-space of a soul whose power of beholding has been extinguished by perpetual aiming-at" [638–639]. Klages closes with Oscar Wilde: action is "the last refuge of those who do not know how to dream" [639].

**Key terms.** *Tatwille* (will to deed); *Entsinnlichung* / *Entseelung* (desensualisation, de-souling); *Gegendrang* (counter-urge); *Reizbarkeit* (irritability); spatial vs temporal not-yet-presence; static vs dynamic time; *Jetzt* (the Now) as detached by willing; "objectity of willing"; expectation as the source of illusion.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter delivers the genesis of time-consciousness promised in Book I (ch. 3–5) and Book III's account of recollection (ch. 38, which ch. 41 also took up). It completes the account of the "I-was" and "I-am" begun in ch. 9 and 12. It prepares ch. 49's question of how volition becomes *action* (the counter-urge, inhibition), and supplies the anthropology (desensualisation, the "men of will") that Book V's image-doctrine sets against.

**Critical note.** Deriving the *direction* of time from the structure of purposive anticipation is a substantial phenomenological thesis, close to Heidegger's priority of the future, and the walking/remembering observations make it concrete. The vulnerable step is the claim that animals, and drive-goals generally, involve only *spatial* not-yet. That is asserted from the definition of drive (ch. 45), not shown. The hibernation and migration rhythms Klages himself cites look like temporal anticipation without purpose.

### Chapter 49 — Volition and Action (*Wollung und Handlung*) [640–678]

**Thesis.** The will does not drive. It *steers*, by inhibiting. "Willing-to-do = willing-not-to-let-happen" (*Tunwollen = Nichtgeschehenlassenwollen*) [641]. The act splits the impulse into a *double urge*: a driving part-force from life, and a counter-impulse ("the universal inhibitory motive-spring", meaning the will) that holds the urge to the purpose's direction. A long action is possible only because *wish-states* lie between drive and volition, and because the resolve sets up a disposition that keeps renewing the wish. Will-capable wishing is at bottom *aversion* (privation, insufficiency), not attraction. The world-picture that purpose-consciousness needs (finitude, countability, rectilinearity, thinghood, persistence, lawfulness, causality) is the will's own, and the will is "a will to destruction" [673].

**Argument.**
1. *Scope.* The previous abstraction from the conditions of realisation now has to be undone. How does the quality-less urge, which is related to the goal, actually *act* toward it? Inner action (mental arithmetic) and outer action (lifting weights) are both movement. Thinking is felt as an "inner movement", even lying on a couch. Only possible volitions are examined, on the assumption that the conditions of failure will follow from those of success [640–641].
2. *The basic equation and the ship simile.* The act fixes the *direction* of an urge to move. It does not generate the urge. The command is no more a driving force "than the holding of a course belongs among the causes of the walker's steps" [641]. The will is the rudder and impulses are the oarsmen or wind. The best image is the self-steering Phaeacian ships, since drive-impulses also steer themselves. Three reasons for the "will drives" illusion: (a) willing-to-do is identified with movements, yet willing to sit still or be silent (Tell refusing to bow to Gessler's hat) is also willing-to-do, often costlier; (b) the vital movement is the inseparable "vehicle" of willing, as the builder is said to build the house his masons build; (c) the purpose has no intuitive data, so the urge is canalised into a straight line, like a canalised river that reaches the sea faster. The canal walls are not causes of the flow [642]. Mechanics will be shown to derive from the experience of willing-to-do (ch. 50–51) [642–643].
3. *Linguistic confirmation.* German calls feelings aroused, surging, blazing, unbridled. The will is called strong, tenacious, steadfast, iron, steely, unwavering: words for a force "that thwarts being-moved". Hard and cold are akin to will, soft and warm contrary to it. *Zweck* originally meant the centre of the target, hit only by suppressing every wavering of the hand [643–644]. Aiming and visual fixation show the same thing: willedness is the *fettering* of a movement to one direction. "Steering of movement is and remains the vital achievement of the willing act" [644]. But how can spirit, with no forces, steer?
4. *Limits and the near end.* Just as the will to work breaks against spirit, the will to deed breaks against actuality. A resolve needs belief that it can be carried out (one can wish but not resolve to fetch the moon) [644–645]. Every step of will, however remote its final end (founding a party, reshaping the state, making mankind happy), reaches no further than "the grasp of my hand or the call of my mouth". The telephone crank, the lever of a "machine of murder", the signature on a declaration of war all show this [645–646]. Hence the doer is *always* tied to the local, unrepeatable particular. "The doer as doer is never a Platonist, but invariably a 'materialist'" [646–647]. The chain of means is reckoned by relating thought. The act of will produces only the nearest link, through the body.
5. *The double urge.* Example: blasting a boulder uses the natural force "explosion" against the natural force "boulder". Inwardly in the same way, the urge left after the split is "a double urge, divided within itself", an urge plus a counter-urge forcing it back into the aimed direction [647–648]. This only restates the fact-boundness of thinking: knowledge of what is takes the form of counter-impulses. Redefinition: motive-springs (*Triebfedern*) are "drives to which the counter-impulse in question is attached", and "the will itself [is] the universal inhibitory motive-spring" [648]. Objection that this still gives spirit motor power: no. A weir is a mechanical force, but if the vital cause is the *pull of a goal*, knowledge of the resistance of facts can occasion inhibitory impulses. Klages admits this touches "the unresolved, and probably unresolvable, riddle of the coupling of spirit with life" [648]. The judgement does to an impulse, on the occasion of an impression, what the act of will does spontaneously. So "logical compulsion is … the consequence of an inner action" [648–649].
6. *Will is single, drives are many.* Counter-impulses as such are animal (the dog: meat against fear of punishment), but they come in polar pairs. The inhibitory side of every motive-spring is "one and the selfsame will" [649]. Proof from the reverse: no drive could resist *all* drives without abolishing itself. The will, which is not vital in origin, can deny every drive even to death: ascetics, hunger strikers [649–650]. Raising the arm: a vital cause moves it, and an equally vital cause of *inhibition* blocks deviation and overshoot. Purposiveness shows itself as "an expressively unmistakable narrowing of the range of variation" of natural movement [650].
7. *Body and tool.* One's own organs are not means in the strict sense. Their use rests on inner-bodily feeling, and perfection lies in returning to the animal's natural mode. We could grasp the nail better without thought, but driving it into the wall needs spiritual experience. Only the tool is a true means [650–651]. Walking from A to B needs one initial act, and skill does the rest automatically (cf. ch. 25 on habit). Hypnotic and post-hypnotic suggestion shows intricate purposive action done unconsciously [651–653]. Yet the final end modifies even the first step: putting a foot forward to demonstrate a stride differs from setting off for B [652].
8. *Means-end structure and the mechanising abstraction.* Example: ascending a difficult peak, with its hierarchy of partial ends (wall, chimney, crevasse, crampons, rope). The factual conditions of each step are "nothing other than its mechanical causes", so end-consciousness produces an abstraction opposite to the Platonist one. It keeps only what bears on compulsory sequence (tone = longitudinal vibration) [653]. "The theorist is blind above all to space and time … the practical man is blind to the world of qualities". Hertz's *Principles of Mechanics* is quoted: knowledge is for foreseeing in order to act, and agrees with things in only one relation [653–654]. The concept of cause is the will's tool for destroying actuality "with the expenditure of a minimum of insight".
9. *Wish (Wunsch) as the intermediate layer.* There is no drive to climb icy peaks, nor to power, money or happiness for mankind [654]. A motive-spring unites a constant inhibition with a particular impulse-generation that lacks a drive's independence. Its conscious form is the *wish*, unknown to animals (note 18 praises Sigwart's account of wishing as the most perceptive treatment of the will). Etymology: West Germanic *wünschen*, not *wollen*, served magic (*Verwünschung*, *Wünschelrute*) [655]. Rational wishing equals magical wishing minus belief in magical effect: a consciousness of present unattainability combined with striving toward the goal. Wishing is the source of all many-membered volitions once it is joined by belief in mechanical attainability [655–656]. The "reversal of poles" that separated humans from animals subordinated near-experience to far-experience (roaming, faring forth). There is "no fact-relatable impulse without the cooperation of phantasms of the goal" [656]. Wishing is not a separate feeling but "a quite peculiar side of all feelings of spiritual beings": fear wishes to avoid, hope that the hoped-for comes, longing to bridge the gap [656–657]. Fantasy works without consciousness (dreams), so a vital *aiming* at fulfilment-phantasms is posited. Klages admits it may look like "a dubious duplication" [657–658]. Evidence of human "centrifugality": upright gait, the hand, the gesture of pointing (no animal has it), and the historical shift in writing from retrograde to progressive line direction, interpreted as the "promethean" backward turn (contemplation) against the "heraclean" forward drive (will) [658].
10. *Objections and the It-wish.* Does the urge into the distance contradict the will's tie to the nearest, its inhibitory nature, self-mastery, and the thesis that will is negating? Klages admits a gap in his account of will-capable wishing, to be filled below [658–659]. The intellectual worker's real motive was not fatigue but the *wish* to regain strength, or else the sunshine, in which case one must also explain the motive-spring of his *self-deception* [660]. Terms: the effective motive-spring is an **It-wish** (*Es-Wunsch*), and conscious wishes are **I-wishes** (*Ich-Wünsche*). Most I-wishes are products of hidden It-wishes [660]. (The translation garbles this sentence. The German says most I-wishes are outcomes of the interplay of quite different It-wishes.) Klages holds that no one has ever climbed a dangerous peak for the view. The real motives are proving one's courage (Nietzsche: "to mock his trembling knees"), outdoing rivals, "conquering" the mountain (Everest), curiosity, or the drive to know [660–661].
11. *Will-capable wishing is aversion.* Every action aims at "the non-being of a being", so it is rooted in privation, never in overflowing fullness. In will-capable wishing, striving *toward* is subordinated to striving *away from* the undesired. Fulfilment-phantasms become motives only by turning into aversion to phantasms of privation [661]. The will to work shows the gladdening image (through its own self-negation). The will to deed shows "only the repulsion of the painful image" [661]. Nietzsche unmasked the ideals but did not see that "the will to power is a will to destruction" [661]. Klages refers to his *Grundlagen der Charakterkunde* and to Vol. I [411]. Negative feelings (fear, hatred, envy) are akin to will, positive ones (love, veneration) contrary to it. "Self-assertion feelings" and "self-surrender feelings": "at the moment of acting — love does not act!" (note 19) [662]. Mountaineering again: the wish for proof rests on doubts of one's worth, the wish to know on being troubled by ignorance. Petrarch on Mont Ventoux sought nature *praeterita oblivisci … et praesentia non videre* [662–663]. The Icarus wish did not produce aeroplanes. Aversion to the bodily Here and "envy of eagles" did, and the flying machine killed the "soul-bird" of dream-flight. "The distance cannot be 'conquered', but it can indeed be killed" [663–664].
12. *Lyrical-metaphysical interlude (assertion, explicitly marked as a digression).* "What we behold things as, that is what we do to them". German *antun* means both to harm and to clothe [664]. Negation leads to blindness, blindness to will, and will destroys what blindness cannot behold. "Will is ever-renewed life-murder, and its symbol is suicide" [665]. The soul-forsaken body makes the world a concept. The body-forsaken soul makes it a phantom.
13. *Resolve as disposition.* The willer "always chooses an evil", the least of several [665]. Will wrestles down vital resistances, either to interrupt movement or to continue it (going on despite fatigue, or keeping off a detour despite a sense of direction). The watchword is always "hold out!" [665–666]. Objection: if each step needs a renewed vital wish, is determinism back? Reply: "will-capacity is indeed founded on wish-capacity, but it is only the resolve that establishes the disposition that brings it about that wishing renews itself so often, until the … final purpose is reached" [666]. The resolve engraves "the barrier of not-letting-happen" on wishing, as judgement engraves "irrevocable being" on impression. Without it, Calypso or fatigue would win.
14. *The detour diagram (Fig. 7).* A drive or wish moves along a spatial direction AZ. Purpose (EZ) is "merely the simile of a direction", which can be pursued through breakpoints at any angle to it. The chamois can leap or run along a ravine but cannot fetch a log to bridge it, and lacks "intelligence" only because it lacks will [667]. Each breakpoint is an act of will serving Z, or a hidden Z′. Drives and wishes follow rhythms. Intention persists for decades and even beyond death (testaments) [668]. Why resolves fail: change of mind, a better route, changed circumstances, flagging will. **Energy of will** is the resolve's capacity to make the wish-renewing disposition last. **Ease of will** is how steerable living movements are [669]. A middling energy plus "life-thin poverty of kind" can beat great energy. "The world-historical doers … belong … on the side of the will-gifted cripples of the heart" [670]. All "talents" share a constitution hostile to life. The experienced conflict of driving and inhibiting part-forces is the source of the concepts of force and mechanical cause (handed on to ch. 50) [670].
15. *The "intelligence of the will": the implicit world-picture of purpose-consciousness.* Seven mutually required notions [670–672]: (i) **finitude** of the world (an infinite world makes purposes Sisyphean, as in Christian creation and eschatology, and in physics' finite world-process); (ii) **countability**; (iii) **rectilinearity** (the straight line as the symbol of the I's direction toward its goal, which explains its puzzling definition; note 20 distinguishes the actuality of direction from "direction as such"); (iv) **thinghood** (number's element is the one, and the localised one is the thing); (v) **persistence**; (vi) **lawfulness** (the wooden ball behaves predictably); (vii) **causality**, for which the others are "the pedestal". Only East Asian thought is an independent "tree of knowledge". Buddhist, Platonic, medieval-Christian and modern-physical thought are branches of one tree whose driving force, announced in ch. 1, "is called the will, and the will … is a will to destruction" [672–673].
16. *How can misunderstanding succeed?* Repeatability needs dead time and quality-free properties. But the world's *pulsing* really shows temporal and spatial similarities, so something actual answers to spirit's cuts, as the dead wood of a table answers to the living wood of the tree [673–674]. This is *displaceability*: things cannot be isolated, but natural contexts can be shattered and rebuilt as contrivances, like a machine out of lifeless parts. "Objecthood and lifelessness are convertible concepts" [674]. Ideological abstraction declares happening null, mechanistic abstraction declares images null [674–675]. Making a table involves abstracting (cutting), combining (assembling) and the "idea" (purposive design). "Conceiving (= objectification) is one and the same thing as mechanizing" [675]. The required over-wakefulness presupposes "the blinding of the eye of the soul". *Natura non vincitur nisi parendo* [676]. Lawfulness is the reverse side of arbitrary will: without trusting that the pencil has fixed properties, I could not even form the intention to write. Kant's cinnabar point is extended: without fixed properties one could not *will* anything about cinnabar. "'Objects' as such are rule-obedient means of possible purposes and nothing besides" [676–677].
17. *Summary propositions I–V, parallel to ch. 45's* [677–678]. (I) Volition is felt as self-activity and lacks the drive's *vis a tergo*. (II) Impulse and purpose are separate: the impulse has no goal of its own, and the resolve produces no movement. (III) The quality of the split impulse evaporates and the resolve binds the remaining intensity in place of the *vis a fronte*. Action bends the impulse to the "laws" of the "world of facts" through a double urge. "The strength of the will equals the magnitude of the expenditure required to force down the intensity of drive-impulses." (IV) Drives are rhythmic, and will stays fixed on a goal "for months, years, decades". (V) Drive-goals are reached pathically, by allurement. Action is reactive and negating, and the common element of all motives is "the breaking of resistances". "There are countless drives; the will, by contrast, stands in the singular!" [678]

**Key terms.** *Tunwollen = Nichtgeschehenlassenwollen*; steering (*Steuerung*) vs driving; *Doppeldrang* (double urge: driving part-force + inhibiting part-force); will as "universal inhibitory motive-spring" (*Triebfeder*, rendered variously as mainspring, motive-spring, motive-force); *Wunsch* (wish), rational vs magical; *Es-Wunsch* / *Ich-Wunsch*; *Fernantrieb* (far-impulse); self-assertion vs self-surrender feelings; resolve (*Entschluß*) as wish-renewing disposition; energy of will vs ease of will; nearest end vs final end (Z, Z′); "intelligence of the will"; displaceability; objecthood = lifelessness.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter completes the analysis of the will begun in ch. 41. It realises ch. 43's promise that spirit acts "this side of" causality (by inhibiting, not by adding force), and gives content to ch. 43's motive-springs and will-dispositions (energy, ease). It generalises ch. 46's "judgement seals apprehension" into "resolve seals wish". It derives the categories of the thing-world (Book I's critique of thing, number, line and law) from purpose-consciousness, and hands on directly to ch. 50 (*Willensleistung und Energieerhaltung*) and ch. 51 (*Von der Ursache und der Kraft*). The wish layer and "far-experience" anticipate Book V's nearness/distance chapters (62–65).

**Critical note.** The steering-by-inhibition model is the book's best answer to the interaction problem. It explains the phenomenology of effort, the vocabulary of firmness, and the "narrowing of variation" in purposive movement without giving spirit energy. But it only moves the problem: an inhibition that redirects a movement is still a causal difference in the course of the movement, and Klages concedes the "probably unresolvable riddle" [648]. The thesis that all will-capable wishing is aversion is reached by redescribing every counter-example (Petrarch, the climber) as a flight from insufficiency. That makes it unfalsifiable. The slide from "purpose needs fixed properties" to "the will is a will to destruction" is rhetorical escalation, not inference.

### Arc of Ch. 41–49

Book IV turns the metaphysics of Books I–III into a theory of agency. Its guiding claim, stated in ch. 41 and confirmed in ch. 49, is that spirit shows itself most fully in the *willing* I, and that its "act" is everywhere the same timeless, splitting intervention. What distinguishes perceiving from willing is *which side* of the bipolar life-process is split: the impression/image side, which yields subject and object, or the impulse side, which yields willing subject and end. Klages builds on the doctrine of the I as the pivot of spirit and life (ch. 9, 26, 38–39), on the act as a response to a "life-convulsion" (ch. 31), and on the actuality of images (ch. 23). He asks for credit on a theory of sensation and embodiment not yet given, and says so [583].

The First Part (ch. 41–43) clears the ground. Ch. 41 shows that deliberative "conflict of motives" models presuppose the will, and that the end is *generated* by volition. Ch. 42 separates *necessity* (the real, living course of happening) from *lawfulness* (spirit's command-form). Klages argues that determinism and indeterminism share one root in a commanding will, wraps this in a polemical genealogy of "law" from Stoicism through Kant to "Jahwism", and against it sets the archaic fate-consciousness in which necessity is a blessed "must". Ch. 43 gives a two-tier account of conditioning. Impulses are suffered and necessary. Commands are spirit's own. Motives are only the purpose seen from its "why" side, and the problem of will becomes the genesis of *motive-springs* (*Triebfedern*, impulses referable to fact) out of *drives*.

The Second Part (ch. 44–47) supplies the vital material and shows what the act does to it. After a polemic against utilitarian teleology (ch. 44), ch. 45 builds a positive theory of drive. A drive is the vital cause of movement: a pull of non-present images combined with bodily privation, goal-directed without representing the goal. Ch. 46 then gives the central analysis. The will splits off the impulse's *specific character* and keeps its *pull*, which the command directs. So the purpose is a thought that fixes a goal in happening, as the object fixes something in appearance. Several results follow: self-reflection is first occasioned by willing (*volo ergo sum*), every judgement is a concealed prohibition ("the positive judgement is the original negation"), truth and error belong only to judgement, and error lies in interest acting on a weakened *sense of actuality*. Ch. 47 turns that sense of actuality into the *will to truth*, which contradicts itself because judgement both serves and betrays the pull of actuality. It sets the image-maker's "will to work" (the self-negation of willing) against the "will to deed".

The Third Part, as far as ch. 49, shows what *acting* is. Ch. 48 grounds the capacity for will in *imageless privation*, makes volition a counter-urge against the present, and derives from it dynamic time, the detachment of the Now, linear measurable time and future-oriented recollection. That fulfils the promises of Book I on time (ch. 3–5) and Book III on memory (ch. 38). Ch. 49 states the steering-by-inhibition model (*Tunwollen = Nichtgeschehenlassenwollen*), adds the intermediate layer of *wishes* (It-wishes vs I-wishes) and the *resolve* as a disposition that renews the wish, and ends by deriving the categories of the thing-world from purpose-consciousness: finitude, number, straight line, thing, persistence, law, cause. So the critique of the "thing" in Book I is finally traced to the *will*. This sets up ch. 50–51, which are to show that energy-conservation and mechanical cause are projections of the experience of willed effort.

Open questions and tensions handed forward:
(1) The interaction problem. Klages insists that spirit adds no force and only splits and inhibits, yet that it produces "immeasurable effects" in appearance (ch. 43). He admits in ch. 49 that the coupling of spirit and life is "probably unresolvable" [648].
(2) The status of the I. It is "nothing but" a geometrical locus (ch. 43), yet it issues commands that fall under no concept of necessity. This verges on either determinism or regress.
(3) Klages's own discourse. If every judgement is a hidden negation of actuality and every truth a "tendentious one-sidedness" (ch. 46–47), his own theses need the "indicative" exemption he grants only to the deep will to truth.
(4) A tension in the theory of motive. Ch. 49's "urge into the distance" and centrifugal wishing sits uneasily with the claim that will-capacity rests on privation and aversion, not on the lure of images. Klages answers it by making the wish a motive only once it turns into aversion [661], which saves the thesis by redescription.
(5) What the will replaces. Ch. 46 says the I takes the place of the drive's *vis a tergo* (direction from privation) once the *vis a fronte* falls away [607]. Ch. 49 says volition *lacks* the *vis a tergo* and that the resolve takes the place of the *vis a fronte* [677–678]. Since ch. 45 gave direction to the image (I) and need to the *vis a tergo* (II), the two formulations do not fit together without adjustment.
(6) Unevenness in register. Careful phenomenology (the dilemma about motives, effort, the vocabulary of firmness, planning and remembering) sits beside asserted typologies (gender, sexuality, "cripples of the heart"), an antisemitic genealogy of law, and cultural-apocalyptic polemic. The arguments rarely depend on the polemic. The guide should mark these passages as assertion.
(7) Translation. *Triebfeder* appears as "mainspring", "motive-spring" and "motive-force", and *Antrieb*, *Regung* and *Drang* are not always kept apart. Check the German at key points.

*Covers the end of Book IV (Doctrine of the Will, ch. 50–54, Vol. II) and the opening of Book V, Part 1, Section 1 "Spirit and Soul" (*Geist und Seele*, ch. 55–56, Vol. III).*

### Chapter 50 — The Performance of Will and the Conservation of Energy (*Willensleistung und Energieerhaltung*) [679–690]

**Thesis.** Every earlier theory of will either explains the will away or turns drives into will, and none can square the felt efficacy of willing with the law of conservation of energy. Klages's own theory removes the antinomy. The act of will creates and adds no energy. It splits the drive-impulse (*Triebantrieb*) into an unmeasurable quality and a kindless, measurable intensity, and harnesses that intensity to a purpose. The energy balance therefore stays unchanged, while something real but unmeasurable, the destruction of quality, has happened.

**Argument.**
1. *Two groups of theories, both abolishing the will* [679–681]. Group A (associationism) treats the feeling of spontaneity as an illusion arising from expected sequels; it is shaped "from the outset" by a sidelong glance at energy conservation. Group B turns will back into drive: Schopenhauer on the grand scale, Wundt crudely (a volition as "an affect that concludes with a pantomimic movement", *Grundriß*, p. 216). This is refuted by ridicule: a scholar would have to work up an affect each time he reached for his pen. Lipps gets credit for proving that actions presuppose prior impulse-movements, that deriving instinct from earlier voluntary acts is circular, and that the feeling of self-active exertion cannot be built from sensations of tension. But Lipps doubles "striving" and "willing" and so completes the "voluntarizing" of living stirrings. The shared hidden premise is atomism: building wholes from "elements".
2. *The antinomy* [681–682]. Modern theorists are all determinists, and the belief in lawfulness has taken the numerical form of the conservation law. So either (a) the will adds energy or measurably alters (for example steers) a movement, and conservation fails; or (b) it does nothing, and the experience of acting becomes an inexplicable self-deception. A waking intentional arm-movement would then be no different from a reflex or a sneeze. The examples of speech (the purpose of communicating "causes" the innervation, or the speaker is a speech-automaton) and of believing one makes the moon rise sharpen the dilemma. Monism, dualism, parallelism, and the real/phenomenal distinction are all rejected as evasions.
3. *The dilemma survives any weakening of the law* [682–685]. Klages sets aside doubts about the law's status (hypothesis or law, closed universe or not) and the experiments of Rubner, Atwater and Mayer. Using a frictionless pendulum and a crane lifting 10 kg through 10 m (100 kg-m), he states the law in its simplest form. When a worker carries the block, his resolve and intermediate volitions either add nothing or reduce the calories needed. The "directing function" and "triggering" (*Auslösung*) escapes fail, because steering and triggering also cost energy (note 22 adds that even work-free steering would leave the felt effort of will unexplained). The indeterminist's reply, that energy may flow into the cosmos wherever wills act, is turned into a reductio: the willing organism would then be a perpetuum mobile of the first kind.
4. *Turn to a critique of the concept of energy* [685–687]. Measurement never reaches appearance. In the crane case, lifting wood and lifting iron are "the same work" but intuitively neither identical nor equal. Weight and measured distance are not found in actuality, and the "propertied thing" block-of-wood is a fiction against the appearance of the block (cashing out ch. 5–6). So it makes no difference whether we measure a machine or a will. One cannot ask what work the mirror-image of a hand does on the mirror-image of a bowl.
5. *That is not enough* [687]. It would leave unexplained why a will moving "amid sheer unrealities" produces intuitable effects, and why energetics succeeds. The vital root of the concepts of force and energy is the lived intensity of the drive-impulse, just as the lived rhythm of happening was the root of the belief in laws (ch. 42).
6. *Positive solution* [688–690; Fig. 8]. The act of will separates the drive-impulse's quality or kind from its intensity or strength. The kindless remainder further splits into a driving and a reining component. That remainder is exactly what physics measures as force and energy. Parallel: perception "kills transformation" and leaves the thing as slag; will "kills the images" and leaves force. The will neither adds an impulse nor subtracts one. It "tears [the impulse] loose from the embrace of appearance". In vector terms, the vectorial drive-impulse splits at P into unmeasurable quality and scalar intensity. Coupled at once to a purpose, the intensity becomes a vectorial volition of the same strength, so energy content is unchanged. Spirit can therefore do the deepest destructive "work" on life without touching the mechanical equivalents.
7. *Corollaries* [689–690]. No law is a law of happening; laws say only what would happen under isolable conditions. Conservation forbids spending more energy than exists but says nothing about whether, when or to what end it is spent. The "voluntaristic superstition" that willing generates happening is the counterpart of the "logical superstition" that apprehension generates the image (Book III). The felt self-activity of effort is spirit's presence showing as a narrowing of the course of movement against an inhibiting counter-urge. The feeling of exertion itself is vital.

**Key terms.** *Willensleistung* (performance or achievement of will); *Triebantrieb* (drive-impulse, vectorial); *Qualität/Art* versus *Stärke/Intensität* (kind versus kindless strength); *Wollung* (volition); *Auslösung* ("triggering", rejected); *Willkürbewegung* (voluntary movement); *Spaltung* (splitting by the spiritual act).

**Contribution to the whole.** Completes the Book IV model of volition (ch. 45–49: will as splitting of the drive-impulse, with a driving and an inhibiting component) by testing it against physics. Supplies the claim that force is "will minus quality", which ch. 51 develops into a genealogy of mechanical force and cause. Parallels the Book III result that the act of apprehension does not produce the image (ch. 38).

**Critical note.** The strongest move is turning the antinomy around: if the will only separates and redirects an existing intensity, conservation is untouched. That is a neat compatibilism of a kind. The weak point is "redirects". A volition "coupled to the thought of purpose" that turns a drive's direction toward an ideal goal is exactly the "steering" Klages had just said costs energy. He also gives no account of how a temporally unextended act achieves a real separation in a physical process without exchanging energy, so the solution is asserted by stipulating the will's nature.

### Chapter 51 — On Cause and Force (*Von der Ursache und der Kraft*) [691–730]

**Thesis.** The modern concept of cause is a vague generalisation of the modern concept of force. That force is a "de-forced force" (*entkräftete Kraft*), obtained by projecting the lived experience of willing, minus its vital vehicle, onto a world first broken into lifeless thing-points. Galileo's punctual, future-directed velocity and Newton's extensionless mass carry out this projection. They destroy happening (by concepts of being) and matter (by spaceless force), and lead to formalist scepticism (Hume, Kant, relativism) and to a technology of destruction. Causal thinking is a mirrored volition (*eine zurückgespiegelte Wollung*) serving the will to power.

**Argument.**
1. *Historical relativity of causality* [691–692]. Aristotle's four "causes" (*Metaph.* Δ) mostly do not match the modern concept; only the efficient cause does, and only remotely. The Stoics founded the modern concept together with the world-law. Cause grew out of force, originally a power that produces an effect. Hume's attack on "productive" causes confirms that this was the received meaning.
2. *The vital concept of force* [692–694]. Its source is the impulse-experience. The pull of images meets the body's resistance, and one's own effort (raising an arm, lifting a weight, stopping a ball) gives graded strength. A force built on this would differ in time from its effect, be inseparable from its direction (comparable only within kinds), and be purely active against a purely passive mobile, with the two polarly belonging together. Examples: Empedocles' Love and Strife (incommensurable forces), the Pythagorean table of opposites, Aristotle's unmoved mover and his complaint that Platonic Ideas move nothing. For life-bound thinking rest is polarly different from motion, not motion at zero velocity. Matter and body are the resting, passive pole (note 23).
3. *The Galilean revolution* [694–696]. The principle of persistence makes rest a special case of motion. Force becomes the cause of a *change of velocity*, not of motion.
