It is "the one secondary property without whose co-presence the primordial property would sink down into the abyss of mere possibilities". Aristotle would be right about this if he had recognized that matter is independent rather than mere potentiality for immaterial form. Matter as experienced is *intensive and gradable* (plasticine, fluids, gases). Etymology: *weich* (soft) from *weichen* (to yield), *hart* (hard) cognate with κρατύς. Atomism is a hopeless attempt to quantize the continuity of intensity. The two camps err in opposite directions [962]: the materiologist thinks something can be found without experienced intuitability, the ideologist thinks shapes can be found without things already found.
7. *Thing as self-asserting place; I as source of identity* [964–967]. Within the now-here, the here bears the primordial object and the now bears the primordial *concept*. That is why every cult of the concept is paired with a cult of shape. The thing is "a self-asserting place" (*ein sichbehauptender Ort*). The I is the extensionless pivot of the personal I and the "ever-repeated deed of self-assertion"; since the primal act bounds body from body, the bearer of spirit must itself be corporeal. All vital working is assimilation of like to like, and the likeness that lets spirit set the boundary is not that of soul and image but the like disturbability of the soul-body and the image's body: the impenetrability of both [966]. Spirit forms the primordial object by shooting "a copy of the I-point" into the corporeality of the appearance. The object's fictive sameness is a "reflex of the real sameness of the subject". Early, anthropomorphic thought therefore made *persons* of objects. As spirit gains power it bores the bare I-point into actuality, to the point where persons can doubt that persons exist. What spirit gains is *possession*, on the model of the fish on the hook and the bird in the cage, "and every living thing definitively by killing it". Contents of experience are "nailed" onto the spaceless plane of objects of thought: *Stat crux dum volvitur orbis*. These are wishes for de-temporalization.
8. *Existence* [967–968]. *Ex-sistere* means to step forth. The existent is supposed to be both being and actuality, a fusion of timelessness with real time. Klages resolves this for the thing: thingness is fictive and corporeality is actual. For the personal I, the fusion has "in fact taken place" (deferred to ch. 61).
9. *Two sub-theses on the existential judgement* [968–973]. (a) No object of thought exists without the prior finding of the thing. (b) Doubts about existence are always settled by appeal to things. One's own body plays a double role: bearer of the I, and a thing among things that "becomes the tormentor of the I" in pain. The I-consciousness "kindles" on the consciousness of its self-moving corporeality (cf. ch. 12, 46). On recognition versus identity (cf. ch. 34): Odysseus' dog recognizes by similarity, while the nurse *identifies* by the scar. Corporeality is the last link in every chain of identification, even in inference to processes on the sun from a deflected magnetic needle. Examples of existential appeals to touch: Heraclitus' common world of the waking is a world of things, not of appearances. Then come the desert mirage, the stick bent in water, converging street walls, and the Zöllner illusion corrected by a ruler. Polemic against relativism: even a contraction hypothesis presupposes the rigid body, and a thought fixed on visible appearance turns its object into "rules of the game".

**Key terms.** *Urakt* (primal act of spirit, which sets the boundary). *Grenze* vs *Gestalt* (boundary, which has no shape, vs shape/form). *Körperding* (body-thing). *das objektiv Uridentische* and *sachlicher Urgegenstand* (the thing as the first identity). *Ureigenschaft* (primordial property, a minimal field of impenetrability). *Jetztgestalt* (now-shape). *Selbstbehauptung* (self-assertion, both of the thing as place and of the I as act). *Ichpunkt* (the extensionless I-point). *Wiedererkennen* vs *Identifizieren* (animal recognition vs human identification). *Existenzialurteil*.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter gives a vital ground for the account of the I as self-assertion (ch. 9) and of the finding of the I by way of the thing (ch. 12) and for the willing I in ch. 46. It turns the "doubled here" of ch. 58 into the point where spirit enters. It prepares ch. 60's polemic about matter and ch. 61's derivation of the One and Being from the thing and the I. It also sets the pattern of every later critique of "ideologism" (cult of shape/Idea) paired with "materiologism" (cult of the atom).

**Critical note.** The analysis of identity through change of shape (snowman, ash) is acute, and so is the case that boundary is not shape. Both are good reasons against Democritean and Platonic identity-by-form. But the core move is a metaphor. Spirit "shoots a copy of the I-point" into the body. The thing's sameness is a "reflex" of the I's. Klages can say both that the I is *found* by way of one's own body as a thing (968–969) and that the thing's sameness is a reflex of the I's, because he separates the order of finding from the order of grounding. But the grounding claim rests on the I's "real sameness" as an act identically repeated, and that is asserted here (and only defended in ch. 61), not shown. The anti-relativity paragraph proves less than it seems: that measurement presupposes a rod rigid "for an empirical now" is something relativity itself grants, so it tells against no physical theory, only against a "relativism" that Klages simply equates with fixation on visual appearance.

### Chapter 60 — Matter and the World of Appearance (*Materie und Erscheinungswelt*) [974–1000]

**Thesis.** Matter cannot be derived from the thing or the point. In the terms of the science of life, matter is "the placed-determinateness of the transformation of appearance" (*die Ortsbestimmtheit der Erscheinungswandlung*), the "receiving-place of images". It stands to the image (happening) as place stands to space, or as the receptive feminine stands to the begetting masculine. "Pure" appearance, as the dream shows, would be placeless and so would lose identity and existence. The original seat of disturbance (*Urstörungsstelle*), and so the precondition of apprehension and judgement, is the experience of resistance. Its corporeality yields the grammatical subject and its strength yields the predicate.

**Argument.**
1. *Underivability of matter* [974–975]. "Substrate of motion" is correct but not enough, because mechanical motion itself still needs deriving. Matter is reached only by way of the *body*, which is "the appearance-capable site of happening". Starting from things leads to two absurdities. Things cannot act on one another, so such matter would be inert, and inert matter is nothing. And separate corpuscles lack anything to bind them into a body. (The English is garbled at 974–975, "der sie mortar".)
2. *Boscovich* [975]. His force-points with force-spheres, attraction turning into repulsion at close range, explain impenetrability without contact. Metaphysically, though, the spheres are "the ghostly form in which the thing makes a last effort to smuggle itself into the world of happening". The point swells into a volume. Making the thing-point actual costs the reality of the continuum (n. 37). That modern mechanics ends in "equations of condition" proves that physical judgements cannot reach matter.
3. *The formula* [975–977]. Sensed and beheld, impenetrability and appearance, are polar. Neither reduces to the other and neither appears without the other. Matter is the placed-determinateness of the transformation of appearance. Vital motion is a sub-case of transformation, and mechanical motion is what is left of it for detached thought. A **digression on sex polarity** follows, stated as assertion. Matter/place is receptive and feminine. The image is acting and masculine. The appearance, which is their offspring, begets in turn on souls capable of distance-beholding, and so is masculine as well. The feminine is "the hidden secret", the masculine "the secret to be revealed". Arche stands to telos. Heraclitus' "hidden harmony" is cited, and Apollo's lyre is mute without the god.
4. *Divisibility* [977–980]. In this view the stress falls on place, not on divisibility. Bodies are *separable* from one another but not separable in relation to elementary happening, whose medium is spatio-temporal continuity. Klages appeals to language: the executioner "separates" (*trennt*) the head rather than "dividing" it, and *teilen* means sharing (*mitteilen*, "shared joy is double joy"). When an apple is broken, neither its impenetrability nor its bare volume is divided. Quantifying parts by size or weight leads into noumena and "out of space". The vital origin of all quanta is intensity, gradable only by estimation. Following the Eleatics (once stripped of their errors), Klages distinguishes **motion** from **displacement** (change of distance). Only displacement is intelligible. Motion is the side of happening that needs places in space, that is, matter. Once the thing replaces the body, every transformation is read as motion and every motion as displacement. Chemistry's "separations and combinations" (electrolysis of water) quietly rely on two unintelligibles, interspace and motion. A world of things would be "a heap of separate worlds". The real body is an *individual*, decomposable only into parts different in kind.
5. *Primitive confirmation* [981]. For pre-scientific man the space between bodies is an *acting* space: the tree planted at a boy's birth, magic at a distance, cardinal directions treated as substances. Klages takes these as distorted witness to the real polarity between happening and places of appearance, whose scientific example is gravitation (n. 39). Intuitive space "strikes the bridge" between matter and primordial image. Matter would not appear without the now, nor the primordial image without the here.
6. *Placelessness of "pure" appearance* [982–995]. Klages recalls Vol. I: "the image-shape, without the body, is located nowhere or everywhere". Objection: images always need some carrier (screen, film, curtain of rain). Method: pure appearance is an *ideal limiting case*, like the gas law. One shows that the more bodily support is removed, the more placeless appearance becomes, until it ceases altogether. The test case is the dream. Waking reverie is only half-way, since the dreamer swings back to waking space. The sleep-dream is defined as "sensationless beholding" (first approximation), "in regard to phasms of the waking state" (second approximation). Stimulus theories of dreams (alarm clock, cold feet; Aristotle, *On Prophesying by Dreams*; Droste-Hülshoff, Eichendorff) are allowed as accounts of occasions, not of dreaming. Only what can be converted into visibility reaches the dreaming soul (alum-filter simile). Dream-characters, cited from Klages' own *Vom Traumbewußtsein* (1914/1919): change of place without motion, sudden shifts in time, loss of the objects' sameness, slippage of word-meanings. Examples: Keller's "kiss of Judith" that was apple cake, the rows of boys that turn into dentures, the "solution of the riddle of the world" that reads "everyone paints a goat on the wall". These show that dream appearances lack fixed places. Long quotations follow on fairy tale and Romanticism: Grimm's *Machandelboom* (in Low German), Hoffmann's *Golden Pot* with Anselmus in the flask on the Elbe bridge, Indian, Arabian and German tales in which size relations collapse (pomegranate beds, a dress in a nutshell), and the Witches' one-times-one from *Faust*. (The *Golden Pot* passages illustrate a different case, the "fascinated" consciousness, which stays bound to things and suffers the loss of shape and identity *in* them.) Thesis: in dreams space and time sink to the status of mirror-images, with "an unboundedly mobile Here" and "Now". Two conclusions are drawn [994]: as place becomes labile the unity of meaning of what appears vanishes too, so only place-bound corporeality admits the existential judgement; and since the essence of intuitive space and time "lies in the polarity of nearness and distance", the dream loses both and keeps only a "phantasm of presentness". Application: Chinese landscape painting, "sprung from dream-space", has a "distanceless depth" in which everything is distant and nothing properly so. Contrast Böcklin's *Triton and Nereid*, whose "shudder of distance" is to be had only "at the price of the actuality of nearness" (*nur um den Preis der Wirklichkeit der Nähe*), i.e. only where nearness is bodily real. "Only in bodily space do distance and nearness become demonic actualities."
7. *The seat of primal disturbance* [995–1000]. Ideologism flees pain (Plato, Schiller). But sensing corporeality gives as many delights as torments: Klages lists pains, reliefs, touch-pleasures, tastes, dance (partly self-quotation, n. 45). So the seat of primal disturbance is not pain but resistance to the impulse to move. Animal sensibility depends on motility more than the reverse. In man, the dominance of the *willing* over the *cognizing* attitude let spirit emancipate itself: "the body as barrier" provokes war on the here and now, and "the body as tool" lets it abolish distances. This settles the question left open on p. 265 about why even indifferent objects catch attention: everything perceived is tied to the bodily-actual. Example from p. 444: the sleeper who realizes the knocking had already sounded twice. These are "hovering" intuitions not yet joined to a place-determined sensation. "Awakening and disturbance are one and the same state of affairs." Only embodied images, that is, objects of perception, wake or disturb. **Grammar:** the corporeality of resistance is the vital origin of the grammatical *subject*, and its *strength* is the origin of the *predicate*. The existential judgement is also an intensity-judgement, because existence means self-assertion and a sphere of power. Hence the primitive's sense of mana (n. 47) and the child's sense of the power of things. The oldest comparison is a comparison of strengths. Judgement grades and compares, and at the "summit of the self-sovereignty of the understanding" it only counts.

**Key terms.** *Ortsbestimmtheit der Erscheinungswandlung* (matter as the place-determinacy of appearance's transformation; "receiving-place of images"). *Bewegung* vs *Verschiebung* (real motion vs displacement). *teilen* vs *trennen* (sharing/dividing vs separating). *Phasmen des Wachzustandes* (phasms of the waking state, the waking residues dreams work with). *empfindungsloses Schauen* (dreaming as sensationless beholding). *Labilität des Ortes* (lability of place in the dream). *Urstörungsstelle* (seat of primal disturbance). Intensity as "primal predicate".

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter completes the derivation of matter promised at the start of ch. 58. It joins the dream theory (ch. 55; ch. 30, "The Conceptual Affinity of Visible Appearance", which it asks the reader to reread) to the body theory. Its thesis that intuitive space is the polarity of nearness and distance is the hinge to Section 3, "Nearness and Distance" (ch. 62–65). The Chinese/Occidental contrast recalls Vol. I (ch. 33, on East Asian making-present); it is not developed further in 63–65. Its subject/predicate genealogy anticipates the treatment of grammar in ch. 66. The sex-polarity digression anticipates the Pelasgian world-picture (ch. 71–75).

**Critical note.** The dream argument by "ideal limiting case" is methodologically careful. The correlation of lost bodily anchorage with lost spatial fixity and identity is well documented with examples. But the evidence is selected for its fit. Dream-reports are filtered by Klages' own "criteria of the science of essences" (p. 988), and the reading of fairy tales as dream-consciousness assumes the thesis it is meant to confirm. The derivation of subject and predicate from the corporeality and strength of resistance is suggestive. But Klages extends it by mere assertion to all judgement: intensity, he says, is the "mediating link for all predicative connections" [1000]. That sits badly with his own view that qualities come from beholding and are not sensed (ch. 58), and he gives no account of how predicates of quality or relation are mediated by degree.

### Chapter 61 — Being (*Das Sein*) [1001–1021]

**Thesis.** Abstraction from the thing yields, by separate routes, the concepts of unity (from the thing as bearer of many properties), the One (from the bare "for-itself" of a separate existence, *nacktes Sonderdasein*), and Being (from bare self-assertion against happening, "empty sameness"). Their common source is the personal I, "the one and only metaphysical place where the changelessly existing intersects with the changing actuality". So spirit = being = "positive nothing". Objects of thought have a "near-character" (*Nahcharakter*), which confirms that conceiving arises in the here-and-now of disturbing resistance.

**Argument.**
1. *Unity* [1001–1003]. The thing is the point of relation for a plurality of properties: shape and impenetrability first, then degree, warmth, colour, gloss. "Unity in multiplicity" therefore needs no aesthetics. It can be read off any thing. Seeking it in *appearances* is "false reification". The more appearances captivate us, the more the thought of unity dissolves. In primordial images there is neither unity nor multiplicity, only "undivided and indivisible cohesion" or the "self-revealing of elemental souls". Example: Impressionism dissolves things and with them unities (Renoir; Faust's "In the coloured reflection we have life"). Unity is a late concept and presupposes number.
2. *The One* [1003–1007]. Appeal to language: the indefinite article comes from "one" (IE *oinos*). Derivation (of conditions, not of prehistory, n. 48): the primal act separates body from body, so a *between* belongs to the thing, spatial (one thing cannot be in several places) and temporal (several things cannot be in one place). The personal I is also a bearer of properties, but not of intuitive data. What is common to individual things and I's is the bare for-itself. Removing place as well leaves "one and yet another and yet another". The decisive cut removes existence itself. Klages gives a word-diagram here (garbled in the translation): *Sonder|dasein* splits into the One (*Sonder-*) and Being (*-dasein*). He rejects both older logicians (number from space or time) and the "newest logisticians" (spirit generates number from itself). The ideal between goes back to the intuitable between of things. The series of ones cannot be closed because nothing tells one step from the next. For the One, individual and species coincide. 1 = 1 is not an identity equation. It is the "primal equation": the sides differ by borrowed content and coincide by the pure concept. The equals-sign means "interchangeability without loss of sameness". Things are only *mistakable* for one another. Calculation prepares itself by turning thing-properties into magnitudes.
3. *Being* [1008–1011]. Isolating self-assertion against boundless happening leaves Being, "empty sameness", related to time only as a co-relation. "Being is" exhausts its content, while "God is" does not exhaust God. In Being, *esse* and *essentia* coincide. The copula derives from the identity of being with sameness (etymology of *sein*'s three stems, n. 51). "Being is one" (Parmenides, Plutarch) shows that Being needs individual existences as its counterpart. Eleatic Being and Pythagorean number are the two halves of the split *Sonderdasein*. Their common root is the I. Hence spirit = being = positive nothing. When spirit apprehends itself it appears as one, as unity, as "absolute" or "pure" I, and even, mistakenly, as a person (n. 52).
4. *Positive nothing* [1010–1011]. A history in brief: Plato's non-being as contrary (space), Aristotle's matter as potentiality, then Neoplatonic and negative theology ruled by *omnis determinatio est negatio*. The more Being is treated as the highest actuality, the more it must be stripped of predicates. Being is "nothing" in that no feature taken from appearing actuality applies to it. It is "positive" because, through the human I, it produces changes in actuality that cannot be derived from actuality. Pre-conceptual thought has no concept of nothing. Its "nothings" are always something real or the highest reality. Nirvana is the example: it counts as the only positive state.
5. *Duration* [1011–1014]. Physics interpolates between observations, and so does all ascertaining of fact: the desk could have vanished for a microsecond. Because spirit's acts are discrete and unextended (cf. the intermittence doctrine, ch. 40), no consciousness can "catch up with" continuity. The I has no continuous presence. It is absent in dreamless sleep, and dreams carry only the "phasm" of the person. Yet the I is the one real *invariant*, because it repeats identically with each act. Thought must posit an "I-basis" (*Ichanlage*), a functional constitution of the organism (n. 53). This mitigates the logical puzzle and leaves the metaphysical one: how spirit entered the cell. Continuity is the primal experience of intuition (movement may be its pattern, as later chapters will show). It is not produced by interpolation. Interpolation is what discrete thought must do to turn appearances into things.
6. *Body, soul, spirit; meaning vs concept* [1014–1018]. The body bears sensing and self-movement, the soul bears beholding and working, spirit bears acts of apprehension and will. Correlated with these are the world's materiality, its characters (appearance), and its self-identical objects. Applied to language: the sound is the body of the name, the concept its spirit, the *meaning* (*Bedeutung*) its soul. Linguistics since the Renaissance knows only body and spirit, which is like explaining music by instrument and score while forgetting the composer. Examples: the vessel words (bowl, cup, tureen, pitcher…) are clear in meaning but resist definition. *Lenz/Frühling* share a concept but differ in meaning (*Frühlingsgleiche* vs the comic *Lenzesgleiche*). Then the many German verbs for dying, and the parlour joke. K. O. Erdmann is credited (n. 54), but criticized for treating differences of meaning as subjective feeling-tones. Nine theses on meaning vs concept follow (the translation's numbering skips 5 and repeats 6). Meaning inheres in the name as soul in body. It is never absent, as old as language, changing, lived and undefinable, inexhaustible, never exactly the same twice, untranslatable, and serves "indicative thinking" (*hinweisendes Denken*). A concept is an artifact, historical, unchanging, definable, empty of content, exactly translatable, and serves judgement. The soul of language is "supra-organic": the speaker's soul is its medium but not to be equated with it, which is Klages' answer to the subjectivism he finds in Erdmann. Mastery of language is a *gift*, like a mood. One can choose *what* one says but not the *how* (he refers to ch. 7 of his *Ausdrucksbewegung und Gestaltungskraft*; ch. 66 of GWS is promised).
7. *Near-character of objects of thought* [1018–1021]. A pure concept-word would be a conventional mathematical sign (the Baroque dream of a logical algorithm). No such words exist, but there are "meaning-poor concept-words" (matter, thing, resistance, unity, the One, being) and "concept-weak meaning-words" (dawn, sea, storm, curse, sin). The former lack *distance* and have an "almost painful near-character". Objects of thought are outside space and time and have no nearness, so the nearness must come from their vital origin in the here-and-now of disturbing resistance. Thinking is "distance-blind", a "razor-sharp knife". Plato's system tastes as "atmosphereless" as atomism. The distancelessness of the personal I matches it, while the victory of distant images brings self-forgetfulness. Self-reflexive admission: the chapters themselves have used this "shallow reflection" (*flache Besinnung*). It cannot accomplish the reconnection of the near-world of sensing with the far-world of beholding. Eichendorff's "Es redet trunken die Ferne": whoever *thinks* distance gets only "so-and-so-far". The advocate of "deep reflection" can only point toward the inconceivable while insisting that it is actuality.

**Key terms.** *Einheit* (unity, from the thing as bearer of properties). *das Eins* (the One, the bare for-itself). *nacktes Sonderdasein* (naked separate-existence). *Fürsichsein*. *Sein* = *leere Einerleiheit* (empty sameness). *positives Nichts* (Being/spirit as a nothing that has effects). *Urgleichung* 1 = 1. *Ichanlage* (I-basis/disposition). *Bedeutung* vs *Begriff* (word-meaning, the soul of the name, vs concept). *bedeutungsarme Begriffswörter* / *begriffsschwache Bedeutungswörter*. *Nahcharakter der Denkgegenstände*. *flache* vs *tiefe Besinnung* (shallow vs deep reflection).

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter closes the "innermost ring around the concept of being" opened in Book I (ch. 1–9). It derives the formal concepts (unity, number, being, identity) from the vital ground set out in ch. 58–60. It supplies the equation spirit = being = positive nothing that the whole polemic depends on. It returns to the intermittence of acts (ch. 40) and to the I (ch. 12, 59). The meaning/concept theses (cf. ch. 11, p. 93) anticipate ch. 66. The closing judgement on "shallow reflection" applies the distinction of ch. 14 ("The Shallow and the Deep Reflection") to the method of Section 2 itself. The near/far language opens Section 3, Nearness and Distance.

**Critical note.** The meaning/concept contrast is the best part of the chapter. It treats word-meaning as lived and undefinable yet not a private feeling-tone, so it is neither Fregean sense (a shareable, definable content) nor Erdmann's subjectivism, and the examples are good. The weakest step is the equation spirit = being = "positive nothing". Being was derived as an *object of thought*, the residue of abstracting self-assertion from the thing. Spirit is the *power* behind the I's acts. Equating them lets an abstraction take on the causal role ("changes verifiable … in the actuality of happening") that alone earns the word "positive". The negative-theology problem he describes is renamed, not solved. The "near-character" of abstract words is an introspective report of his own (their "psychic aftertaste"). It cannot carry the genetic claim placed on it, that conceiving *originates* in the here of resistance.

### Chapter 62 — On the Experience of Movement (*Vom Bewegungserlebnis*) [1022–1062]

**Thesis.** The experience of movement (*Bewegungserlebnis*) is as original as the experience of impression, and cannot be built from sensations. Palágyi's "virtual movement" correctly explains place, position and distance. It cannot explain shape or the experience of movement itself, because it gives only direction and dynamics. The core of the experience of movement is *movedness* (*Bewegtheit*). Movedness is pathic, a being-moved, and contains no change of place. It is the form in which non-intuitable happening appears, passing through the impulse-capable body. All vital movement is therefore "suffered, never done". Co-movement (*Mitbewegtheit*) is the medium of all expression and all efficacy of images.

**Argument.**
1. *Against sensualism* [1022–1024]. For three centuries motility has been explained by concepts made for sensibility. Like Ptolemaic epicycles, this has produced sensations of position, force, tension, innervation, muscle, joint, "even of movement". Survey of visual illusions of movement: cinema, the ripple that seems to roll outward, the neighbouring train, a cliff that seems to topple against clouds, the autokinetic point of light, planetary loops. From these: visible appearance cannot carry the existential judgement about movement. A being that could only see would perceive no movement, because it would lack bodies.
2. *Palágyi's virtual movement* [1024–1029]. Report and praise. Palágyi alone "uprooted sensualism" with the equal originality of motility (cf. ch. 40). Quotations: only a self-moving being perceives movement. We "live our way into" movements without carrying them out, and this "movement-phantasm" (*Bewegungsphantasma*, later *tactile beholding*) explains localization of touch (the blind find the touched point P), the circular rim of a beaker felt with the palm, and why a gaze fixed on our body is felt there. Touch "educates" sight (the finger moving along a red line). Automatized movements keep their "virtual rudder" outside consciousness. "Inverse" virtual movement, driven by vegetative processes, underlies fantasy and dream. Klages' own illustration (from his foreword to Palágyi's *Wahrnehmungslehre*, n. 56) is *virtual speaking* in silent reading. Three results are accepted as established: inner movement is original and polar to impression; virtual movement solves the problem of place; virtual movement takes part in apprehending distance.
3. *Critique of Palágyi* [1029–1034]. (a) It is undecided whether virtual movement produces or only shapes the image of space. (b) Astonishingly, Palágyi never uses it to explain the perception of movement. It explains only static data (place, shape, distance), and it *cannot* explain movement perception. (c) On shape: directional impulses link places, but "from ever so many feelings of direction there does not flow together the experience of a shape". A polygon of aims is not a circle. "Imaging movement" assumes the shape it is meant to produce. The mistake is kinematic: curve replaced by an infinite series of tangents, the science of appearance confused with mechanics. Language: one speaks of the *direction* of a blow, not its shape. Shape is "now-shape" and the opposite of movement, much as space is the opposite of time (n. 57). (d) Even *place* needs intuitive space already. Without at least embryonic mirroring beholding, sensations would "fall apart in time". (e) Palágyi takes too much from sight. If visual contents had no shape they would have no colour either, and could give no hold for touch to correlate with. His demonstration with the finger presupposes a visible finger. Diagnosis: Palágyi called himself a Pythagorean in his view of spirit, but as a biologist he was an extreme Heraclitean. He was bound to the stream of time, so space and shape dissolved for him into succession, while his belief in mechanics made "vital movement" a copy of mechanical movement. His virtual movement gives "only its geometrical view".
4. *Varieties of movement-feeling; dynamics* [1034–1039]. Direction is still only an isolated abstraction. It must be tied back to happening, which requires the lived experience of original time. Present intuitability alone gives either successive positions or a rigid trace (the circling spark as a luminous circle). A mere onlooker would be in Plato's motionless world of shapes. Klages now *does* use "activity/passivity", reversing ch. 58, with a caveat. Active movement-feelings (spontaneous, reactive) share a *dynamics*: feelings of tension, impulse, exertion, force and achievement. All presuppose experiencing the opposition of alien and own corporeality. Polar to the sensation of resistance, which makes objectification possible, the drive-impulse makes purpose-positing possible. Kinematics confirms "laws of the figural movement-schema", not laws of movement. Inner "movement of thought" and walking are treated alike (cf. the doctrine of will). A disembodied soul could not even think "movements of thought". From ch. 45: the image contributes only *direction*. Strength and speed come from privation. So dynamics is only one side, and the way to the source runs through *passivity*. Reflexes are not experienced as movement-feelings, and they are not machines: they answer only similarly to the similar. Klages criticizes the concept of the "reflex arc".
5. *Expressive movement and its suffering-tone* [1039–1043]. Every bodily process is expressive, and expression grows as automatism falls. One sees in an animal's gait *whether* it seeks, flees or attacks, not *what for*. Two neglected points: expressive movement is *suffered*, and expresses something only for that reason. Dynamic feelings vary by strength. *Mood* (*Stimmung*) is qualitative. Every dynamic has its mood, and the excitable soul answers the goal-character of images with dynamics, the beholding soul answers the image-character of goals with mood. Activity depends on passivity as the limbs on the heart. "The life-process … is suffered, never done" (n. 59). Expression is perceived through co-movement (n. 60 rebuts the view that others' inner states are inferred). Co-movement is strongest with "resistanceless being-seized". The *actus purus*, and all doing as such, has no expression and is known only by inhibition ("regulation"). Process outdoes state in expressiveness (blushing over redness), and rigid forms are read as movement in language (the path "meanders", the rock "plunges"). Theses: vital movement acts by awakening co-movement; the passivity of co-movement already belongs to the acting movement; self-movement is a "having-to-move-oneself".
6. *Origin of movement in the individual* [1043–1047]. Inner-bodily sensations (thirst, joints, eye convergence) are not parts of impulses or images, only here-and-now symptoms used for steering (n. 62 against Poincaré). What links the bearer to non-present images are vegetative processes, "sleeping efficacy and sleeping beholding". The intuitive image is the actuality of the image transposed by pairing beholding with sensing. The drive-impulse is efficacy transposed into dynamics by pairing with bodily mobility. Waking means being-for-itself, which means corporeality. In a mobile being, happening "necessarily becomes space-traversing movement, and the impulse to movement becomes urge against resistances". The microcosm thesis of ch. 56 is sharpened: the individual relates to the world only because its here-and-now is also a there-and-then, "a place of passage of elementary efficacy". Five conclusions follow: the impulse is *compelled*; the compulsion takes the form of dynamics because only movement unites what is separate in the body; it is *inner* compulsion, not mechanical pushing; co-movement is the basis for clarifying every coming-into-movement; the remaining question is how happening passes into movedness. Protozoa: outer happening induces inner happening, which induces self-movement.
7. *Demonstration of pathic movement* [1048–1052]. Klages gives a scale of appearances from pathic to mechanical: sail, leaves, clouds; then pond waves, cornfield, river; rain, waterfall, migrating storks, fleeing deer, sprinter; finally thrown stone, tap, machines. The pathic ones show "self-movement wholly without impulse" and imply a *virtuality of being-moved* that is distinct from virtual dynamics. Ontogenetic proof: rocking and carrying the infant. Man's unusually long immaturity, without locomotion, makes "being-moved-along" his primordial experience. This confirms the human preponderance of beholding over efficacy, and it is the root of human co-movement through images. Adults show it in the love of vehicles, of riding, skating and swimming, and of the fairground: swings, carousels, roller-coasters. The line runs straight back to the cradle (n. 64 laments that rocking is now frowned upon). This also explains the age-old view that the soul moves the body (*aeikineton*). The view comes from the two-sided feeling in which a movement stands out as brought about by an impulse. It is right that psychic happening is needed for movement. It is wrong to assign movement to the soul: "without psychic happening no movement of the body would take place; but it is only by virtue of corporeality that this happening assumes the form of movement". Movement is a "propagation" from the non-intuitable interior into the world of intuition.
8. *Movedness as the appearance-form of happening* [1052–1056]. Isolate movedness from its dynamics and bearer, and it shows continuous change of state but *no change of place*. Three proofs: (i) virtuality itself (silent speech, co-experiencing fighting animals or drifting clouds) would be impossible if movedness were tied to change of place (n. 65); (ii) dreams: lability of place forbids real locomotion (hence the nightmare of running on the spot), while the pathic character of dreaming allows *dream-flying*, aimless, effortless and placeless; (iii) dance: dynamic self-movement is gradually carried and swallowed by goal-free being-moved, "being-borne by the stream of happening" (n. 66, Rudolf Bode). Result: in movedness, image-receptive and stimulus-sensitive soul, acting and suffering, are neutralized. The "stream-like", efficacy without activity, is the third term between happening and movement. "The ground of all real efficacy … would be the excitation of similar rhythms." Without this inflow, space would fall apart into positions and dynamics would face an untraversable between (the "spectre of forces in physics"). In happening, places are not yet articulated: "Time as the soul of space, every distance a distance-of-the-past". Movement needs the *body* to separate activity from passivity and place from space.
9. *World-images depend on self-movedness* [1056–1060]. Movement comes first among appearances of change because only it connects the there with the here. All change becomes intuitable only through co-excited movement-feelings ("plunge" of temperature, hours "trickle", years "flee"). Experiments: the threshold for movement is lower than the spatial two-point threshold, about a quarter (Exner, n. 67). Animals notice what moves, and the best scarecrow is the most irregularly mobile. Fork between animal and man: in the animal, co-movement turns at once into self-excitation for the care of life (the grazing deer). Man can stay with the image and sink into its meaning. This is Goethe's "intuitive judgement" ("inventing … a revelation kindled within, at the outer"). Dynamics disturbs co-movement and comes close to "a willing that is simply contrary to rhythm". In ecstasy, at the limit, even feeling and movedness vanish: "Feelings are the wealth of men and the poverty of the gods."
10. *Movement-content of forms* [1060–1062]. Every datum of intuition, rigid forms included (column, tree, horizon, ornament), can be beheld pathically as a symbol of happening. But the pathic does not *generate* shape. Left to itself it would dissolve shape. As resistance is the pattern of intensity, so the continuity of being-in-motion is "probably" the pattern of all continuity (redeeming the hint in ch. 61). Even so, shape comes from intuitive space, not from motion. Shape, and above all visible shape, is the emblem of intuitive space as the empirical now, so it "offers itself as an emblem to the faith in being and to the Ideas". People differ in how strongly they feel co-movement before forms. Klages warns against reading this directly as a difference in nearness to happening. Geometry hollows out shape. The distance from geometry to Moorish ornament is almost the distance from spirit's imageless being to the actuality of images. Plato required geometry for entry and reduced bodies to triangles.

**Key terms.** *Bewegungserlebnis* (experience of movement, original and not made of sensations). *virtuelle Bewegung* (Palágyi's movement-phantasm). *Dynamik* (tension, impulse, exertion, force). *Stimmung* (mood, the qualitative tone of feeling). *pathisch* / *Pathik* (suffered character). *Bewegtheit* (movedness, being-moved, placeless continuous change of state). *Mitbewegtheit* / *Mitbewegung* (co-movement, the medium of expression and empathy). *Mitwahrnehmung* (co-perception of movement in rigid forms). "the stream-like" (efficacy without activity). "excitation of similar rhythms" as the ground of real efficacy. *Ausdrucksbewegung* (expressive movement). Goethe's "intuitive judgement".

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter opens Section 3 by grounding nearness and distance in movement. It redeems the promise of ch. 40 about Palágyi. It takes up the drive-impulse (ch. 45–46) and the microcosm (ch. 56), and applies the protozoa evidence of ch. 58. It defers the perception of movement to ch. 63. Its principle that efficacy is the excitation of similar rhythms, and that expression is co-movement, prepares Part 1's final division, the Efficacy of Images (ch. 66–70), and the theory of word-meaning promised for ch. 66. The infant/cradle and dance material feeds the Pelasgian chapters.

**Critical note.** The critique of Palágyi is precise and strong. A set of directional impulses does not yield a shape, and both a shapeless visual field and the kinematic tangent picture are well diagnosed. The positive doctrine, that movedness contains no change of place and is the appearance of non-intuitable happening, rests on three cases (virtuality, dream-flying, dance). They show that felt movedness *can* separate from locomotion, not that it is prior to it or of cosmic origin. The leap from fairground rides and the cradle to "time as the soul of space" is asserted, not argued.

### Arc of Ch. 58–62

Chapters 58–61 make up the whole of Section 2, "Soul and Body". Their single task is the one ch. 58 states at the start: to find where the *here* comes from, as Vol. I and ch. 55–57 found where the *now* comes from, and so to answer at once the questions left open since Book I: identity of the changing, the existential judgement, movement, corporeality, place, matter. The method mirrors the earlier genesis of intuition. Sensation is isolated artificially (ch. 58), its key duality is shown to be where spirit strikes (ch. 59), its object, matter, is defined against both mechanism and dream-appearance (ch. 60), and the abstractions built on top of it (unity, number, being) are traced back to it and to the I (ch. 61). Chapter 62 opens Section 3. It turns from the near-world of sensing to the bridge that leads back to the far-world of beholding: movement, and in particular *movedness*.

The block cashes out several earlier results. The thesis that reflection springs from points of disturbance (p. 280) gets its bodily location: the "doubled here" of resistance (58), the *Urstörungsstelle* (60). The doctrine of the intermittent, extensionless act (ch. 40) and of the I as self-assertion (ch. 9, 12, 46) becomes the account of identity: the thing's sameness is a projected "copy of the I-point" (59), and the I is the only genuine invariant (61). The doctrine of the drive-impulse (ch. 45–46) is taken apart into resistance, want and direction (58) and then into dynamics and pathic movedness (62). Book III's thesis that thought "interpolates" a continuity that only intuition has (61) completes the case against both "materiologism" (Democritus, Boscovich, mechanics) and "ideologism" (Plato, the cult of shape). Klages presents the two as sharing one error, taking the thing for actuality, and as differing only in whether they make a fetish of the point or of the form.

The block's main achievement is a *vital genealogy of formal ontology*. Corporeality = impenetrability. Thing = self-asserting place. Matter = place-determinacy of transformation. Subject/predicate = corporeality and strength of resistance. The One and Being are the two halves of a "naked separate-existence". Spirit = being = positive nothing. Alongside it runs a positive phenomenology of the living: graded resistance, quality, voluptuous pleasure and pain in protozoa; the dream as sensationless beholding with labile place; meaning as the soul of the word; movedness, co-movement and expression as the medium of all efficacy. The second strand hands forward directly to ch. 63–65 (the perception of visible movement in 63, then the plant-soul and the elemental souls in 64–65, under the heading of nearness and distance first stated in ch. 60) and to ch. 66–70 (the efficacy of images, "excitation of similar rhythms", word-meaning).

Some tensions should be noted. (1) Ch. 58 makes *motility* the ruling pole in animals and has beholding "led by the hand" by sensing. Ch. 62 makes the human infant's *being-moved* primordial and asserts a human preponderance of beholding over efficacy. That fits Klages' claim of human exceptionalism, but it means the derivation of the thing from sensation (58–59) describes a structure that in man is supposed to be secondary. (2) Ch. 58 sets aside the words "activity/passivity" only "for the time being", so their adoption in ch. 62 is staged, not inconsistent. The real shift is elsewhere: ch. 58 makes self-movement the "ruling pole", and ch. 62 makes self-movement itself a "having-to-move-oneself", life "suffered, never done". The two are compatible only if the primacy of motility in 58 is read as primacy over *sensing* and not as activity. (3) The I is found *through* one's own body as a thing (59, cf. ch. 12), yet the thing's identity is a reflex of the I's (59). Klages separates finding from grounding, but the grounding rests on the I's "real sameness" as a repeated act, which ch. 61 asserts and then leaves to a metaphysical remainder (how spirit entered the cell). (4) "Positive nothing" is given causal powers through the I, while the block says that how spirit entered the cell is beyond explanation (61). The central metaphysical claim of the book is thus restated, not grounded. (5) In ch. 61 Klages admits that the method of these chapters is the "shallow reflection" (*flache Besinnung*) they criticize, and that it cannot accomplish the "reconnection" of sensing with beholding. This is a frank methodological admission, and it marks the shift in register in ch. 62: from derivation to pointing, from argument to scales of examples (sail → machine, cradle → carousel → dance).

Questions handed forward: how movement is *perceived* (explicitly deferred to ch. 63); the full theory of nearness and distance and of the "demonic" actuality of distance in bodily space (63–65); the soul of language and word-meaning (66); the claim that all real efficacy is the excitation of similar rhythms (66–70); and the sexual-cosmic polarity of matter (feminine) and image (masculine) introduced in passing in ch. 60, which belongs to the Pelasgian world-picture (71–75).

*Source note.* Chapters 63–65 close the subsection on Nearness and Distance (62–65). Chapters 66–69 make up the Fourth Section, *Vom Wirkungsvermögen der Bilder (Zusammenhang und Beziehung)* [1143]. The translation is unrevised. In the layout, the last lines of Ch. 66 (on feminine abstract nouns and goddess-names) have been spliced into the first page of Ch. 67 at [1176]; I give that material under Ch. 66. The schematic figures referred to in Chs. 66–67 (Figs. 9–15) are missing from the text, so they are described here only as far as the prose describes them.

### Chapter 63 — On the Problem of the Perceivability of Movement (*Zum Problem der Bewegungswahrnehmbarkeit*) [1063–1080]

**Thesis.** Movement is seen only because seeing is always also the experience of a touchable, self-located body (the "indicator"). Movement appears only within a narrow band of speeds set by the rhythm of the perceiver's moments of making-present. Since every appearance of movement presupposes such pulsation, the happening of the world must itself pulse: "actuality-time pulses" [1079].

**Argument.**
1. *Recap of Ch. 62.* An intuitive image looks moving only because it arouses a virtual co-motion (*virtuelle Mitbewegung*) in the soul. So all appearance of movement is expression, and the question "how is movement perceived?" is a question about expression: under what conditions does a change of position *appear as* movement? Physiological conditions (eye position, retinal displacement) are set aside in favour of psychological and metaphysical ones [1063].
2. *Non-relativity of seen movement.* In the moment of perception, the direction, speed and path of a seen movement are always fixed one way. We see the moon racing toward the cloud *or* the cloud toward the moon, never just a shrinking distance between them. Physics' relativization of motion has only a partial support in experience: perceived direction can reverse, but it is never left undecided [1063–64].
3. *The indicator (Indikator).* A being that only saw would not perceive movement at all, not even apparent movement. Movement is visible only against the felt rest or felt motion of one's own body, which serves as an unconditioned reference that cannot be swapped for any other. The body is the centre of a "system of positions" (*Lagensystem*). The example of scanning a bookshelf shows this. Retinal images shift, yet nothing seems to move, because the shelf's position relative to the body is fixed at a glance and then "stands fast." This undercuts the sensualist account that starts from retinal displacement [1064–65].
4. *Moon/cloud reversal explained.* The indicator is always the body. What changes is which appearance the soul binds into the body's system of positions, and so where the virtual co-motion attaches. The *auxiliary indicator* (*Hilfsindikator*) is the earth, taken as absolutely at rest. The train illusion is the example: the neighbouring train pulls out, and we feel our own standing train moving. A glimpse of a station pillar switches the system of positions and corrects the illusion without any need to check by touch [1068].
5. *Excursus: movement as the paradigm of meaning.* Klages restates that intuitive images are not mosaics of timeless sense data (sensualism). They are unities of meaning shot through with the perceiver's life-past. Examples: an ill-humoured face, and a station façade nobody can describe from memory. The sense data have to be dissected out afterwards by the understanding. Appearance of movement is the "most immediate shape-character of happening," so it is the bridge from what can be shown to what appears. This makes his theory a "physiognomics of the universe," in contrast to "geometrical" theories. It also implies that a physiognomics should begin with processes rather than shapes. The Eleatics and Plato, and later mechanism, prove the point by having to abolish or "de-actualize" movement in order to keep the primacy of being [1066–68]. This is assertion backed by historical illustration more than argument.
6. *Rest-forms and self-movement.* Shapes too arouse co-motion: the poplar "strains upward," the Gothic piers "tear the beholder upward." But everything belongs to one rigid system of positions, so this co-motion is felt as inward and incorporeal. In one's own locomotion there is a split between bodily dynamics and the co-felt "unmovedness of the earth." The dream of being unable to get anywhere shows this standstill as a felt obstacle. Klages warns that cultivating sensitivity to the "life of forms" can thin out into the subjective abstracta of aesthetics [1069–70].
7. *Speed thresholds.* We see the sun or a clock hand change position without seeing it move. A fast-rotating light becomes a circle, and a cannonball is invisible. Perceived movement depends reciprocally on spatial and temporal discrimination thresholds. Klages sets out four cases (a, b, a', b') for how visible speed rises or falls [1071–72].
8. *Von Baer and Sigwart.* He reports at length K. E. von Baer's 1860 address, which imagines life slowed a thousandfold so that a year passes like 8¾ hours, and Sigwart's 1884 treatise on altered spatial and temporal senses. He then corrects three errors in them. (i) Every such transformation could equally come from a change in the *world*, so the dependence is a reversible polarity of appearance and soul, not a one-way subjectivism. (ii) Both authors think in terms of things, not appearances. The microcosm's power of intuition is shaped far more by the macrocosm than the reverse, which is why no animal has been found that reacts to movement where we see rest. (iii) Their notion of "inner time" is really a change in the *subjective metric* (fractions of the second). It rests on measuring thought and on an individualism that cuts the perceiver and its world off from all others [1073–77].
9. *Positive result.* Suppose there are at most about 16 moments of making-present per second (a hypothesis Klages calls uncertain). Two positions then appear as succession at about 1/8 s, as movement at about 1/16 s, and as one figure at about 1/32 s. Seen movement requires that spatial displacement neither lag far behind nor greatly outrun the rate of the "turning-points" (*Wendepunkte*). Movement is therefore always expressed by an actual change of position, but only if that change can arouse virtual movement. What comes to expression is an unintuitable happening that "borrows" its speed-differences from matter [1077–79].
10. *Metaphysical conclusion.* The pulsatory character of life is now "brought to unshakeable certainty," since without articulated moments there would be no before and after. He then infers that world-happening itself pulses, because otherwise beings that pulse could not have arisen. The image of waves borne by ever larger waves, up to the "world-ocean," is offered as "more than mere simile" [1079–80].

**Key terms.** *Indikator* (indicator): the felt rest or motion of one's own body, against which all visible appearance is measured. *Hilfsindikator* (auxiliary indicator): the earth. *Lagensystem* (system of positions). *virtuelle Mitbewegung* (virtual co-motion; in rest-forms, "co-resting"). *Momente der Vergegenwärtigung / Wendepunkte* (moments of making-present, turning-points: the pulse of lived experience). *Wirklichkeitszeit* vs. metric time. "Physiognomics of the universe" vs. "geometrical" theories of perception.

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter finishes the theory of movement-perception begun in Ch. 62, which is the chapter's own back-reference. It ties the wave and pulse model of the life-process (Ch. 40's "counting of spiritual acts" is cited) to a cosmological claim: actuality is rhythmic. That claim is used again in Ch. 66 (similarity of rhythms as the link between elements and speech) and in Ch. 68 (wave-like reciprocity of past and present). It also announces the later discussion of "plurality of worlds" (Ch. 67's *singulare tantum*).

**Critical note.** The indicator analysis is a sharp phenomenological point against retinal sensualism. The bookshelf and train cases are well chosen. The final step from "perceivers pulse" to "world-happening pulses" rests on a bare causal-likeness premise: pulsing beings could not have arisen from a happening that does not pulse [1079–80]. It is no more than asserted. It also undercuts the chapter's own result. The pulse was introduced as exactly what separates the *appearance* of movement (coming and going, before and after) from happening "in itself" [1079]. Transferring it to world-happening erases the distinction that the argument needed.

### Chapter 64 — On the Plant-Soul (*Von der Pflanzenseele*) [1081–1115]

**Thesis.** The plant is essentially a being of *sleeping beholding and working* (*schlafendes Schauen und Wirken*). It is bound to the earth's gravity and to light, not to sensation and locomotion. Biology's finds of plant "sensitivity" come from carrying the animal stimulus–response schema over illegitimately. In the animal matter depends on the body; in the plant the body depends on matter [1097]. The plant, and above all the tree, is the transition from self-beings to the elemental souls, and its soul belongs largely to the soul of landscape.

**Argument.**
1. *The problem.* Earlier chapters made the plant more "soul-like" than the animal and so nearer to primal man. Since Romanticism, biology has reported plant sensitivity that seems to outdo the animal's, which appears to contradict this [1081].
2. *Survey of distinguishing marks.* Nine textbook contrasts (cell membranes, independence of parts, unlimited growth, external surfaces, liquid nutrition, mineral nutrition and chlorophyll, absence of stimulus-movement, sessility, up–down vs. front–back axis), each with its exceptions. Klages accepts that the concept must be fixed on the most developed forms [1082–85].
3. *Evidence against him.* Phototropism with fine intensity discrimination (Molisch's nasturtium; resultant of two light sources), the law of stimulus-quantity, an oat seedling responding to 1/2000 s of light, slime-mould moisture tropism, tendrils that react to a cotton thread at 1/4000 mg but not to mercury or rain (Pfeffer), and the motile plants [1085–86].
4. *Reframing.* The motile protists are a "world of pre-forms" in which plant and animal are not yet split. Bacteria and swarm-spores belong on the animal side, so algae, ferns and similar plants alternate between plant and animal phases. The plant's primal image is shown by: (a) the ordinary person's single schematic drawing of "plant" against no single schema for "animal," together with vegetal ornament; (b) palaeontology, where sessile tree-forms go back to the Palaeozoic; (c) the development from water to land and from rootless to rooted, ending in the angiosperms. Cormophytes (rooted, wind-pollinated, motionless germ cells) stand against thallophytes (rootless, akin to water, with motile sperm). Taking motility as the plant's essence would be like denying man upright gait because infants crawl [1087–89].
5. *Methodological warning.* Time-lapse film and magnifying growth-recorders mislead, because they separate visible process from bodily process (metric time detached from actuality-time). A Gulliver-sized fly would collapse (Galileo's scaling). The scientist of appearance may admit only movements actually *seen* [1089–90].
6. *Critique of tropism theory.* "Tropism" and "nasty" are classificatory names that have been mistaken for explanations, like "association" in psychology. The geotropism case: statolith starch (Haberlandt, Němec) cannot explain the root's downward tendency. Roots also grow sideways or toward light (mangroves), fungal roots have no starch, and in a sown sack half the seeds lie root-pole upward, where the inverse pressure would not count as a "disturbance." In any case pressure is not "felt," and it could not produce earthward tendency in a non-intuiting being unless that tendency were already "prescribed." The "leaf-eyes" and "plant nerves" of some authors, and Fechner's plant-soul alongside his psychophysics, show that spiritualizing is the reverse side of mechanism. This is a reductio plus genealogy, aimed at "English sensualism" in botany [1090–94].
7. *No sensation of gravity.* Gravitation, light, magnetism and electricity are what metaphysics would call "forms of distant presence in the here and now" [1094]. A being shaped chiefly by them is a being of sleeping beholding. Even in animals, semicircular canals and otoliths give no localizable sensations, only intuitable posture-data. Gravity penetrates every body completely and depends only on distance, so it can never mark off one impenetrable body from another, which is what sensation requires. That is why position receptors lie inside the body, never on its surface. Receptivity to gravity is a side of sleeping beholding. Klages reads the physicist's equation of inert and heavy mass as two faces of matter: resistance (realizing place) and weight (belonging to cosmic happening, "the most general form of the presence of the cosmos in the here and now") [1094–97].
8. *Objective vs. subjective axis.* The plant's axis lines up with the earth's radius. It is "objective," running nadir to zenith, with no front or back. The animal's front–back axis is "subjective" and free of the earth's directions (infusoria, fish, birds) [1094–95].
9. *Symbolic excursus.* Here Klages announces "thinking in images" in preparation for Part 2. Plant as earth-symbol and kin to the "telluric soul of woman" (flowers, wreaths, herb-lore). *Zoon/animal* (life, sensation) against *phyton/Gewächs* (growth). Root and underworld, Pluto/Aidoneus, wreaths and graves, falling leaves and the "fallen," *cadaver* from *cadere*. Leaves as powers of the sun: the plant *feeds on light*, "distance materializes itself in the plant" [1100]. Man shares the plant's vertical. In man the "presence of what is distant" has been transformed into the "appearance of what is distant" [1101]: the plant's sleeping beholding and working returns in him as the beholding of the soul. The right angle grounds "upright," "straight," and in Native American sign language "truth." The tree of life is a genuine symbol and the "tree of knowledge" a sham-symbol made by putting the "light of spirit" in place of the light of nature. Goethe's quatrain on the rose is cited [1097–1102]. This section is assertion and appeal to language and myth, not argument.
10. *Distance-receptivities and "atmosphere."* Gurwitsch's mitogenetic rays (onion roots) would show the reciprocity of working and beholding between two distant cells. This is used to give "atmosphere," aura and *genius loci* the status of working actualities [1102–03].
11. *Near-receptivity as receptivity to kind.* Plants register pressure, moisture and warmth (the water-lily's opening) with great fineness. Klages argues that intensities exist only for beings that become aware of foreign bodies. Underneath, every intensity difference is a difference of *kind*: pointed, rough, woolly and so on. The decisive evidence is that the tendril answers roughness, not pressure; dodder twines only on living hosts, not brass; phototropism needs blue light while CO₂ uptake needs red; and pollen begins to grow only on a stigma of its own kind. With Ch. 56 cited, nearness and distance hardly differ for the plant: it is "a soul of matter with happening itself," and every felt change in its surroundings "embodies itself" as growth. Its "mood" is compared to our vegetative moods and dreams [1103–09].
12. *Genuine plant movements.* Desmodium, circling shoots, and diurnal leaf movements express inner movedness. Mimosa, Drosera and Dionaea are "animal-plants" at the furthest point toward the animal, yet even they lack seeking and fleeing and are reflex-like [1109–10].
13. *Formula.* The perfectly beholding being is a *patient of the soul*, the animal a *dynamic agent of the body*, the plant a *patient of the body*. The animal counter-works against touch, the plant transforms what penetrates it. Touch is the mystery of corporeality, penetration the mystery of happening, and only touch was open to conceiving thought (physics, chemistry: alloiosis can never be reduced to kinesis) [1110–11].
14. *Toward the elemental souls.* The plant's individual soul is best approached through scent, which is to the flower as the nimbus is to the soul. Most of its soul-content belongs to the souls of landscapes. Against the nominalist ("landscape as a mere thought-thing, like the sevenness of seven goats"), Klages argues that "landscape" is a general concept just like "man" or "plant". The individual landscape is as concrete as the individual man, and the concept is grounded in individual intuitive images in which a "supra-organic" life appears. Consistent abstraction from it would dissolve every concept down to points. Regional character is carried chiefly by flora (heath, uplands, tropical rivers). Destroying it "wounds the earth itself." Tree-cult came before both animal-gods and human gods. The chapter closes with Xerxes' plane tree (Herodotus VII): "the elemental souls take flight" [1111–15].

**Key terms.** *schlafendes Schauen und Wirken* (sleeping beholding and working). *Schwereempfänglichkeit* (receptivity to gravity, a mode of sleeping beholding and not a sensation). "Objective" vs. "subjective" axis. *Artempfänglichkeit* (receptivity to kind) vs. intensity. *Pathiker des Leibes / der Seele* (patient of the body / of the soul) vs. *Dynamiker des Leibes* (the animal). Touch (*Berührung*) vs. penetration (*Durchdringung*). *Tellurismus/Siderismus* (earth-pole and star-pole). Genuine vs. sham symbol.

**Contribution to the whole.** Completes the "metaphysics of the cell" begun in Ch. 58 (receptivity to kind as a basic property of protoplasm, cited [1108]). Applies the nearness–distance theory of Ch. 56 to the plant. Fixes the plant / animal / primal-man triad that runs through Book V. Supplies the bridge (matter has a soul; landscape is actual) that Ch. 65 needs. Resumes the devastation motif ("as we have heard and shall go on hearing" [1114]) that Chs. 65 and 68 develop.

**Critical note.** The reductio of statolith-pressure accounts of geotropism, and the point that tendrils and pollen track *kind* rather than force, are real conceptual gains against an overstretched stimulus model. The step from "difference of kind" to "beholding" is the weak point. It equivocates between qualitative selectivity, which any chemical specificity shows, and an ensouled receptivity, so the plant-soul is assumed under the name *Schauung* rather than demonstrated. The gender symbolism (woman's suicide methods [1099]) carries no evidential weight at all.

### Chapter 65 — The Elemental Souls (*Die Elementarseelen*) [1116–1142]

**Thesis.** The appearances of nature and of great anonymous art reveal *extra-human* powers, the souls of the elements. They are real because intuition requires reciprocity between soul and happening. Elemental souls are "unreleased" (*unentbunden*) image-souls. They come to appearance only in a *gamos* with a receptive soul, through elementary similarity. They are souls of distance and therefore of the past. They are now leaving a planet devastated since about 1830.

**Argument.**
1. *The question.* The tree has lived experience but no senses. Light and darkness, which have no receptivity, seem to have no claim to soul. Yet something seizes us in landscapes and art. Witnesses: Eichendorff, Goethe (the artist sees "sacred vibrations" everywhere; the shudder in a sacred wood), and Eichendorff's Memnon image. This shows that what is meant is not human mood but extra-human actuality, revealed only under rare conditions [1116–18].
2. *Anonymous works.* Karnak, the pyramids, Ravenna, the Alhambra, Gothic architecture, folk song, the Vedas and the Nibelungenlied work through the persons who made them. Personal feeling *disturbs* their elemental content. That the mightiest works are nameless is read as a sign of this. "The poet is the heart of the world" (Eichendorff) [1118–19].
3. *Reductio of vitalism.* Natural science works only with externally conditioned, calculable processes. Inner forming forces (form-monad, *vis essentialis*, *nisus formativus*, entelechy, the "immaterial factor") all mean *spirit*, and they cancel calculability. Kant's remark that "living matter" cannot be thought is turned around: if living beings are made of matter, matter cannot be lifeless. Klages restates his own view of spirit: it acts only in man, only destructively, and never in a mechanically lawful world. Vitalism leads to spiritism [1119–21].
4. *Transcendental argument for the life of happening.* Intuition presupposes beholding. Perfect beholding is the soul's becoming one with happening, and so also happening's becoming one with lived experience. If happening were alien to soul, nothing would appear. So the life of happening is "certain beyond all doubt," as certain as intuiting souls are. The open question is only how elemental life differs from the life of individual beings [1121].
5. *Evidence from language (cf. Chs. 20–23, 36).* All words for the psychic are image-words (sweet love, bitter grief). This refutes the three-thousand-year confusion of the soul's *distinguishability* from the intuitive-corporeal with its *separability*. It leaves open how the bitterness of quinine differs from the bitterness of grief [1121–22]. Worked example: dry/moist. Dry = un-youthful, fixed, isolated, sober (an etymology of *nüchtern* from monastic *nocturnas vigilias*). Moist and flowing = youthful, communicative, world-connected, intoxicated. The Ionian element-doctrines (Thales, Anaximenes, Heraclitus) and Empedocles' god-names for the four roots mean such physiognomic powers, not modern substances. Aristotle (*Metaphysics* I.8) misread them by his own measure [1121–24].
6. *Essence-research and fact-research.* They share a ground, so fact-truths are folded inside essence-truths. Myth does not guide science, but science carries the "eggshells of its origin." Earth = isolated matter; water = what connects; air nearer to happening; fire = the substance of happening (Heraclitus's *archē*; Cardanus denied fire was an element). Facts that fit: *corpora non agunt nisi fluida*; colloidal plasma; water content of organisms; the most fluid parts (blood) are the most living and decay first, the hardest (bone, wood, coal) the deadest. "Soul of man, how you resemble water" [1124–27].
7. *Böcklin as witness.* Baroque painting's rendering of material qualities ends either in "realism" (theatricality) or in conjuring the souls of substances. Böcklin is "the discoverer of the soul of water." Unlike Corot, the Dutch or Stäbli, his water is neither useful nor a mirror for human feeling. In *Play of the Waves* the sea-creatures are "one and the same thing" with the wave. Ancient demons come from the Poseidonian element and take man back into fluid happening, the reverse of personifying nature [1127–31].
8. *Metaphysics of release.* Soul is the "psychic pole" (pure capacity to undergo) and happening the "world-pole" (capacity to act). Without the world-pole the psychic pole does not take place. The world-pole without souls still exists, but in a neutralized state, "like magnetism in unmagnetized iron," comparable to the ancients' chaos. The elemental souls "are there and are not there." In the *gamos* with the receiving soul they divide into working power and archetype. What strikes us is not made up but "released" [1131–32].
9. *Elementary similarity (elementare Ähnlichkeit, the doctrine of Ch. 34).* This is the "means of all actual efficacy," and it reaches into chaos too [1132]. Anaxagoras' homoeomeries, "like to like" (Homer), *assimilation* (*Anähnlichung*) in all life processes, crystal and rock formation, water gathering into seas, and the chemistry of same-element molecules (beyond valence) are all offered as evidence. Empedocles' fragment ("with earth we see earth...") leads through Plotinus ("the eye would never see the sun were it not sun-like"), Goethe's couplet, Patrizzi and Campanella. Anaxagoras' "opposite by opposite" may hint at the *polar* character of this similarity [1132–35].
10. *Unreleased light.* What exists of sunlight on an unpeopled tundra? Light as mere happening, the "unreleased appearance of light." The whole world of qualities is present in happening unreleased. This "disposes of epistemological nihilism" [1136]. In the sense organ, happening divides into motion and estranged appearance, and that is possible only through similarity between the soul's kinds of experience and happening's kinds of efficacy. Both modes are two modalities of one "primal matter," so the equations reverse: "Were the sun not eye-like, to the eye it could never appear." Resistance is experienced only by the resistant, and sensation is a special kind of beholding: the beholding of "nearness as such" [1136–39].
11. *Distance and the past.* Images get their distance-character only against the appearance of nearness. Elemental souls are souls of distance. The soul of spatial distance is temporal distance, which is distance of the past. Beholding is "primal recollection" (*Urerinnerung*): man's primal image is younger than the animal's, the animal's than the plant's, and oldest are mountains, sea and starry sky. Hence elemental souls are also ancestral souls in cult. Klages rephrases this through yesterday's materialism: man as a layered microcosm (human, animal, vegetal, elemental) [1139–40].
12. *Lament.* Spirit as will, through "herculean man," clogs the earth's pores: deforestation, extinction, drainage, river regulation. The "cradle of images" is broken and the elemental souls flee. Novalis says the soul's seat is where inner and outer world touch, so souls show themselves in physiognomies, not in entrails. Since about 1830 (Goethe's death) world history has left "the spell of the earth." "It has become too late" [1140–42].

**Key terms.** *Elementarseelen* (elemental souls). *unentbunden / Entbindung* (unreleased / release). *Gamos* (marriage of the working pole with the receiving soul). *Weltpol / seelischer Pol* (world-pole / psychic pole). *elementare Ähnlichkeit* (elementary similarity, here made polar). Sensation as beholding of "nearness as such" (*Nahcharakter*). *Urerinnerung* (beholding as primal recollection). Chaos vs. cosmos (neutralized vs. polarized happening).

**Contribution to the whole.** This is the metaphysical high point of the nearness–distance subsection. It cashes out the transcendental claim in Chs. 55–57 that soul and happening are poles, and Empedocles' doctrine, which Ch. 57 already presented as the fork between Democritus and Protagoras. It turns the elementary similarity of Ch. 34, there a principle of the life of consciousness, into the cosmic medium of efficacy between soul and happening. The ideas of "release," elementary similarity and polar reversibility are what Chs. 66–69 operate with. Ch. 67 applies the gradation of actuality to these "landscapes and wonders of art." Ch. 68 returns to it for the macrocosm–microcosm reciprocity. Ch. 69 adds "an expanding commentary" on it [1233].

**Critical note.** The argument from intuition to the life of happening (step 4) is the strongest thing here. If appearance requires a participatory reciprocity, the world-side must be capable of it, and the "unreleased light" formula offers a real middle path between naïve realism and phenomenalism. Its weakness is the move from "*X* is a necessary condition of appearing" to "*X* is ensouled." That holds only if "participation" is already defined as reciprocity of *lived experience*, a premise built into Klages's definitions [1121]. The 1830 dating of the souls' departure is pure assertion.

### Chapter 66 — On the Actuality-Content of Language (*Vom Wirklichkeitsgehalt der Sprache*) [1143–1175/76]

**Thesis.** Connection (*Zusammenhang*) and relation (*Beziehung*) are fundamentally different. Connection is real, reciprocal and experienced. Relation is conceptual, one-sided, and arises only when connections are severed. Language at its origin is the phonic representation of image-characters, the place where elemental "voices" take on a sound-body through similarity of rhythms. So the name has evocative power, there is no signless thinking, and thinking is a kind of inner speaking that has kept, in every abstract word, the trace of an appearance.

**Argument.**
1. *Seven theses on connection and relation.* The examples are a door attended to vs. a wrist tied to the door-handle, seeing an island vs. stepping on it (footprints remain). The theses: connection is not relation; connections involve mutual influence, relations none; connection is real, relation conceptual; connections are experienced and not conceived, relations conceived and not experienced; connections rest on the spatio-temporal continuum, relations on extra-spatio-temporal spirit; connections can exist without relations but not the reverse; and (7) relation requires that connections be *abolished*. Since corporeal connection presupposes the connection of distant with distant, which is inconceivable, connection as such is inconceivable [1143–44].
2. *Solution to the problem of the concept (promised at Ch. 38, p. 429).* Why does a general concept fit its cases? Logocentric thought has confused relation with connection. Concepts relate, and so do objects, but neither explains the other (Plato tried to derive object-relations from concept-relations). The concept holds of its cases because the *name's meaning* reflects an essence for which the cases' boundaries do not exist. Ethnology needs "symbolic identity": the sacrificed bird is all birds, the wafer is one body [1144–46].
3. *Evocative power of the name.* Assembled evidence: curses and "touch wood," *lupus in fabula*, *nomen est omen*, secret divine names (Ra and Isis, Brahmanaspati, the "great name," Rome's secret tutelary god), naming taboos for the dead, verbal suggestion (which fails if the recipient lacks the language), the Finnish *laulaja* (singer = sorcerer), the "wolf" in children's games, the change in one's relation to a stranger after "a few words," and the suggestive force of print and advertising. All of it shows that language is "an operative power of the highest rank" [1146–48].
4. *Schemata (the figures are missing from the text).* The naturalist schema (thing determines name and concept) and the ideological schema (concept determines name and thing) show only relations and reduce the name to an appendage. The relation of name to concept is set by an act of will, near to a proper name or trademark. Against them stand the *animal schema* (corporeal appearance – life-state – expression, a closed triangle; the reflex as limit case) and the *biocentric* or symbolic schema (name at the apex, meaning, character of appearance) of the "Promethean stage." Language is "phonic representation of units of meaning," a "phonic mirror of characters of appearance," not expression of the speaker's state. This is aimed against the "bow-wow" theory [1149–53].
5. *What the ideological schema reveals.* (1) The bond between name and meaning was cut. (2) The name became an arbitrary sign. (3) The bond between meaning and character of appearance was cut. Founding a relation therefore means devaluing the "weaving-power" of the name and of the image [1153].
6. *Language and the elements.* Biocentric thinkers (pre-Socratics, Romantics, Nietzsche) take names literally, and language itself believes in its knowledge. *Name* goes back to IE *gnō*, "recognition" (Kluge); "in the name of" carries power. Against Wundt's chicken-and-egg remark, languages came before thought: *Vernunft* comes from *vernehmen*, *Verstand* from understanding speech. Primal language was probably sung speech. Noises and sounds are the most immediate self-expression of the elemental souls [1153–56].
7. *Against onomatopoeia.* Imitated sounds are not the core of language (*kokko* vs. *gackgack*; Manchu *kilang-kalang* for bells). Sound-symbolism also covers movements (*stolpern, torkeln, krabbeln*). The euphony of a poem largely disappears for someone who does not know the language. Homonyms (*Bauer, Tau, Tor, Steuer, Ton*) make us "hear differently." So sense is not in the phonetic sound. The link is a "similarity of rhythms" (melos, tempo, stress, joining). Ovid's frog-line "sub aqua, sub aqua" is the example. The poet's magic is renewal of primal sounds through rhythm [1156–59].
8. *No signless thinking.* Apprehensions without judgment exist, but judgments without inner linguistic form do not. Three sources of the contrary illusion are taken apart. (a) The pre-verbal striving of a researcher is a form of experience, not thought, and "a eureka without sayability has never yet occurred." (b) Reformulating changes the concept ("the earth's force of attraction" vs. gravitation; "lying is forbidden" vs. "it is not permitted to speak untruth"). (c) The unsayable (smells, timbres) is what one can only *think of*, not *think about*.
