Primitive languages' wealth of concrete names shows that conceptual words displace sensory vocalization [1159–62].
9. *Manner of speaking and manner of thinking.* Thought-activity requires virtual speech-processes. Communication is the outer layer of language and representation the inner one. Communicative predominance gives garrulity, representational predominance gives depth. Slack spirit gives flight of ideas, slack soul gives "profound stammering" where madness and poethood touch. Conceptual thinking keeps to language's outer layer; Nietzsche: consciousness is a means of communicability. Language is still the bequest that reaches furthest back, older than menhirs [1163–65].
10. *Thinking is a kind of speaking* (not the converse). Silent thought grew late, as an inner dialogue with an "imagined friend" once symbiotic bonds had dissolved. Plato's dialogues; the gendered aside on women's diaries vs. men's treatises; the scholar's card-index replacing the inner partner. "To philosophize is to attempt an interpretation of word-magic." Etymologies: *scheinen* (shine → seem), *denken* as factitive of *dünken* ("to make appear," Kluge), *wissen* from *wid* ("see, find"). Every noumenon comes from a phainomenon [1165–69].
11. *Grammar as fossilized metaphysics.* Attribution grew out of ownership ("the leather *has* a brown colour"). Sensualism's "bundle of properties" lets the owner consist of his possessions. The many cases of Finnish (15) and Hungarian (about 21) mark spatio-temporal connections. In Greenlandic, Basque, Turkish and Quechua, processes are rendered as undergoing ("my tear" for "I weep," "he dies to me" for "I kill him"), which shows an excessive feeling of dependence. Only Indo-Europeans and Semites sharply oppose noun and verb, and nominative and accusative. That sharp opposition reversed the poles: the suffering pole became the *attacking* bearer of the will to deed (nominative → subject) and the acting pole became its target (accusative → object). This was a "tragic" linguistic event. The neuter coincides with the accusative as *passivus* [1170–73].
12. *Suffixes and goddesses* (continued on the spliced page [1176]). *-heit* comes from a root meaning "gleam, shine, see." Abstract nouns once named quasi-divine powers, and when they become "proper" nouns they are feminine (*die Güte, die Wärme, die Schönheit*). Greek and Latin abstractions are equally goddesses (Sophrosyne, Nike, Dike, Themis...; Veritas, Justitia, Fortuna, Fides...). The road then runs from Plato's Ideas through the realist universals to objects that can only be thought, and to a subjectivism in which values are what a group "finds" so [1173–76].

**Key terms.** *Zusammenhang* (connection) vs. *Beziehung* (relation). "Abolition" of connection as the condition of relation. *symbolische Identität* (symbolic identity). Evocative power (*Beschwörung*, conjuring) of the name. Animal schema, ideological schema, biocentric schema. *Darstellungswerte* vs. *Mitteilungswerte* (representational vs. communicative values). Similarity of rhythms. Signless thinking (denied). Thinking as inner dialogue.

**Contribution to the whole.** Gives the long-promised solution to the problem of universals (Ch. 38) and builds on the account of symbolic language in Chs. 35–36. Applies the elemental-soul doctrine (Ch. 65) to sound. Supplies the connection/relation distinction that Ch. 68 generalizes into the *coincidentia oppositorum*. The nominative/accusative argument is the linguistic version of Book IV's claim that will reverses the pole of receptivity (cf. Ch. 26, cited for "Janus-headed" polarity).

**Critical note.** The case against signless thought is careful and well-argued, especially the distinction between *thinking of* and *thinking about*, and the observation that rewording changes the concept. The theses on connection and relation, however, *define* experience as connection and conception as severance, so the "solution" to the problem of universals moves the explanandum into the "meaning of the name" without saying how that meaning holds of many cases beyond invoking "essence." The typological claim about Indo-European and Semitic case systems is speculative, and Klages himself admits the translations are "only approximations."

### Chapter 67 — On the Essence of Actuality (*Vom Wesen der Wirklichkeit*) [1175–1198]

**Thesis.** "Actuality" is a *singulare tantum*: grammatically singular, it means an uncountable plurality of actualities, as many as there are worlds of appearance. All actualities share one mark, *estrangement* (*Entfremdetsein*) of what is experienced. They differ in a gradable *depth*, which is distance. Poetic worlds are exemplary of the "unrestrictedly actual." Language can renew vanished actualities because the symbol was born in a *visionary act*. That act strikes in the moment of greatest momentum in the swing of the life-pendulum, not at its turning-point, where ordinary acts of apprehension fall. So it keeps, in a sound-body, a "moment of eternity."

**Argument.**
1. *Objection.* If speech mirrors actuality through rhythm, how can there be thousands of languages, homonyms and translations? Easy answers (that this held only at the pre-rational stage, or that speakers differ) still presuppose one actuality of *objects* [1175–76].
2. *Images vs. things.* The forest as thing (perceived, the same from afar and near) differs from the forest as the changing actualities that appear from afar, from near, and on entering (experienced, beheld). Perceiving relates, beholding connects. The split that spirit makes between them is the same split that tears inner speech away from the soul's magical sphere [1177–78].
3. *Singulare tantum.* Linguistics has "collectives" and "pluralia tantum" but no name for a singular form with plural meaning. "Actuality" is offered as one. So the question becomes why any language is shared by more than a dozen people. Every village, indeed every individual, has its own language. Communicative will drives toward a world-language and representational will toward multiplicity, and existing languages are their resultant. All understanding is translation. Translating representational values is new creation ("re-creation in verse"). Linguistic individuality comes with every formative power; Nettelbeck's memoirs are "of which no Goethe would need be ashamed." Grimm's Estonian legend: the god lets each tribe take a voice from the singing water in a kettle [1178–81].
4. *Logical objection.* If there are only actualities, what do they share, and isn't actuality back again? Answer: the common trait is the estrangement of what is experienced as actual. That is an ur-phenomenon and cannot be further analysed, and it carries plurality within it. The proposition is not convertible, because there is *apparent* actuality. Ordinary illusions are corrected by facts, but "illuminations" and "revelations" cannot be. The criterion is *depth*, the "penetrating urgency" that guarantees extra-personal origin, (*Eindringlichkeit*), as opposed to mere obtrusiveness (*Andringlichkeit*). Depth can intensify only together with a change of quality (yellow → blue-violet). Depth equals distance. Hence "actual, more actual, most actual" holds for images, though it is nonsense for Being [1181–83].
5. *First application: poetry.* The richer an image's actuality, the more it becomes a world. Romantic works surpass Classicist ones in aura. Mörike's "History of the Fair Lau" compels acknowledgment of its actuality even though no one believes in water-nymphs. Fairy tales, Homer, and Shakespeare's *Julius Caesar* lead into worlds incompatible with each other and with the world of things. These are "exemplary images of what must be understood under the unrestrictedly actual." He rejects both "realism" (a court transcript holds more factual truth) and the hunt for Ideas: either great art is "grey haze" or it unlocks actual worlds [1183–85].
6. *Second application: nearness without loss of distance.* Sensation gives the thing a "distanceless nearness" and a matching "depthless shallowness" of reflection (*Untiefen*, shoals). But bodily nearness, through "deep voluptuousness," can let depths flow into the closest nearness: the image is born when the soul loses itself in happening, like a breaking wave. Actuality can change *without* any change in the intuitive image: brook, hut and mist suddenly "gaze with nameless mysteries" and then sink back into the "frosty day of consciousness." The world of things is "a dungeon walled up out of the mortared splinters" of shattered actuality. Receptivity matters too: the same poem opens wider worlds to a deep soul [1185–89].
7. *Metaphysical objection.* If actualities keep happening and changing, how can the relatively persistent word capture them? Homer's sun "no longer shines for us"; "what does the one still know of the actuality of the other?!" [1189–90].
8. *The visionary act (Der seherische Akt).* Ecstatic images are the goal and the primal beginning of world-happening, and works are "objectively become means of their renewal." Language is the best case: everyone has it, it records the soul's struggle with spirit, and it keeps the "voices of the elements" alive (quatrain: "The rain is streaming," says Peter...). Seer and poet were originally one (*vates*), unlike the sign-interpreters. Visionary thinking = symbolic thinking. Symbols, and so language as the sound-body of symbols, were created by favoured individuals [1190–92].
9. *Renewal, not repetition.* Renewal brings back the *similar*: a hundred readings make a hundred different but similar actualities. Against Nietzsche's Zarathustra ("How lovely is all speaking and all the lying of sounds"), sounds do not lie. Sappho's *Poikilothron' athanat' Aphrodita* gives entrance to an actuality like the Aeolian one [1192–93].
10. *Mechanism of the act.* Normally the act of spirit strikes at the *turning-point* of the life-pendulum and splits thing from happening, space from time, I from world, and soul from body. A symbol has to keep the appearance's nimbus. So the visionary act must strike "not at the beginning or end of the pendulum's swing, but in the moments of its most violent momentum" (mid-swing, not at the extreme), producing a structure whose halves are distinguishable yet "strike back together." Hence *symbolon* from *symballein*, preferred to Ramler's *Sinnbild*. (Fig. 15 is missing; the text also refers to Fig. 13, apparently in error.) The experience is "rapt repose in wild motion." Its expressions are the Sphinx, where mystery becomes riddle under spirit's first thrusts and the "symbols of horror" of Babylon, India and Egypt appear, and the universal comparison of vision to lightning (an unattributed passage, quoted at length) [1193–96].
11. *Linguistic confirmation.* *dichten* < *dictare* (eye-poetry). Greek *poiētēs* is a "maker," but *mantis* comes from *mainesthai*, "to rage," which ties in *thymos*, the Thyiades and the *thiasoi*. *Geist* goes back to a root "to rush, ferment" (Old Norse *geisa*). Wodan is a storm-demon who became god of seers and poets: *Wodan* ~ *wüten* ~ *vates* ~ Old Irish *faith*; Old Norse *óðr* = fury and poetry; Vedic Vata. Odin's Rune Song ("I hung on the windy tree nine nights"; "word led me from word to word") confirms that language grew out of the inspired, "hieromanic" state [1196–98].

**Key terms.** *Wirklichkeit* as *singulare tantum*. *Entfremdetsein* (estrangement, the one common mark of the actual). *Tiefe* (depth = distance; gradable only with change of quality) and urgency (*Eindringlichkeit*) vs. *Andringlichkeit* (obtrusiveness). Apparent actuality. *Darstellungs-* vs. *Mitteilungswille*. Renewal (*Erneuerung*) vs. repetition. *Der seherische Akt* (the visionary act). *Seher/vates*. *symbolon/symballein*. "Rapt repose in wild motion."

**Contribution to the whole.** Draws the epistemological consequence of the image-doctrine. It gives a criterion of actuality that cannot be falsified by facts but is not arbitrary (depth). It answers the "plurality of worlds" question deferred in Ch. 63. It connects the pendulum and act theory of Book III (the act at the turning-point; p. 433 on the act tearing its object "out of the stream of time") to symbol-formation. It provides the theory of the seer and the ecstatic that Part 2 (Chs. 71–75) and the account of the Pelasgian world rely on, and it points forward to the "final section" on personified abstractions.

**Critical note.** The *singulare tantum* move dissolves the "one or many" objection neatly: shared estrangement plus differing depth gives unity without identity. The appeal to depth as the criterion separating justified from "unjustified" claims to actuality is left almost empty, though. It is felt urgency that "harbors the guarantee of an extra-personal origin," so it cannot tell the poet's actuality from the mystic's "illuminations," which Klages lists as apparent actuality [1182]. The chapter itself then blurs the line: a reader of Sappho learns more of Aeolian Greece than years of archaeology can teach without "interspersed 'illuminations'" [1193]. The visionary-act mechanism is openly conjecture ("esoteric inquiry... a value of probability" [1194]).

### Chapter 68 — On the Coincidentia oppositorum (*Von der Coincidentia oppositorum*) [1199–1222]

**Thesis.** Cusanus's formula contains an unwitting knowledge of *connectedness*. In actuality, opposites "coincide" as poles of a reciprocal happening. In thought they stand apart as *opposita* with an impenetrable "between." This holds (a) between persons ("circle and centre"), (b) between macrocosm and microcosms, since the cosmos depends on the souls that behold it, and (c) between present and past, since the past lives or dies through the present. When the poles are torn apart, opposita arise as enemies: class war, the destruction of landscapes, the "vengeance of the Erinyes."

**Argument.**
1. *Connectedness against relation in space, time and movement.* Every real volume is connected with every other: the Heidelberg Tun with the Pyramid of Cestius. Every real lifetime is connected with every other: Napoleon with Hannibal. Only the act of spirit makes them summable opposita. Spaces and times cannot be summed; things can. Movement turns into an "infinite series of infinitely small summands." Reciprocal dependencies (source and mouth, root and blossom, rising and falling wave) can be pointed to but not conceived. Mechanism knows only one-way dependence, so there is no interaction (Schopenhauer was right). Actuality knows *only* interactions [1199–1201].
2. *Gaze and cloud.* In perceiving I am only *related* to the cloud, and nothing on the object's side corresponds to this. Eckhart (to see God = to be seen by him) and Cusanus (our love of God coincides with being loved) began to grasp beholding. Crossing glances between persons remain opposita. Mutual effects come from the expression of one soul working into the other, not from the act [1201–02].
3. *Circle and centre.* Souls divide into those that predominantly *induce* and those that predominantly *receive*. This recalls male and female; compare sun and planets. Each pole needs the other: the "born ruler" without a people turns into a ruler over inwardnesses; whoever kindles love grows by it; indifference impoverishes. Vital circles form out of Eros, not out of a will to power. Examples: Inca and people (Pizarro's capture of Atahualpa is read as decapitating a body), patriarchal farmer and servants, Odysseus and Eumaeus, Germanic retainers, "Little Father" Tsar. When symbiotic circles break up, opposita arise, "opponents." Klages's polemic: communist "state-machine," democracy as "the straightest path to the unbounded rule of the money-bag," "America is Dollarica." Calculable end: destruction or automatization of all [1202–04].
4. *Macrocosm and microcosms.* Objective space, time and matter found an impenetrable "between." Hegel's dialectic of concepts "turning over" into each other is "a childish thing." In the world of images, that same "between" is a bond, and its name is *happening*. The research programme: find a polarity in every elementary similarity, and build homologous series (space/time, night/day, matter/ether, left/right, female/male) as particular cases of the primal polarity of suffering and acting [1205–06].
5. *The difficulty.* In beholding, the cloud is pure acting and the soul pure suffering, so polarity should be most complete here. Yet consequences seem to show only on the soul's side. To vindicate polarity, Klages must show effects on the acting pole. Landscape shaping living beings is granted: climate science, and Boas on immigrant skull forms [1206–07].
6. *Answer, positive side.* He cites p. 664–65: "As what we behold them, so we do unto things." Cosmic happening completes itself only in beholding souls, through which primal images are released. Through the soul's body, beholding leads to dance, song and works, and these change the earth. A wampum belt, a totem pole or a pyramid differs from shell, sycamore or limestone the way the products of beholding differ from those of mere life (coal seams, coral). Such works are *res natae*, and nature-dependent shapers "crown" the landscape: sacred groves and springs, Pelasgian piety [1207–09].
7. *Answer, negative side.* As beholding weakens, will-tense "subjects" face "objects." Elemental acting gives way to counter-acting, demiurgic building to an "amok-run of destruction," whose end is "the killing of the earth itself." The extermination of birds for fashion, oil derricks and whaling factories stand against sacred ibises and crocodiles, and against China, where a railway was abandoned because it would have moved ancestral graves. Photography and museums are ways of petrifying the slain. A long quotation from Friedrich von Gagern's *Grenzerbuch* on the destruction of the Ohio valley and the Native peoples follows. *Because* only the tearing-apart of the reciprocal intercourse lets this destruction loose, the original polarity is "irrevocably confirmed" [1209–12].
8. *Essences and blood-glow.* Whether the aura pales because beholding weakens, or the reverse, does not matter, given polarity. Essences are really present, since they can break forth as aura, but never *objectively* present: actuality becomes thing-like exactly to the degree that it has become essence-less in the perceiver. Esoteric extension: "primal fire" (Heraclitus's ever-living fire, Homeric *ichor*, Schuler's *Blutleuchte*, Stephani's *Nimbus und Strahlenkrone* of 1859), the polarity of ember-bearers (clinging, telluric, feminine, founders) and flame-bearers (roving, sidereal, masculine, heroes), the Vesta flame, the Yule log. Summary: the cloud-thing does not change, but the actuality-essence appearing as cloud is renewed and strengthened by being beheld, and dwindles when the "way inward" is barred. A non-esoteric fallback: will-driven humanity measurably changes climate (karst, floods, coal dust, warming). Klages also predicts that electrical currents and radio waves will "one day prove monstrous." The difference between soul–soul and soul–cosmos intercourse is kept: between souls there is alternation and magical mirroring [1212–14].
9. *Present and past.* Metric time makes the past "irrevocably over" and produces the phantom of reversible, repeating sequences. The man who only plans the future has lost his past and empties his present. Klages's reversal: only a present filled by primal images of the past escapes "running idle." Since the appearance of distance is the appearance of the past, present and past coincide as poles. Evidence: the cult of the dead (lares, libations, desecration); the feeling of antiquity at the Colosseum or Segesta; the destruction of Russian churches as robbing a people of its past; museum objects that are "no longer what they were"; the thousand-year-old oak. Against the objection that these are only present facts: is Rembrandt's *Saskia* canvas plus oil? If it is more, then old things are more than new things, and patina holds "distances of the past" waiting for the beholding gaze [1215–18].
10. *Direction of happening.* By analogy, happening comes *out of the future* and pulls each present into ever deeper distance. A thousand years past has more distance-content and is more completely "image." That is why the ancestors become gods. Objective and symbolic thought agree that the past cannot be repeated, but for objective thought the past loses efficacy with its age, while for symbolic thought it gains. The time-line becomes "a growing nothingness" pushing "the foot-wide sheer wall of the present" before it [1218–19].
11. *Consequences.* The image of the past is co-determined by the receptivity of the living, which explains ancestor-tending and adoption in China, India and among primitive peoples. Spatial distance pulls the soul outward (roving type: mystical excursions). Temporal distance draws it inward (clinging type: conjurations, necromancy). Longing aims at the past, not the future. Penal law is the "strongest remnant" of the past's actuality, but it is decaying into protection of capitalist property and "reform," away from retribution. Law will die into "might makes right." A humanity that falsifies its past faces "the vengeance of the Erinyes" (von Gagern again): either "man as an unconscious machine, or the globe without man" [1219–22].

**Key terms.** *Coincidentia oppositorum*: inseparability of polar processes, as against *opposita* (sundered poles, "opponents"). *Zwischen* (the "between": separating for objects, binding for images). Circle and centre (*Kreis und Mittelpunkt*), inducing vs. receiving souls, Eros. Homologous series of polarities. *res natae* (human works as natural products). *Blutleuchte* (blood-glow), ember-bearers vs. flame-bearers. Polarity of present and past. "Running idle" (*Leerlauf*).

**Contribution to the whole.** Generalizes Ch. 66's connection/relation into a full metaphysics of polarity. Answers the one-sidedness worry left open by Ch. 65's world-pole/psychic-pole scheme. Turns the "distance = past" equation of Chs. 65 and 67 into a doctrine of the past's dependence on the present. Gives the devastation lament of Chs. 64–65 its metaphysical ground (macrocosmic dependence on microcosms). Anticipates the concluding section on law and on the Pelasgian cult of the dead.

**Critical note.** The Rembrandt argument is effective against a flat physicalism about cultural objects. If the painting is "more than" pigment, destroying it destroys more than matter. But it shows only that *meaning-bearing* features depend on present beholders, not that "past actualities" themselves change. Klages concedes that such considerations "do not strike the core" [1217]. The core is the claim that the event is interaction coming "out of the future" [1218], and that claim is posited by analogy, not argued. The proof of macrocosmic dependence is also circular as stated: it reads the existence of destruction as confirmation of the polarity whose rupture supposedly causes it [1210]. The political passages (democracy, "Dollarica," "Christian methods of treachery") are polemic, not argument.

### Chapter 69 — Primal Images and Phantoms (*Urbilder und Phantome*) [1223–1237]

**Thesis.** Every content of intuition appears from some stratum of happening. The deepest appearances are *primal images* (*Urbilder*), the shallowest *thing-images*. The will cannot abolish intuitability, but it can produce *phantoms*: images whose intuitability masks the "image-murdering nothingness" they serve. "World history" is the struggle of phantoms with primal images. Its causes are the wandering and self-transforming of images, not human agency. This determinism is not teleology, and it does not remove values or partisanship.

**Argument.**
1. *Strata.* Thing-images stand to primal images as a face seen through a strongly clouding, distorting medium. Remove the primal images and even thing-images would vanish. A world-fire that left slag continents would still be intuitable [1223–24].
2. *Three Ohios.* The Ohio of the Indians, the Ohio of today, and an Ohio burned by an unheard-of fire. The burned one relates to the first as primordial death to primordial life. Today's industrial Ohio, and "the Chicagos" (embankments, gasometers, wires, "planimetrized" land), are a *counter-image*, essentially different: powers that want to annihilate appearing itself have entered it. Burned land heals like a wound, industrial land "slays the melos" like car horns. Hence the name *phantom*. The growing intrusion of phantoms into actuality is "an intuitable criterion for the extent of the rule of spirit" [1224].
3. *Image-dependence of all process.* Unreleased images exist on unpeopled planets, among plants and in vegetative processes. They are partly released in animals and wholly released in beholders. The image is "that for the sake of which the event happens." Matter is the "sum-total of the places of its image-formation," what persists for a while. Plutarch is inverted: "we become many, in that our image changes and in the end forsakes the matter that held it fast." *Assimilation* (*Anähnelung*): organ formation, reproduction, instinct are all attachments to renewing images of the past. "Image-formation is the form of the soul's becoming corporeal." The yucca moth is no more a "riddle" than the kidney excreting urea [1224–26].
4. *How spirit phantomizes.* Even the timeless act must form an image of itself to have actuality, and that is how images become phantoms. The disappointments of love and veneration, and mankind's chasing of delusive images, show the "inducing effect of nothingness." He refers back to the medicine man and the "envoys" of spirit who spread suggestion. World history could be written as a "murderous game of deception," steered for nearly a century by phantoms alone [1226].
5. *Spirit's two great historical camps.* Taoism is set aside (a "winter of the soul"). Islam is an interlude. Buddhism wrapped all images in a false aura of suffering and offers the phantom of Nirvana. It keeps its promise, at the cost of the adherent's own barrenness, and destroys no other life. Christianity aimed from the start at phantomizing images. Its morality sanctifies the will, which is the will to power, which is the will to destruction. Its present symbol is Mammon, and its history culminates in the phantoms of skyscrapers, stock exchanges, factories, the assembly line, poison gas and the press. "By their images shall ye know them": the pictorial works of Christendom illustrate a history of deeds that confirms Nietzsche's "metaphysics of the hangman." Atrocities listed: Vasco da Gama in 1502 (the pilgrim ship, the mutilations at Calicut), the slave trade, Charlemagne's 4,500 Saxons beheaded at Verden, the destruction of temples (Apamea, the Serapeum), heresy persecutions from the Manichaeans to the witch-burnings, Soviet atheist against theist. A sage from "a happier star," seeing crucifixions, martyrdoms and Last Judgments, would name the present phantoms: hell made real on earth (*Nulla est redemptio ex infernis*) [1227–31]. This section is polemic and historical illustration; the inference from iconography to essence rests entirely on the premise that "it is the effective powers that speak out of images."
6. *Images as metaphysical causes.* Since the powers ("demons") are present only as images, released or unreleased, everything that happens depends on the wandering and self-transforming of images. Physical forces, animal behaviour and human volitions are "mere means," "transitional forms." Even spirit and life become "auxiliary concepts" for studying the struggle of primal images with phantoms [1231–32].
7. *Not teleology.* Teleology posits purposes and preparatory means toward an end. In the actuality of images there are moments of consummation that are at once primal beginnings, and no separable preparations. The appearing image can be treated as the "metaphysical cause" or "sufficient reason" of its antecedent chain only because discursive thought has swapped the moment for the line of objective time. "Actuality belongs only to the moment" [1232].
8. *Plausibility from art.* Dance masks and idols from New Guinea to the Tyrol look like demons congealed, with carvers as somnambulant tools. Egyptian animal-headed gods appear as offspring of desert, river and starry sky. The Greek sculptor "freed" the god from the marble, and Pallas Athena is more than fantasy. The lemurs of Notre-Dame look down on Paris as its fate. All this is "an expanding commentary" on Ch. 65 [1232–33].
9. *Time-spirit.* Pre-European cultures (Native American, Mexican, Peruvian, early Chinese, Japanese, Indian, African) are conditioned predominantly by primal images. "History" in the narrow sense is conditioned by phantoms, which uniformize customs and drive out the *genii loci*. Each epoch is an image-like total image. The examples: the Baroque wig, Leibniz, the Thirty Years' War, the forms of muskets and fencing stances belong together. Leibniz and Newton "had to" invent the calculus because the phantoms of machines "intended" to appear; Watt "had to" invent the steam engine. The mechanist who protests shows that mechanical causality masks a belief in the self-sufficiency of arbitrary will, and he is the phantoms' most willing tool. Research programme: read epochs back from their "ends" (e.g., brandy, the compass, gunpowder and movable type as the "ends" of high scholasticism) [1233–35].
10. *Two objections answered.* (a) If all human doing is compelled, guilt and merit evaporate and the author's passion is self-contradictory. Answer: since Nietzsche, views are consequences, not causes. Klages goes further. Theory and practice are equal expressions of a "constitution," and constitutions serve the images that need them, so the author's passionate partisanship is itself in the service of powers pressing to appear. (b) This does not deny value. Two crossing scales are set out: a scale of *significance* (the few with immediate connection to images vs. the many who live by their coined values) and a scale of *value* (bound to primal images vs. bound to phantoms). "The constitution of the blood alone decides" which end gets the plus sign, and so partisanship is unfailingly passionate [1236–37].

**Key terms.** *Urbild* (primal image: appearance of the deepest strata of happening). *Dingbild* (thing-image). *Phantom* (an image whose intuitability masks the nothingness that wills the death of images; a "counter-image"). *Gegenbild*. *Phantomisierung*. *Anähnelung* (assimilation). Wandering and self-transforming of images (*Wandern und Sichwandeln der Bilder*) as metaphysical cause. Time-spirit (*Zeitgeist*). Scale of significance vs. scale of value.

**Contribution to the whole.** Ends the Doctrine part of Book V. It gives spirit's destructiveness (Book IV; Ch. 65) its image-theoretic form, and it provides a philosophy of history based on images instead of agents. It extends the "inducing" bearers of primal images from Ch. 68 to deceptive induction. It prepares Part 2 (the Pelasgian world-picture, Chs. 71–75) by contrasting primal-image-bound cultures with "history." It refers back to the "prehistory of the discovery of images" (Ch. 57) and to the medicine man and conjuror discussed earlier.

**Critical note.** The criterion that separates phantom from primal image is supposed to be "intuitable." In practice it is given by examples (the Ohio triad, car horns) and by the felt "slaying of the melos," and the final value-scale openly hands the verdict to "the constitution of the blood." So the doctrine cannot settle a dispute with anyone who feels differently, which Klages half concedes. The anti-teleology clause has its own reply: the image counts as "metaphysical cause" of its antecedents only because discursive thought lays the moment out on the time-line, which is a mere thought-thing [1232]. But the proposed historiography reads epochs back from their "ends" along exactly that line (scholasticism from gunpowder and printing [1235]), and it has Newton and Watt invent what the phantoms "intended." So either the programme is final causation under another name, or it rests on what Klages calls a fiction.

### Arc of Ch. 63–69

The block runs from a narrow perceptual problem to a metaphysics of history. Each step widens the polarity of the receiving soul and the acting world, the polarity established in Chs. 55–57. Ch. 63 finishes the account of perceived movement from Ch. 62. It shows that seeing movement requires a felt body as indicator and a pulsed rhythm of moments of making-present, and it concludes that world-happening itself pulses. Ch. 64 then turns the theory of beholding toward the bottom of the scale of wakefulness. The plant is "sleeping beholding," bound to gravity and light as forms of the "presence of the distant." The animal, by contrast, deals with bodies through sensation. This lets Klages pull the "content of matter back into happening" [1081] and attribute a soul to matter itself. Ch. 65 takes that last step. It argues transcendentally that if intuition is participation, happening must be alive. The "elemental souls" are then unreleased image-souls that come to appearance only in a *gamos* with a receptive soul, through polar elementary similarity, and they are souls of distance and of the past.

The Fourth Section (Chs. 66–69) turns these results into a doctrine of *efficacy*. Its key is the distinction between connection and relation (Ch. 66): real, reciprocal, experienced connection against conceptual, one-sided relation, which arises only by severing connection. Language is the first test case. Names have evocative power because the original word was a rhythmic sound-body of image-characters. There is no signless thinking. Grammar records the reversal of the poles, receptive into aggressive, in the Indo-European nominative/accusative. Ch. 67 draws the epistemological consequence. Actuality is a *singulare tantum*, unified only by estrangement and graded by depth or distance. Poetic worlds are exemplary actualities. The symbol can renew past actualities because it was born in a visionary act that strikes mid-swing, at the life-pendulum's greatest momentum, rather than at its turning-point. Ch. 68 generalizes connection into the *coincidentia oppositorum* across persons, cosmos and time. It answers the one-sidedness problem left by Ch. 65: the cosmos depends on beholding souls, and the past on the present. Ch. 69 closes with the opposite of primal images, the phantoms, and a philosophy of history in which images, not agents, are the metaphysical causes.

The block presupposes a good deal from earlier: the act of spirit striking at the "turning-point" of the life-pendulum (Book III, esp. Chs. 38–40; p. 433), elementary similarity (Ch. 34), symbolic language and symbolic identity (Chs. 20–23, 35–36), the destructive will of Book IV, nearness and distance (Ch. 56), the cell's receptivity to kind (Ch. 58), and Empedocles' "like by like" (Ch. 57). It hands forward the Pelasgian world-picture and the "final section" on law, personified abstractions, the cult of the dead and the mystery-question of the egg and the hen [1155, 1176, 1222]. It also hands forward a research programme it admits it will not carry out: homologous series of polarities, a characterology of elemental souls, and an image-based historiography.

Several tensions are worth flagging. (1) Ch. 63 corrects von Baer and Sigwart by insisting that the dependence of appearance on perceiver is *reversible*, yet the same chapter says the macrocosm conditions the microcosm "incomparably" more than the reverse [1076], and Ch. 65 speaks of an "overweight of the world-pole" [1132], and Ch. 68 then needs strong dependence of the cosmos on souls. The asymmetry is never fixed quantitatively. (2) Spirit is said to be purely destructive and unable to build [1120, 1232], but Ch. 69 has the timeless act "form an image of itself" and so *produce* phantoms with real inducing power. The phantoms are a positive product of something defined as purely negative. (3) The determinism of Ch. 69 (human doing "unrestrictedly compelled") sits awkwardly with the block's moral language of murder, guilt, "Christian methods of treachery," and "vengeance" for desecration. Klages answers the formal objection by treating his own partisanship as an expression of the images, but this makes his evaluations immune to argument by construction. (4) The value criterion swings between something "intuitable" (Ch. 69's phantom as visible counter-image), something felt (Ch. 67's depth), and something given by "the constitution of the blood" [1237]. This leaves the central normative distinction of the book, primal image against phantom, without a shared public test.

Finally, the block's style changes. Chs. 63–64 argue closely with the science of their day (thresholds, statoliths, tendrils). From Ch. 64's symbolic excursus on, Klages openly announces "thinking in images" [1097], esoteric inquiry valued for "probability" [1194], and truths that "one cannot prove... but neither refute" [1213]. Polemical passages (on democracy, America, Christianity, colonial atrocities) take up increasing space in Chs. 68–69. A study guide should mark clearly where the shift from demonstration to symbolic exposition and polemic happens, since the later chapters rely on the authority earned by the earlier, more argued ones.

Ch. 70 closes Book V, Part 1 (Doctrine). Chs. 71–75 make up Book V, Part 2, "The World-Picture of Pelasgianism" (*Das Weltbild des Pelasgertums*). The Retrospect (*Rückblick*) ends the work.

### Chapter 70 — The Achilles' Heel (*Die Achillesferse*) [1238–1248]

**Thesis.** How spirit (*Geist*), an extra-spatiotemporal "nothing," could ever enter life cannot be explained. What can be shown is this: spirit can only "be present" in an organism where the life-cell has already begun to split (soul starting to separate from body). It then speeds that splitting up, and the speed grows once spirit takes over the motor processes. A purely medical account of this decay, "life as sickness," cannot explain why the decay passes through consciousness, so spirit cannot be done away with as a posit.

**Argument.**
1. *Restatement of terms.* Life is the "weaving power of primal images"; spirit is the counter-power that turns primal images into phantoms (cf. ch. 69). Life is "enigmatic"; spirit is neither mysterious nor enigmatic, only destructive. The open question is how "the nothing intervenes in actuality" [1238].
2. *Analogy of catalysts and enzymes* [1238–39]. Enzymes make reactions happen, chiefly the breaking of large molecules into small ones, without taking part in them. In the same way spirit "must be present" without being touched, and its effect is a "putrefactive fermentation of life." Klages then limits the analogy himself: enzymes are spatial substances that can in principle appear, while spirit cannot appear at all and is "inferred only from processes of decomposition." The analogy shows only that a causation without precedent is possible.
3. *Historical restriction of spirit* [1239–40]. Spirit is absent from microbes, plants, animals and "man in general." It has been found for perhaps ten thousand years, and only in groups that entered the "historical process." Humanity is perhaps a million years old, so a gradual advance from animal to historical man is "forbidden": it would have happened long before. His summary of the stages: original man is "ungeisted" (*unbegeistet*, not yet touched by spirit) but has speech. He arose by the exchange of leading roles between soul and body. The gifted groups climb through the cultic-magical stage to the cultural stage, and spirit's first incursion comes before every surviving cultural monument. Primitives are not ungeisted. They were a "poor breeding ground" for spirit because the exchange of poles did not work itself out decisively in them, not because they had too much life.
4. *Periodisation.* The cultural period of the historic peoples is the "life-dependence of spirit" (Promethean, sensory spirituality). Whether it would have turned into the "spirit-dependence of life" (Herakleian, motor spirituality) without Europe is uncertain. Outside Europe, before contact with Greeks and Romans, nothing shows a Herakleian advance (Bachofen: the Orient honours the state of nature, the Occident replaces it with the historical). His evidence is the stability of non-European cultures (Egypt c. 4000–525 BC; China from 3000 BC to the 18th century) set against Europe's "rag-fair" of short-lived peoples [1240–41].
5. *Law of acceleration* [1241]. Spirit speeds up the tempo of life in proportion to its power to act. Once the *motor* life-processes are subject to it, the speed-up becomes ever-increasing ("a ball on an inclined plane") and ends in the disintegration of the life-cell and the downfall of mankind. That end comes either through "posthistorical man," the "human automaton," or through self-annihilating catastrophe. The "miracle" of a turning-back (*Wunder der Umkehr*) is kept as a reservation.
6. *Ethno-vital grounding of the Herakleian victory* [1241–43]. This passage is assertion, not argument. The victory of motor over sensory spirituality is grounded in the vitality of two groups that show, "on linguistic grounds alone," a preponderance of vital acting over vital beholding: Indo-Germans and Semites. Both represent a "will to power turned outward." Among Indo-Germans, active vitality forks into the heroic and the poetic-plastic. Under spirit these decay into political "do-manship" (*Tätertum*) and into rationalism and technology, culminating in Anglo-Saxon "Americanism." On the Semitic side the Arabs are a polar counterpart to Germanism. Klages then gives an openly antisemitic genealogy borrowed from Nietzsche's *Genealogy* and Bachofen. Yahwistic Judaism is "Herakleism itself particularized" as the claim to dominion of a non-localised minority working by cunning. Paul made it "fit for the world" as Christianity. Rome's will to power, which could no longer be ensouled, had Judah's power-will grafted onto it ("papalism is Judaized Caesarism"), and its present consummation is "the spirituality of high finance" [1243].
7. *The real riddle: the first infusion* [1244]. The victory of will over reason is no riddle. The first entry of spirit is, and here the enzyme analogy fails "in principle." A healthy life-cell offers spirit no "being-present." For spirit "space must first have opened itself," and it opens only through a soul that has already begun to separate from its body.
8. *Rejection of the sickness hypothesis* [1244–45]. This is a reductio of the alternative. Suppose all of the decay could be called a disease of life, outward mass murder included. Even so, no decomposition of life "could ever, of itself alone, bring forth a thinking and willing capacity for reflection." Consciousness is the one event on spirit's path that "irresistibly demands" spirit's presence. The order of states of consciousness (symbolic → Promethean → Herakleian) also shows how spirit takes part in the stages of decay. Further, "sickness" applies to conflicts among an organism's organs, while this decay concerns the *substance* of life, "the principle by virtue of which cosmic life enters into the form of organic life." Changes in human blood surely go with it, but they could explain only disturbances of the organism, not the start of the life-principle's self-sundering.
9. *Two clues from the history of symbols* [1245–48]. These are offered to a future "biocentric metaphysician." (a) *Fratricide myths*: Set/Osiris, Odin/Loki, Romulus/Remus, Eteocles/Polynices, Cain/Abel, Hagen/Siegfried, Atahualpa/Huáscar. In these the old interplay of the life-poles is increasingly mixed with *rivalry*. They hint that a rupture of the poles in the souls of the strongest life-bearers allowed spirit's irruption. Spirit is still needed to turn the appropriative drive into a true will to power: catastrophes "ease the deeds of the doer spirit only if the gate has already let him in!" (b) *The vulnerable spot*: Achilles' heel, Ajax's armpit, Siegfried's shoulder, Karna, Baldur and the mistletoe. Klages reads it as the emblem of "the vulnerable spot of life itself" in the soul-bearer of world history, no longer descended from fire. Death by an arrow or spear from behind foretells an age decided by "perfidy, guile, and betrayal: the murderous weapons of spirit" [1248]. Notes 105–106 concede that these legends can also be read through quite different motives.

**Key terms.** *Geist* as "nothing" that cannot appear; *unbegeistet* (not yet touched by spirit); *Lebenszelle* (cell of life); *Lebenspole* (life-poles) and their splitting; *Dabeisein/Darinsein* of spirit (being-present / being-within); Promethean (sensory, *Lebensabhängigkeit des Geistes*) vs. Herakleian (motor, spirit-dependence of life) spirituality; *Tätertum* ("do-manship"); *nachgeschichtlicher Mensch* (posthistorical man); the "miracle" (*Wunder*) of turning-back (*Umkehr*).

**Contribution to the whole.** This chapter gathers the historical metaphysics of Book IV (the three states of consciousness, ch. 53; will as spirit's key) and places it on the doctrine of images (chs. 66–69). It also states openly the work's admitted gap, which the Retrospect repeats: the first eruption of spirit cannot be explained. Its anti-medical argument protects the irreducibility of spirit (X ≠ Y in the Retrospect).

**Critical note.** The strongest step is the reductio against "life-sickness": decay as such does not yield reflective consciousness. That is a real argument for an irreducible second principle. The weakest steps are two. First, the dating and ethnic-linguistic placing of spirit's effect sits badly with spirit as an acausal, non-spatial nothing, since "vitality" of peoples is then made to carry what spirit cannot. Second, the Judaism/Paul/high-finance genealogy is bare assertion on Nietzschean authority. It also turns a metaphysical principle into an ethnic agent, against his own repeated claim that spirit is no "thing" within the world.

## Book V, Part Two — The World-Picture of Pelasgianism (*Das Weltbild des Pelasgertums*)

### Chapter 71 — On Poetic Genius and Pelasgianism (*Über Dichtertum und Pelasgertum*) [1251–1260]

**Thesis.** What grips a soul in any experience is always and only an image. The proof is genuine poetry, whose untranslatable content is an image freed from thinghood. The "Pelasgian" is the type of consciousness bound to images. Three groups bear it: genuine poets, the "nature peoples," and above all the prehistoric ancestors. The third group is the standard for judging the other two.

**Argument.**
1. *Purpose of Part 2* [1251–52]. The main investigation is finished. Pelasgianism is added to make the doctrine of the actuality of images "intuitable," confirmed by facts from human prehistory, as the picture of a world to which consciousness was once related as decisively as ours is to things. Whether Pelasgianism was ever "perfectly realized" does not matter: the Pelasgian *state of consciousness* is a fact of prehistory, and inner states count as facts. Method: strip away what spirit has deformed and fill in what was "prematurely crippled." The result is "a fleeting afterimage." This carries Bachofen's work forward with a "science of consciousness" he lacked. The manner is admittedly "rhapsodic" [1252].
2. *Core of the experienceable* [1252–54]. The work opened by saying that "experience and a resolve" are needed. Klages now says what that means: experiencing as such, insofar as it rests on beholding (*Schauung*). The core of actuality to be experienced is the image, changing in the stream of time. The barrier is the "belief in facts," which makes us mistake sensory life-processes for acts of perception. The method is phenomenological: recall what has truly gripped you (landscape mood, shipwreck, a work of art, dream, discovery, sudden failure). What made each *Erfahrung* into an *Erlebnis*, an *Erleidnis* ("undergoing") of the soul, was "the power of an image." The *depth*, not the vehemence, of feeling measures how far the image dissolved the barrier of the personal I. Its limit is *ekstasis*, the precondition of the *teletae*.
3. *Poetry as evidence* [1254–56]. The *what* of feeling never enters statement, yet poets present it in a speech close to primeval speech. Poetry's thought-content can be paraphrased or translated without loss; its poetic content can only be re-composed, and always with loss (cf. ch. 66). Lenau's strophe on the still sea ("as on the face of the dead") is the example. The poet's sea is not the objective sea, which is self-identical and can be sounded and represented. It is found "never and nowhere" except in the poem, never twice the same. It has the mystics' "sidereal body." Without that image the thing could not have appeared at all.
4. *Objection: toothache* [1256–57]. Even the worst bodily pain "expresses itself" only in images. If a poet can "say" the anguish of the tortured and we understand him, then the anguish undergone must be the same as such images, freed from thinghood and from the body's rule.
5. *Poetic genius and prehistory* [1257–58]. Lenau's sea is ensouled, a "demonic" world-soul. If the poet had also had a *believing spirit*, he would have taken the waters to be inhabited and built altars. That is what happened in Pelasgianism. From the whole prehistoric legacy Klages will show four basic thoughts at work. Essential oneness: (a) of images with the world's active powers; (b) of images among themselves by elementary similarity; (c) of image and symbolic sign; (d) of these with the soul that receives the image and the soul that gives the sign [1258].
6. *The name* [1258]. In Greek legend the Pelasgians are the earlier autochthons, drowned in the flood except for Deucalion and Pyrrha. The Romantics and Bachofen took up the name.
7. *Differences among the three groups* [1258–60]. The cultured poet is spiritualised and at risk of disintegration. The Vedic priest or Inca shows pathic consciousness at its purest. The primitive shows deformations, which Klages marks with the word "excess": (i) excessive binding of individual to group and group to powers. This comes not from "collective" feeling but from *fear* produced by spirit's isolating intervention. (ii) Excessive magism to ward off fear. The two fuse in boundless taboo, which kept primitives from "progress" but also kept their Pelasgianism from completing itself (example: menstrual taboos that threaten the universe).

**Key terms.** *Pelasgertum* (Pelasgianism), the image-bound, "pathic," symbol-thinking consciousness; *Erlebnis* / *Erleidnis* / *Erfahrung*; *Schauung*; *Bild* vs. *Ding*; *Wortleib* (word-body), *Sinnenleib* of an image; *ekstasis*; "excess" (*Übermaß*) as the mark of spirit-induced degeneration among primitives.

**Contribution to the whole.** It sets the method and the standard for chs. 72–75: prehistory is the norm, primitives and poets are refracted witnesses. It cashes out ch. 66 (word-soul) and chs. 67–69 (actuality = images) in the experience of the reader. It also introduces "fear from isolation by spirit," which drives ch. 75's account of bloody excess.

**Critical note.** The poetry argument is the most persuasive single route to the image-thesis, because the asymmetry of translation (thought survives it, poetic content does not) is real. But its two key steps are asserted, not shown. Defining "genuine" poetry by its power to grip [1255] is harmless; the claim that what grips in it is "solely and exclusively images" rests on a "brief survey" and does not exclude rival candidates (rhythm, sound, the thought itself). Klages also concedes that the whole demonstration convinces only a reader who has had such experiences and "resolves" to look for the image in them [1253], so it is an appeal to experience, not a proof. The step from "what grips in poetry is image" to "everything that grips anywhere is image" (and so "the counterpart of the soul... must be an actuality of images, and of images alone!" [1256]) is an inductive leap from one privileged case.

### Chapter 72 — Image-Worship and the Sense of Actuality (*Bilderdienst und Wirklichkeitssinn*) [1261–1300]

**Thesis.** Every original practice (art, polydaemonism, idol and fetish, the "double," shadow and dream belief, magic with body-parts and effigies, sign-magic) follows from one premise: for the Pelasgian the actual is the image, not the thing. Practices that look like "confusing likeness with thing" are not errors about things. They are accurate orientations to images. Every genuine symbol bears its contrary meaning (*Gegensinn*), and this repeats the bipolarity of actuality.

**Argument.**
1. *Image-worship and art* [1261–63]. Klages attacks the "saccharine doctrine of aesthetic semblance" and "pure forms" because it either denies art's symbolism or treats it as a crude stage to be cleansed. He distinguishes an animal *urge to imitate*, which becomes the *urge to represent* (realist or idealist, either way aimed at something thought), from the *formative drive* (*Bildnertrieb*), aimed only at "conjuring-fast" the image. In a purely elemental being, formative drive and capacity coincide as organic growth. The Pelasgian, spiritual but bound to soul, makes images fast by magic or by impressing them on material, creating actuality-laden "signs." Prehistoric art, tools included (hammer, spindle, wheel), was a symbol-language of belief: "no invention without a divine sense." Conclusion: genuine art "is believing, and accordingly symbolic, art" [1263].
2. *Image-worship and polytheism* [1263–68]. Gods are "unfolded symbols" that only slowly took human shape (soul-bird → Anubis/Thoth/siren → eagle of Zeus; the thunderbolt before Jupiter). Polydaemonism is "the preliminary form and root of the knowledge of the actuality of images." There are as many spaces and times as there are experienced images (night-space, house-space; Ra three times another by day; thousands of Baalim, each "the whole and real Baal"). Hegel's "Nothing is essential that does not appear" is the "truth of paganism." *Theion* is from *theaton*. Concepts cannot appear, so the god of images and the conceptual god exclude each other. "Unigodhood" is only the conceptuality of whatever one would like to clothe with divine dignity, and *"He who seeks God will never find gods."* Political centralisation produces accumulation and fusion of gods (30,000 in imperial Rome; pantheons), never monotheism. The test cases: Akhenaten's decreed sun-cult, wiped out by the people; and Israelite prophetic monotheism. Klages reads the latter (quoting Amos and Hosea in Duhm's rendering) as "naked destruction-will" against "image-laden soul" [1267–68]. Residues of image-worship inside Judaism (Asherah, Deborah's palm, Bethel, the burning bush; gods bound to place, per Robertson Smith) confirm the rule that "inseparable from every demon remains the earthly face of its place." Cults resemble one another by element (burnt offerings for the upper gods, offerings sunk in water, buried offerings), not by concept.
3. *Idolity and symbolism* [1269–77]. This is a critique of the "substitution" theory (Tylor). The mother who tends a cradle of feathers, the Ostyak widow with a wooden likeness: if A *reminds* me of B, I know B is absent. These women treat the likeness as the object itself. Their sense of actuality is grounded in the image, which is one and the same across two *things*. "Error as much as truth of logical thinking presupposes an experiencing for which essentially real are durable things; error as much as truth of elementary thinking presupposes" one for which present images are real [1270]. On the objection "but the bundle is also a thing": thinghood does not belong to the essence of the idol. A body is the soul's dispensable "receiving-place" or "house." Idol vs. symbol: the symbol is not "sign for something supersensible" (that smuggles in the concept-word relation). It is an "expressive sign" (*Sinnbild*), a *gesture* of a primal-image soul. So (i) every symbol *is* what it means, and (ii) its souls are cosmic characters. Symbols grow from elementary similarities found by a seer's reflection, unpredictably (ship = womb; "screaming red"). Symbol and idol are two sides of one primal phenomenon, "as the soul to the body... as the flame to the blood." The symbolic shudder runs soul-pole → body-pole, the idolic body-pole → soul-pole. Idol-magic needs a *determinate* thing, place and time, and properties of kind and form (laurel, copper, hazel fork). When the soul dwindles, the symbol degenerates into mere sign (empty pantomime, number-mysticism), and the idol into a "wonder-thing" (sorcery).
4. *The "double"* [1277–81]. In the transition to thing-belief, images take on object form and stand beside things as doubles: Persian *Fravashi*, Roman *genius/juno*, Socrates' *daimonion*, the Kabbalah (a Zohar passage), Egyptian *ka*, Nordic *fylgjur*, and many primitive parallels. Against Usener (who derives the double from the momentariness of daemonic seizure): the original consciousness subordinates bodily things to images, so it cannot separate the demon as a point-for-point copy. The double is a *product of decay*. The congealing image becomes a "secondary-thing," a "ghostly follower," finally a "powerless and forgotten phantom."
5. *Shadow and visions* [1281–86]. Shadow belief (China, Fiji, Tonga, Patagonians, Zulu *itongo*, Estonian sacred-tree shadows, the oath "by the darkness of the oak") shows that original actuality depends on bodilessness, shape and mutability. The Pelasgian's waking is "more akin to our dreaming." Dreams, incubation, the shaman's soul-journey (Hermotimos, Thespesios) and intoxicating drinks (Soma, nectar, mead, each brought by an eagle) all serve to "break apart the merely-perceivable world of day" so as to accord with the actuality of images.
6. *Image-magic* [1286–95]. If images are the actual, then substances share their "signature." The worldwide "eating of god" (Dionysian *omophagia*, Aztec snake-swallowing, the Cora's maize locust) shows that what is taken in is the essence that appears, not the matter: "in-imaging" rather than incorporation. Body-cuttings (hair, nails, spittle, excrement): the individual specificity of this magic (it harms only A) rules out any material reading. The body-part relates to the person as pebbles of a riverbed to the stream (*pars pro toto*). Value follows the body's *outer* appearance, especially the "gates" of the body (eyes, mouth, anus, sex). Then hands, skin and moulting (*versipelles*: "with every change of appearance a change of essence"). Pure image-magic follows: wax dolls, footprints (the Karen: a threefold image-relation), effigies, analogy-healing.
7. *Sign-magic* [1295–98]. The cross runs from image to sign; then knots and loops, the Lappish wind-knots, Aeolus' bags, birth eased by untying. What the Pelasgian binds is "the beheld image of the wind," not the storm as a concept. He believes in sober reflection what a poet believes in rapture: "the actuality of soul perceptible to sense."
8. *Contrary-sense of symbols* [1298–1300]. The elder that heals is the elder that sent the illness. Every symbol carries its counter-meaning, a *coincidentia oppositorum* that repeats the "bipolar character of actuality." This is what keeps it from being either concept or metaphor. A list of examples follows (dog's hair for dog-bite; the lance of Telephus; Apollo as healer and plague-sender; sober and maddening springs; Mother Holle's well). Its root is the Heraclitean "reversal of opposites," "falsified by Hegel."

**Key terms.** *Bilderdienst* (image-worship); *Wirklichkeitssinn* (sense of actuality); *Bildnertrieb* vs. *Darstellungsdrang*; *Polydämonismus*; *Idol/Idolität* vs. *Symbol/Sinnbild*; *Doppelgänger*; *Abbildzauber* (image-magic) vs. *Zeichenzauber* (sign-magic); *Ein-Bildung* (in-imaging); *Gegensinn* (contrary sense); *pars pro toto*.

**Contribution to the whole.** Ch. 36 (symbolic vs. conceptual thinking), ch. 34 (elementary similarity) and ch. 68 (*coincidentia oppositorum*) become a full typology of practices. The contrary-sense prepares the polarity-and-middle analysis of ch. 73. The clothing and mask motif prepares ch. 73's sacrifice customs and ch. 75. The "double" as a decay-form matches ch. 69 (primal images → phantoms).

**Critical note.** The best argument is against the substitution theory. "Reminding" implies absence, and the treatment of the likeness implies presence, so the "error" reading misdescribes the behaviour. Klages also rightly notices Tylor's own dream-state aside [1271]. The weakness is method. The evidence comes from Tylor, Frazer and Mannhardt's decontextualised comparativism, and any counter-instance can be absorbed as "degeneration" or "excess." The image-thesis therefore cannot be tested against the material it claims to confirm. The anti-Jewish reading of prophetic monotheism ("vampiric will to power" [1266–67]) is polemic, not analysis.

### Chapter 73 — Elemental Symbols (*Elementarsymbole*) [1301–1329]

**Thesis.** Three elemental symbols are traced through their elementary similarities: moon, water, tree. Each is a symbol of fate and of time, and each is doubly polar (life/death, male/female). Closer study shows each to be mainly telluric and female. Behind the poles stands a third that holds them together, the resting middle. Symbol-thinking equates it with earth = seed = egg = womb = *mother*. Womanhood as motherhood is not one pole but "the pole-linking middle," the *coincidentia oppositorum* itself.

**Argument.**
1. *Cosmos over organism* [1301–02]. Focus moves from primitives (animal traits) to cosmic traits. Bachofen: earthly things follow the laws of the heavenly. Method: descend to "the Mothers" by following the elementary similarities that link the three symbols.
2. *Survey* [1302–07]. *Moon*: to beholding consciousness it belongs to night-space and to a nightly renewal, "the pulse-gauge of time," the "celestial chronoscope" (*men/me* = measure; divination and lunar farming rules). *Water*: the "stream of time." Time is not a straight line but "eternal otherness," the face of fate, so water symbolises fate that cannot be calculated but knows everything: hydromancy, oracles at springs, Mimir's well, ordeal-rivers, the "spring of justice" at Kadesh. Contrast: the moon is formally determinate and articulates periods; water is formless, always changing, protean (Proteus, Thetis), and so transforming (baptism, the fountain of youth, waters of forgetting and of remembrance). *Plant/tree*: fortune as flowering (Psalm 1, the *Hávamál*), birth-trees in Aargau. With the tree, space-symbolism comes to the fore: roots below, crown above, the world-tree Yggdrasil, the tree of life as tree of fate (a Russian plague-charm with the "old elders").
3. *Digression: symbolism vs. personification* [1307–15]. Against "belletristic subjectivism" and "empathy-projection," Klages argues from language and custom. Speech "explains, not the cosmos by man, but... man by the cosmos." In "blooming health," "withered old age," Wallenstein as a "leafless tree," it is man who is compared to the plant. Hebbel's "last tree" poem identifies a human fate with a tree, not the reverse. Myths of men growing from the earth (Curetes, Corybantes *dendrophyeis*, the Iranian first pair from a plant, Sioux ancestors rooted in the ground) take the person *back* into the vegetative. The decisive proof is sacrifice. Anticipating ch. 75, "to bring a demon a sacrifice always means to sacrifice the demon himself." The victim must take on the demon's form by changing clothes or by mask (from which Greek tragedy came). Mannhardt's plant-masks (Walber, Pfingstl, Green George thrown into water, Pfingstbutz, the Bohemian Pentecost-king "beheaded"; Aztec Xilonen and Huitzilopochtli) show man giving up personality for "the personless character of the plant." In judgment form, the tree-experience is not "the tree is man-like" but "I too am a growing and withering thing... I dive, un-selved, back into the life of the tree" [1313–14]. Lenau's Merlin is the closing illustration.
4. *Dual polarity* [1315–21]. Each symbol is life and death at once, and so is each pair (bright/dark, above/below, drive/restraint, utterance/inwardness). Such pairs are *polar*: conflict and complement at once, and inseparable. Warning: do not import "positive/negative" from physics. The death-side is as "positive" as the life-side, and neither is end or means of the other. Klages gives two series of twelve related properties of appearance (receptivity, below, rest, darkness, earth, space, night, dying, restraint, inwarding, "heart," left / begetting, above, motion, light, sun, time, day, arising, impulse, utterance, "head," right). Their unconscious consistency outdoes conscious inference. Sexual difference is "exactly as justified as any of the others," but no deeper, and dangerous if read "materially." It is used for the reader's convenience and as a bridge to Bachofen. Each pole is pervaded by the other. Today's war of the sexes belongs with the conceptual separation of space from time and the "de-imaging and atomization of the world by the calculating will" [1317]. Then each symbol is shown to be two-sided: water still (female) vs. flowing or rain (male); the moon white against the earth (male) vs. light-receiving against the sun (female); the tree house-like and rooted (female) vs. a trunk thrusting upward with rustling crown (male). Hence water, moon and tree demons come in pairs and hermaphrodites (Nix/Nixe, Poseidon *phytalmios*, Hesiod's 300 river sons and 300 spring daughters, Isis/Apis, wood-maidens and wild men).
5. *Predominant tellurism* [1321–26]. A three-storey Pelasgian cosmos: earth below (solid, female), moon between (liquid, hermaphroditic), fixed stars and sun above (fire, male). These correspond to chthonic, Poseidonian and Uranian daemonism. Solid and liquid are centripetal: water rises but always falls back as "the tear of Kronos." Flame alone is centrifugal. The moon belongs to the telluric because its light keeps waning ("borrowed light"), while the tree belongs there because of roots, fixed place and sap. The moon, joining time to space and light to darkness, is "a sign of Gamos" and "preeminently the symbol of the soul in the narrower sense." The animal soul-symbols are serpent, fish and bird, and in the bird chthonism tips over into uranism. Evidence: nearly every goddess shades into the lunar; the cosmic division of the sexes (men follow the sun, women the moon; the Inca and his sister-wife); "gynaecocratic" peoples' priority of night and moon. Pure sidereal light (Apollo, Mithras, Helios, Ahura Mazda) is never thought of as receptive, and it stands "on the point of turning over into that matter-estranged 'light of the spirit'" that opposes polarity as such [1326]. The bridge to the next step is Homer's leaf-simile: the tree of life, "neither man nor woman," bears both poles and so means "not indeed the woman, but well the mother" [1325–26].
6. *Mother-meaning of the symbols* [1326–29]. This is a conceptual reconstruction. For drive and inclination to form a totality, motion must be circular, governed by a resting centre (target, forum, the hearth of Hestia, church in the village). On the circle schema the *lower* pole (earth, night, rest, darkness) is also the *middle*. So night gains primacy over day and the below over the above. The life-bound valuation prizes the root, while spirit seeks the "pointed" and "ethereal" "for its own devaluation of life." Dying and being born are moments on the circle, which requires a "common third," a resting life-abode holding beginning and end "unexpanded." In the plant this is the seed-corn. Hence "earth = seed-corn = egg = womb = mother." Womanhood as motherhood "does not form the counterpart to manhood, but rather a still unexpanded this-side" of all oppositions. The Mother "alone is the coincidentia oppositorum" [1329].

**Key terms.** *Elementarsymbol*; *Polarität* vs. evaluative positive/negative; *Gamos* (wedding of the poles); *Tellurismus* / chthonic–Poseidonian–Uranian; *Zentripetalität/Zentrifugalität*; *Mitte* (the middle); *Mutter* as *coincidentia oppositorum*; *Entpersönlichung* vs. "personification."

**Contribution to the whole.** It applies the doctrine of polarity (ch. 68) and the time-circle (ch. 74 [1351] refers back to ch. 14 on irretrievability and originality) to concrete symbols, and gives ch. 74 its organising equation. The anti-personification argument backs Book V's claim that the soul belongs to the cosmos, not the cosmos to the soul (chs. 64–65, plant-soul and elemental souls).

**Critical note.** The reversal "man explained by the cosmos, not cosmos by man" is a sharp and well-supported correction of empathy theories (Lipps, Tylor's animism). The central step lets the *lower* pole double as the pole-uniting *middle*. It switches schemas, from the vertical polarity above/below to the concentric circle of centre and circumference. Klages does give a reason: to the earthly observer, earth stands to the heavens as centre to circumference [1327–28]. But the circle schema itself is stipulated: it is chosen so that "drive" and "inclination" can form a totality, "following the symbol-language of the ancients." The step also equivocates between "below" (one term of a polar pair) and "centre" (what unites the pair). So the female coding of the "middle" rests on a spatial model that the sexual polarity cannot justify, since he admits that polarity is no deeper than the others.

### Chapter 74 — The Magna Mater (Marginal notes on Bachofen's discoveries) (*Die Magna Mater. Randbemerkungen zu Bachofens Entdeckungen*) [1330–1400]

**Thesis.** The equation earth = egg = mother governs the whole symbolic order of Pelasgianism. The Mater is the middle of the poles. Her time is circular, a *renewal* of the origin, not a *repetition*. From this follows a "natural right" (*jus naturale*): no lordship, no possession, boundless Eros, life held only as a "fief" to be paid back by death, and retribution (*jus talionis*) understood as *healing* a wound in the world-image, not as personal revenge. Bachofen's findings are endorsed, with one correction. "Gynaecocracy" was a *cultic* priority of the Mater, not a political rule of women.

**Argument.**
1. *All-motherhood of the earth* [1330–34]. Pagan cosmogony is *birth*, against the Jewish creation by command. A list of earth-mothers follows (Pachamama, Gaia, Tellus, Nerthus, among others). Womb = grave (crouched Mycenaean burials; the dead as "Demetrians"). Autochthony myths. Numa's *parricida*: every murder touches the common mother. The less familiar converse: woman alone represents the circling middle *because* she can become mother. Following Bachofen: the chthonic goddess, the mother-meaning of woman and the form of law and community belong together (Strabo on the Cantabrians). Marsh-symbolism (Isis in the reeds, Artemis *limnaia*, Helena = *helos*) and death-symbolism (Aphrodite as eldest Moira, *mater dolorosa*, Niobe, Lycian men mourning in women's clothes, Isis releasing Typhon, Auxesia/Damia, Holda) show the double meaning.
2. *Birth as fulfilment and end* [1334–40]. The earth is ageless, both oldest and youngest, so the goddess is crone and maiden, virgin-mother (Adonis as son and father of himself; Anna Perenna). Worship is aimed at "the undivided source of life," and to the mother "belongs not the man, but the child." Childhood is the "oppositionless and supra-sexual fulfillment" in which the life-movement ends (the cult of the "divine child"; earth-born warriors spring up fully grown). From Bachofen's reading of Roman law: in the *jus naturale* the *prima origo* falls at birth or harvest, not conception. Fatherhood thinks in beginnings and hopes, motherhood in completion. Hence the privilege of the *youngest* (Grimm, Joseph, Kronos, Zeus, Nestor). The last-born is "the first-born a second time." Pelasgianism "lives and knows and teaches the eternal restoration of the origin" [1338]. Bachofen on Deucalion and Pyrrha, with Klages's own letter-spacing: "Mother-right has only ancestors, father-right has descendants." (Note 119: the emphasis marks the Mother's "supra-temporality," later shown to be actuality-time.) The stones are thrown *backward*. Carib parallel.
3. *Spatial significance of woman* [1340–45]. Woman is the mother of fates (Norns, Moirai, Sibyls), and so seems tied to time. Yet she is first documented as space: *chora/hedra* vs. *aion/chronos*; *kamara* → *Kammer*, *Kamerad*, *bursa*; Plato's *chora kai dexamene*; Isis as "house of Horus." Rest vs. locomotion (*Domiseda*; Pythagorean even/odd). Settledness, "motherland," Hestia, the mural crown. The *wall* conceals as the womb does; the phallic thrust breaks the enclosing ring (Brünhild's fire, Sleeping Beauty's hedge, Troy's wall breached by the "death-phallus" horse). Hence the fall of city walls marks the turn to "restless and unreturning... 'progress'" [1343–44]. *Sanctum/hosion* (holy by nature, under the Mother: wall, boundary-stone, grave; *quieta non movere*) vs. *sacrum/hieron* (consecrated, under Zeus).
4. *Time-circle of the mother-world* [1345–52]. This is the conceptual core of the chapter. The Mater is the bearer of cosmic order and avenger of offences against it. Wisdom and judgment belong to woman (Harmonia, Themis, Dike; laws kept in temples of Demeter and Ceres). This is not *moral* right, at which man is better, but a natural right that man no longer grasps. In actuality space and time interpenetrate. Recurrence there is not *repetition* but *renewal*: elementary similarity links a vanishing to a becoming "another and yet the same." "The effective space of things is constituted by the law of the ever-repeating repetition of the same; the effective space of images by the rhythm of the ever-resembling return of the similar" [1348]. Law vs. rhythm = drill-step vs. dance, work vs. play, machine vs. body (work-songs, music in cult, Plato's paideia). Cosmic time "has the shape of the circle and flows... into its own beginning," a judgment of "boundless absurdity" for the intellect. *Contrast with Nietzsche's eternal recurrence*: recurrence concerns *things in time* repeating the same totality. It is mechanism carried to the extreme, and the world would age. Circular time is renewal of the moment's "never-diminished youngestness." In it "time becomes a function of space": Pythagorean Pneuma outside the hollow sphere; the spindle in the lap of Ananke. "Pelasgian time is the rotation of Pelasgian space" [1351]. So there is "no order of things, no duration, no preservation, no property." Any "arrogated freedom of arbitrary will" (*Willkür*) breaks the magic circle. Law and *Willkür* are the same thing in principle, since all law comes from a legislating will, and both deny the unalterability of what happens.
5. *Natural right and its idea of freedom* [1352–58]. First principle (Pindar): gods and men "breathe from one mother." Consequences: (a) boundless Eros, no erotic private property, originally no incest taboo. Sacred unions are reserved, not forbidden (Isis/Osiris, Zeus/Hera, the Inca). Monogamy arises with private property and the *jus civile*, and marriage as "sacrament" is Christian. Eros and Sexus diverge, and every order imposed on sexuality presupposes decay begun: "Eros and law resist one another as soul and spirit do." Evidence: the avunculate (Tacitus); sacred prostitution as expiation of marriage's "theft" from Aphrodite (Herodotus, Bachofen). (b) No lordship. One is not "having, but had." Natural law "rejects and punishes... every 'will to power'." Klages adds a linguistic aside: abstract nouns are feminine because the widest universals were originally names of the great maternal spheres, so the capacity for "synthesis" is traced to the "heart" ("figment of the blood" [1356]). (c) "Equality" means being a vein in the network of blood, a leaf on the tree of life. *Jure naturali omnes homines liberi sunt*; deities as "Loosener"; the stone seat of Feronia. (d) But this freedom is bought by submission to transience: "The 'freedom' of life coincides with the commandment to die." The Platonist's and Christian's longing for immortality, and modern science's wish for unbounded duration, are for the Pelasgian the supreme sacrilege. Life is a fief. Each life feeds on others, and so a "debt" grows that "can be discharged only by the sacrifice of life."
6. *Talion against Nietzsche* [1358–65]. This is the most sustained polemic. *Genealogy* II derives guilt from creditor and debtor and punishment from the creditor's pleasure in cruelty. Klages objects: (i) primal retribution is never between *persons*. It is a matter of the clan and lasts for generations; intent is irrelevant (Oedipus); a clansman or an animal may atone. (ii) Exchange presupposes value, which presupposes an earlier experience. (iii) The "creditor" is a blood-bond, "a corporate member of life itself," and it aims at "the renewal of the original image of life" by restitution. Shed blood, not the killing, "cries out" (Arab tribes). Talion re-performs the deed on the doer because by elementary similarity the letting of blood *gives back* what the shedding took: "a healing... performed upon the wound of the image" [1362]. The Wendhagen law (whoever strips a willow is wound in his own entrails). Etymology: *Schuld*/*sollen*, *büßen*/*bessern*, *sühnen*, *vergelten*. Against Nietzsche contra Dühring: Nietzsche is right that justice does not come from reactive affect, but wrong to side with a law-based justice that rests on the will's lawgiving. The norm of the will "depersonalizes willing, but by no means also de-selves it." Anger, ressentiment, equality and conscience all take the *injured person* as their basis. Talion takes the *damage* to the world-image as its basis (to a person, animal, plant or grave). Hence *Sünde* (sin, against spirit, *hamartema*) vs. *Frevel* (sacrilege, against life, *hybris*). Sacrifice stands opposed to sacrilege as virtue to sin. Rationale (Ivo of Chartres): "what takes life... is also able to lend life" [1365].
7. *Primeval piety* [1366–70]. The Pelasgian knows the All only as the concrete things he lives among, through sensuous Eros. This gives a twofold valuation: brotherliness within and readiness for war without (Aeschylus' *eunomoi* Scythians). Killing a tribesman is sacrilege, killing a foreign warrior is not. Kinship can widen to all beings ("paternity... divides... maternity... denies all bounds"). The supreme crime is *matricide*, the natural-law counterpart of the sin against the Holy Spirit (Bachofen on the *Oresteia*: Athena breaks the Mother's right of revenge for Apollo's moral right). Memory records custom, not "history." Second valuation: encroachment is not sacrilege as such. Only "high-handed encroachment that breaks the rhythm" is. To build a house or tend a flower is permitted "collaboration at the loom of fate" when done in the service of "the growth-wishes of the earth" and from Eros toward the image.
8. *World from the egg* [1370–74]. Bachofen's *Gräbersymbolik*: the Orphic two-coloured egg; Brahma, Ptah, Bel/Omorka, the Persian 24+24 gods; the egg-born gods (Phanes, Eros, Dioscuri). The *pileus* of the freedman as half-egg. Mother = Egg. The *microcosm* doctrine comes from the egg-symbol. It asserts *identity*, not similarity, of world and egg, so it cannot be scientific.
