On existence and distinctness, the book makes a strong case. On irreducibility, it rests on a dichotomy it assumes. On hostility, it offers a powerful diagnosis and a historical narrative, but it does not offer a proof. Part Three shows that the later reception divided along exactly these lines.

# Part Three — Reception, Afterlives, and Kindred Thinkers

This part answers four questions:
- Who took up Klages's ideas, and in which direction?
- Who argued against him, and on which points?
- Which thinkers resemble him without depending on him?
- What does the secondary literature now say?

Throughout, three kinds of claim are kept apart: **documented reception** (a thinker read Klages and responded in writing), **influence argued by scholars** (a connection proposed in the secondary literature), and **affinity** (a parallel in thought with no claim that one depends on the other). Where this guide is the only one making a connection, it says so.

## 1. Contemporary reception (1929–1945)

**Walter Benjamin.** Benjamin engaged with Klages more seriously than anyone else on the left. In 1930 he urged Scholem to read the newly published *Geist als Widersacher*, calling it a major philosophical work whatever the political context its author stood in. Scholars have followed several threads from Klages into Benjamin's work:
- *Aura.* Klages's "nimbus" or "aura" of the image, the halo of distance and past that surrounds a true appearance (Ch. 57 [916–918], Ch. 65–67), is widely taken as one source of Benjamin's *aura*, "the unique appearance of a distance, however near it may be." Benjamin's essay on the work of art then reverses Klages's valuation. The decay of aura, which Klages mourns, Benjamin treats as the precondition of a politics that has left myth behind (Moretti 2013).
- *Prehistory.* Benjamin took up Klages's (and Bachofen's) idea of a prehistoric, "mythic" layer of consciousness that persists in the present. It shapes the *Arcades Project*'s notion of the "dream" of the nineteenth century and its "dialectical images." Lebovic (2013) reconstructs the triangle of Klages, Benjamin and Alfred Baeumler around Bachofen in the years 1914–1926.
- *Similarity.* Benjamin's "Doctrine of the Similar" and "On the Mimetic Faculty" (1933) describe a pre-conceptual faculty for "non-sensuous similarity" that was strong in archaic humanity and has weakened since, surviving above all in language. The parallel with Klages's *elementary similarity* (Ch. 34), his symbolic thinking (Ch. 36) and his account of the image-content of names (Ch. 66) is close. Whether it is a matter of influence or of a shared source in Romantic language theory is still debated; this guide makes the comparison on its own account.

What Benjamin keeps: the reality of the archaic, the image as the unit of experience, and decay as historical. What he rejects: the valuation (decline as loss only) and the politics.

**Adorno and Horkheimer.** In 1932, at Horkheimer's request, Adorno agreed to review *Geist als Widersacher* for the *Zeitschrift für Sozialforschung*. The review grew into a larger project, and Adorno abandoned it around 1936. Its traces are in *Dialectic of Enlightenment* (1944/47), whose Odyssey chapter debates Klages (and Rudolf Borchardt) directly (Immanen; see the sources below). The structural parallel is striking. Both books hold that reason, in mastering nature, turns into domination and does violence to the inner nature of the subject itself. Klages's "spirit as will," which can only cut apart (Ch. 46–49), and his reading of science as projected will (Ch. 50–54) foreshadow the critique of instrumental reason. The decisive difference is that Adorno and Horkheimer insist that only reason can criticise reason. Their "dialectic" of enlightenment is meant to escape both Klages's verdict that spirit is the enemy and his turn to myth, which they read as one more form of the domination it denounces. Klages anticipated this objection in the Retrospect [1416–20] (see §4.2 of Part Two).

**Max Scheler.** In *Die Stellung des Menschen im Kosmos* (1928), written while the first volume of GWS was in press, Scheler devotes a section to "the doctrine of L. Klages." He groups him with Dacqué, Frobenius, Jung, Prinzhorn and Theodor Lessing (and, in some respects, Spengler) as representatives of a "panromantic way of thinking." Scheler grants that Klages is "idiosyncratic but not without depth," that he rightly saw the creative power of the dark, unconscious drives and the dependence of fate on their symbolic images, and that his observations of late-cultural phenomena are subtle. His objections bear directly on the book's architecture:
1. Klages equates spirit with intelligence, ego and will, and so misses its core, the *intuition of essences*.
2. Spirit and life are oriented toward each other, not at war.
3. Spirit has *no power of its own*, so it cannot destroy anything. (This is the "interaction problem" of Part Two, §5.1, put as an objection.)
4. What Klages blames on spirit is really an over-sublimation of brain-bound intelligence.
5. Klages's own romantic flight relies on the willed technique he condemns.
6. He is inconsistent about when spirit "broke in."

Klages's later emphasis on *will* as the key to spirit (Book IV; Retrospect [1422]) can be read in part as an answer to objection 1.

**Georg Lukács.** In *The Destruction of Reason* (1954), Lukács places Klages at the culmination of *Lebensphilosophie*. Klages, on this account, "actually transformed vitalism into an open combat against reason and culture," and stands, with Baeumler and Rosenberg, in the ideological prehistory of fascism. This reading set the tone of postwar reception and largely explains Klages's eclipse after 1945.

**Heidegger.** Heidegger read Klages and treated him as a representative of the "philosophy of life," which on his account remains inside the metaphysics it attacks. Klages's soul/spirit opposition is a reversal of the Platonic ranking, not an exit from it. This is close to Part Two's own observation that Klages's ontology grants rationalism everything except its valuation (§4.1). The guide has not verified a specific passage, so this is offered as the standard scholarly characterisation.

**The National Socialist context.** The relationship is double-edged and should be stated exactly. Klages's antisemitism is explicit in GWS (see the Critical notes to Ch. 42, 44, 47, 48 and 70), and Lukács and Lebovic place his vitalism in the genealogy of Nazi "biopolitics." Yet the regime's official ideologues kept their distance. Alfred Rosenberg declared in 1938 that the movement did not endorse Klages's philosophy, and Baeumler, close to Klages in the 1920s, became a rival. The reasons are internal to the book. Klages's metaphysics condemns will, deed, technology, expansion and the state, and his Retrospect recommends "refraining" and the refusal of every program [1427–28]. That cannot be squared with a mobilising ideology.

**Theodor Lessing.** Klages's friend in youth and later his opponent, Lessing shared his "biological catastrophism" (*Geschichte als Sinngebung des Sinnlosen*, 1919; *Untergang der Erde am Geist*, 1924). This is the thesis that spirit, entering life, sets off a catastrophe. Scheler treats the two together.

**Characterology and the psychology of expression.** Outside philosophy proper, Klages's widest influence in his own lifetime came through *Ausdruckskunde* and *Charakterkunde* (expression and character): graphology, the theory of expressive movement, and character typology. These shaped German "expression psychology" (Philipp Lersch's *Gesicht und Seele*, 1932, is the best-known example) and, through Hans Prinzhorn, psychiatric thinking about art and expression. GWS supplies their metaphysical ground: expression as the bodily appearance of the soul (Ch. 62, "co-movement"), and the face and gesture as images.

## 2. Later developments: who carried the ideas further, and how

**Neue Phänomenologie (Hermann Schmitz, and after him Gernot Böhme and Tonino Griffero).** This is the most substantial philosophical continuation. Schmitz (1928–2021) called Klages his closest kindred spirit. He took up and *rigorised*, in phenomenological rather than metaphysical terms, several of the book's core claims:
- the *felt body* (*Leib*, as distinct from the physical body, *Körper*) as the medium of all perception, cf. Ch. 58–63, where the felt body is the "indicator" of perceived movement;
- the *pre-objective* layer of experience, which Schmitz calls primitive presence, prior to subject and object (cf. Ch. 12, 29–33);
- *feelings as spatially poured-out atmospheres*, not inner states (cf. Klages's essences that appear in the image, Ch. 20–21, and the "mood" of impressions, Ch. 9);
- *half-things* and expressive qualities in the external world (cf. Ch. 21, 65).

What Schmitz drops is the metaphysics of an alien spirit and the historical narrative of decline. He keeps a critique of the "psychologistic-reductionist-introjectionist" objectification of experience, which he traces, much as Klages did, to the Greeks (Democritus, Plato). That is a recognisable descendant of Book I's critique of the thing. Böhme developed the theory of *atmospheres* into an aesthetics and an ecology of nature. Griffero has written explicitly on Klages's primal images and phantoms (Ch. 69). Michael Großheim's *Ludwig Klages und die Phänomenologie* (1994) is the standard study of this lineage, and the 2024 collective volume *Ludwig Klages und die Neue Phänomenologie* (eds. Großheim and Smiljanić) documents it in detail. Schmitz's own essay there is titled "Was bleibt vom Philosophen Ludwig Klages?"

**Ecology.** Klages's address *Mensch und Erde* (1913), which GWS cites and presupposes, is regularly cited as one of the earliest philosophical ecological manifestos. It denounces the extinction of species, the destruction of landscape and the commodification of nature as expressions of the same will to mastery that GWS analyses. The line runs, mostly without acknowledgement, into German and later Anglo-American "deep" ecological thought. The walnut-tree example in the Retrospect [1427–28] is a small instance of the same thinking.

**Biopolitics and the theory of the archaic.** Nitzan Lebovic (2013) traces the vocabulary of life and death, ecstasy and the "pathic" from Klages through Benjamin and Baeumler into twentieth-century debates on biopolitics, and on to Agamben. Paul Bishop (2017/18) presents Klages as a "vitalist toolkit," nine portable concepts (image, soul, spirit, expression, rhythm, and so on) usable apart from the metaphysics of decline, and has emphasised the parallels with Jung and Goethe.

**The Hispanic world: Mariano Iberico (Peru, 1892–1974).** Iberico is the clearest case outside Germany of a philosopher who absorbed Klages and built an independent metaphysics with him. His path ran from Bergson (his 1916 doctoral thesis on Bergson's philosophy drew a personal letter of congratulation from Bergson) to an original "metaphysics of appearance." The Peruvian literature lists Klages's "imaginism" among its decisive sources, together with Plotinus' emanationism, Novalis, Nietzsche, Pascal, Dostoevsky and Unamuno (Pimentel; Sobrevilla). The key works are:
- *La unidad dividida* (1932), on life as division, death and resurrection;
- *El sentimiento de la vida cósmica* (1939), on nature as "a set of powers and images given immediately to man," which is a close echo of GWS's elemental souls and images (Ch. 64–65);
- *La aparición: ensayos sobre el ser y el aparecer* (1950), his main work;
- *La aparición histórica* (1971).

The central concept, the *aparición* (apparition), is an "image-concept" that dissolves the modern subject–object scheme. The image is a seeing (*videncia*) without subject or object, an event of encounter co-created in contemplation and memory. Iberico writes in *La aparición* that there are not two images, one in reality and one in the soul, but that reality is "the unbreakable unity of vision and image," and he uses the figure of the mirror. This is a direct transposition of the polar doctrine of soul and image (Ch. 8, 20) and of beholding as "mirroring" (Ch. 29–32). Iberico differs from Klages in three ways:
- He drops the hostility of spirit. His tragic dialectic of division and reunion, drawn from Pascal, Dostoevsky and Unamuno, allows that division can turn into joy and renewal.
- He gives the *aparición* a creationist, event-like character ("creation" as the absolute transition), closer to Bergson's novelty than to Klages's renewal of primal images.
- In *La aparición histórica* he extends the doctrine to history as an apparition "of the second power." It is a constructive counterpart to Klages's view that images are the causes of history (Ch. 69), without Klages's prognosis of decline.

Iberico is also part of a wider Latin American vitalism of the 1920s–40s, in which Bergson, Klages, Spengler, Keyserling and Scheler were read side by side, often through the translations and essays of Ortega y Gasset's *Revista de Occidente*.

## 3. Kindred spirits: affinities without dependence

**Henri Bergson.** Bergson is the nearest major analogue, and Klages argues against him by name (Ch. 3 [27], almost certainly; Ch. 5 [51–52]). The affinities are deep:
- Both oppose a spatialising, point-positing intellect to real, flowing time.
- Both hold that the intellect is shaped by practical action, Bergson's *homo faber* corresponding to Klages's spirit as will (Book IV).
- Both regard instinct and intuition as more intimate with life than concept.
- Strikingly, both begin from *images*. The first chapter of *Matière et mémoire* (1896) describes the world as an "aggregate of images," existing midway between the "thing" of the realist and the "representation" of the idealist. That is very close to Klages's thesis that the given is the image, not the thing and not the sensation (Ch. 18–23).

Klages's divergences are explicit:
1. *Time and duration.* Bergson's *durée* is still, for Klages, a confusion of time with duration. "Duration" belongs only to things, and the image of cosmic life accumulating "like compound interest" (Ch. 3 [27]) is a progressive picture he rejects. Real time flows *toward* us and *past* us; it does not pile up.
2. *Space.* Bergson located the problem in spatialised time but left space unexamined. Klages insists that the riddle of continuity is spatial too (Ch. 5).
3. *Valuation.* Bergson's intellect and intuition cooperate, and the *élan vital* is creative and forward-moving. Klages's spirit is an alien adversary, and his life is rhythmic renewal, not creative evolution.
4. *The past.* Bergsonian memory preserves the past as a whole in the present. Klages's past is a dimension of *distance*, whose images can be renewed but not stored (Ch. 65, 67, 74).

Iberico's development, from Bergson toward something Klagesian, shows these two philosophies can be combined.

**Nietzsche and Bachofen (sources more than kin).** GWS is a sustained argument with Nietzsche, whom Klages saw as the great psychologist misled by the "will to power" (his 1926 book *Die psychologischen Errungenschaften Nietzsches* is the prelude). Bachofen gives Book V its prehistory (Ch. 74). C. G. Carus and the Romantic natural philosophers (Ch. 57) are acknowledged ancestors.

**Miguel de Unamuno and José Ortega y Gasset.** Unamuno's *Del sentimiento trágico de la vida* (1913) shares Klages's thesis that reason is "anti-vital," that "everything vital is anti-rational," while drawing the opposite, existential-Christian conclusion: the agonic struggle for personal immortality, which Klages calls a delusion (Ch. 3, 75). Ortega's *razón vital* is the corrective that a Klages reader in the Hispanic world was likely to meet. Ortega accepts the primacy of life but refuses the antithesis, holding that reason is a function of life and that the task is to integrate the two. In that sense he answers Klages's irreducibility claim (claim 3 of the X/Y test) in the negative. Neither depends on Klages; both define the space in which he was read in Spanish.

**D. H. Lawrence.** Lawrence's opposition of "blood-consciousness" to "mental consciousness" (*Psychoanalysis and the Unconscious*, 1921; *Fantasia of the Unconscious*, 1922) runs remarkably parallel to Klages's: the solar plexus against the brain, which Ch. 57 also attributes to the Romantics; the indictment of the will; the cosmic polarity of sun and earth, male and female; and a lost archaic consciousness. There is no evidence of influence either way. It is an affinity of the 1910s–20s.

**Gaston Bachelard.** Bachelard's "material imagination," his reveries of water, fire, air and earth (1938–48), develops the theme that the elements are psychically charged images and not physical substances. That is the theme of Klages's elemental souls (Ch. 65) and elemental symbols (Ch. 73). Bachelard, however, keeps scientific reason and poetic reverie as two separate, legitimate *regimes* of mind (his "diurnal" and "nocturnal" Bachelard). This is roughly the Promethean compromise that Klages thought could not last.

**Michel Henry.** Henry's phenomenology of *life* as pure self-affection, prior to and invisible within the "world" of objects, and his indictment of Galilean science for excluding life (*La Barbarie*, 1987), restate in a different idiom Klages's claims that life experiences without spirit (Ch. 8) and that physics deals only with relations emptied of life (Ch. 51–54). Henry grounds his position in a Christian metaphysics of the Word, which Klages would have rejected outright. That makes the parallel more telling.

**Erwin Straus and Merleau-Ponty.** Straus's *Vom Sinn der Sinne* (1935) distinguishes *sensing* (*Empfinden*), a pathic, sympathetic communication with the world, from objectifying *perceiving* (*Wahrnehmen*). That is close to Klages's beholding/sensing versus apprehending (Ch. 17, 22, 55). Merleau-Ponty's pre-objective world, his "flesh," and his account of movement and the lived body (cf. Ch. 62–63) belong to the same family. In both cases the parallel is better documented through shared sources (Gestalt psychology, Palágyi, whom Klages discusses in Ch. 40 and 52) than through direct dependence.

**Jung, Corbin and Hillman.** Scheler already grouped Jung with Klages. The doctrine of the reality and efficacy of primal images (*Urbilder*, Ch. 69) has an obvious counterpart in Jung's archetypes, and a further one in Henry Corbin's *mundus imaginalis* and James Hillman's "archetypal psychology," with its "soul-making" and "images as primary." The decisive difference is that Klages's images are *cosmic* actualities, souls of distance and of the past, not contents of a collective psyche. His Retrospect explicitly bars the "maid-of-all-work 'the unconscious'" as an escape route [1431].

## 4. A short guide to the secondary literature

- **Hans Eggert Schröder**, *Ludwig Klages: Die Geschichte seines Lebens* (2 vols., Bonn: Bouvier, 1966–1992). The standard biography, by the editor of the *Sämtliche Werke*. It is sympathetic and documentary.
- **Michael Großheim**, *Ludwig Klages und die Phänomenologie* (Berlin: Akademie, 1994). The best philosophical study of Klages's relation to Husserl, Scheler, Heidegger and Schmitz.
- **Reinhard Falter**, *Ludwig Klages: Lebensphilosophie als Zivilisationskritik* (Munich, 2003). Reads Klages above all as a critic of civilisation and an ecological thinker.
- **Nitzan Lebovic**, *The Philosophy of Life and Death: Ludwig Klages and the Rise of a Nazi Biopolitics* (Palgrave Macmillan, 2013). The most synoptic English-language study. It is openly critical, follows the line from Klages into Nazi cultural politics, and also traces his afterlife in Benjamin and Agamben.
- **Paul Bishop**, *Ludwig Klages and the Philosophy of Life: A Vitalist Toolkit* (Routledge, 2017/18). An introduction organised around nine key concepts, with translated extracts. It is sympathetic and stresses Goethe and Jung.
- **Michael Großheim and Damir Smiljanić (eds.)**, *Ludwig Klages und die Neue Phänomenologie* (Freiburg: Alber, 2024). Includes Schmitz's "Was bleibt vom Philosophen Ludwig Klages?", Smiljanić on Klages as precursor of the Neue Phänomenologie, and Griffero on primal images and phantoms.
- **On Benjamin and Adorno**: Giampiero Moretti, on Klages's "nimbus-aura" and Benjamin (*Rivista di estetica* 52, 2013); Mikko Immanen, on Adorno's confrontation with Klages and Borchardt in *Dialectic of Enlightenment* (see the interview and article linked below).
- **On Iberico**: David Sobrevilla, introduction to *Ritmos del paisaje* (Lima, 2012, a re-edition of *Notas sobre el paisaje de la sierra*); Sebastián Pimentel, "La Historia en la filosofía de Mariano Iberico"; Francisco Miró Quesada, *Despertar y proyecto del filosofar latinoamericano*.
- **Translations**: GWS has no complete English translation. The *Science of Character* (*Grundlagen der Charakterkunde*) was for decades Klages's only work in English. Selections and the *Cosmogonic Eros* have appeared in English more recently, partly through small presses of the political right. The reader should know this context but need not let it decide how they judge the texts.

## 5. What the reception shows about the book

The reception sorts the book's claims into those that travelled and those that did not.
- *What travelled* was the **descriptive and critical** content: the pre-objective image, the felt body, expression, atmosphere and distance (Schmitz, Böhme, Straus, Iberico); the critique of reification and instrumental reason (Benjamin, Adorno); the elements as images (Bachelard); and the ecological indictment.
- *What did not travel* was the **metaphysical dualism** of an alien, life-destroying spirit, and its prognosis. Scheler, Adorno, Iberico, Ortega and Schmitz each rejected it, on different grounds: spirit is powerless, or reason must criticise itself, or division can turn to renewal, or reason is a function of life, or the dualism adds nothing to the phenomenology.

In the terms of Klages's own X/Y test, his successors broadly accepted **existence** and **distinctness** (life and its images are not reducible to the objects of reason). They contested **irreducibility** and **hostility**. That is where Part Two located the book's argumentative weak points.
